Charles Porter Dies: ‘Finding Faith’ Producer & Actor Was 47

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Charles F. Porter, the actor and producer known for backing the 2025 film Finding Faith and playing the titular NASCAR phenom in ESPN’s Wendell Scott: A Race Story, died on Monday after a long battle with cancer. His passing was announced by his wife, Candice Tab-Porter. He was 47.

Porter was a multihyphenate in more ways than one. Born in the U.K., he went to college at Duke, where he played defensive end on the school’s football team. After a back injury derailed his NFL hopes, Porter dropped 65 pounds and became an actor.

In the early 2000s, he made appearances on The Parkers, Close to Home, Without a Trace, Bones and House.

In 2011, he landed the lead in Wendell Scott: A Race Story. The ESPN TV movie was based on the story of Wendell Scott, who in 1963 became the first and only African-American to ever win a NASCAR Cup race. Porter was the lead. Watch on Deadline

He was also the lead and a producer on Image Entertainment’s 2006 horror thriller Nailed. Additionally, he had a small part in STX’s Jennifer Garner vehicle Peppermint.

Last year, Porter produced Lifetime’s Finding Faith starring Paula Patton, Stephen Bishop, Keith David and Nadine Velazquez. He also had a small part in the film as a bartender.

Porter is survived by his wife, Candice, and two daughters, Juliette and Layla.

Nithya Raman Projected to Win Los Angeles Mayor’s Race, Defeating Karen Bass

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The city councilmember, who represents an industry-heavy district, ran an insurgent progressive campaign against the city's Democratic Party machine, unseating troubled incumbent Bass. One respected outlet has called the race.

Nithya Raman is projected to be the 44th mayor of Los Angeles, according to one outlet, unseating incumbent Karen Bass, a fellow Democrat who struggled with credibility as constituent anger surged over various municipal ills, including housing and affordability, the entertainment production exodus and her response to the Palisades wildfire. A win for the leftist City Councilmember, running as an outsider against the local party machine, would be a significant victory for the region’s progressive movement, which heavily skews toward younger voters who rent instead of owning their homes. Related Stories News Best Memes and TikToks After Midterms' Blue Wave, From Regina George to 'The Office' References Midterms 2026 DNC Vice Chair: "We Had to Hit the Bottom"

Independent election polling site Decision Desk H.Q., which partners with Ballotpedia, announced it was projecting a Raman victory, though other major entities such as NBC News had yet to call the race. Raman is currently up more than three percent, with nearly three-quarters of expected votes now tallied. The full count may not be in for a week or longer, as any mail-in ballots postmarked by Election Day are still valid even if they arrive by early next week.

During her election night party on Nov. 3 at the Greek Theatre in Griffith Park, Raman denounced the “cynicism” which “started to make us believe that L.A.’s problems weren’t just bad, but they were permanent.” She avowed that the city “is worth fighting for every single day of our lives.”

Raman, 45, is a Harvard- and MIT-trained urban planner who founded a L.A. homelessness nonprofit and served as executive director of Time’s Up, the ill-fated advocacy organization focused on workplace sexual harassment, before winning her City Council seat in 2020. The industry-heavy district spans the southern swath of the San Fernando Valley, from Sherman Oaks to Studio City, across the Hollywood Hills to the Eastside enclaves of Los Feliz and Silver Lake. It includes the Hollywood Bowl, the Hollywood Sign and NBCUniversal.

Through each of her campaigns, Raman, who was born in India and will be the city’s first Asian American mayor, has generated Hollywood support — particularly from its TV comedy scene. Donors to her mayoral race have included Tina Fey, Mike Schur, Mindy Kaling and Cord Jefferson. She’s married to Vali Chandrasekaran, a writer-producer who has worked on 30 Rock, Modern Family, My Name is Earl and The Four Seasons. (They have twin 10-year-old children.)

During their campaigns, both candidates said they’d back unlimited film and TV credits at the state level, and champion a federal tax incentive to make the U.S. more internationally competitive. Raman has attacked Bass’ handling of the issue, including her tardiness in appointing a film liaison to the industry. Still, despite her closeness to the industry, Raman’s record of advocating for Hollywood’s needs has been called into question, even as the city and state continued to experience its historic runaway production. (The effects of the entertainment free fall have been felt across the local economy.)

In office, Raman’s key policy focus has been on housing, first championing Measure ULA, known as the “Mansion Tax,” then criticizing the unintended consequences of the legislation once its ruinous impact on apartment construction became clear. She’s since pushed for reform. (The issue has been a recurring subplot on Netflix’s real-life drama about L.A. real estate, Selling Sunset.)

Bass, 73, was Los Angeles’ first female mayor and second Black mayor. A former community organizer turned veteran elected official, she spent years ascending to the top of California’s state assembly before spending more than a decade in Congress, representing a largely African American and Hispanic section of South L.A., which has been her political base. Joe Biden reportedly took her under serious consideration to be his running mate while he was the presumptive Democratic presidential nominee in 2020.

Bass has long maintained Hollywood ties. Her most prominent supporters in her mayoral reelection campaign included Jane Fonda, J.J. Abrams, Samuel L. Jackson and Anthony Anderson.

Raman ran to the left of Bass. Her campaign included a sit-down interview with the controversial leftist streamer Hasan Piker, as well as a fraught relationship with the Democratic Socialists of America. Once a dues-paying member of the organization’s local chapter, citing its commitment to housing affordability, she was censured by DSA L.A. in 2024 for not fully adopting its policy positions against Israel. In the mayoral race, the local chapter ultimately backed her, while the DSA’s national leadership challenged that endorsement.

Raman’s last-minute insurgent run against the local Democratic Party machine put her at odds with a bloc of her fellow progressives on the City Council, who had already endorsed Bass. The top sitting L.A. politicians to endorse her were County Supervisor Lindsey Horvath, who considered her own run against Bass and represents a swath of the Westside affected by the Palisades wildfire, along with the City Controller Kenneth Mejia. Now Raman, Mejia and newly elected L.A. City Attorney Marissa Roy, who’s a member of the DSA, constitute a triumvirate of progressivism at the highest reaches of City Hall.

The general election followed a heated primary race defined by the reactionary-populist run of the former reality TV star Spencer Pratt. He drew national attention for his high-energy campaign of grievance politics after he lost his house during the Palisades wildfire due to what he argued was government negligence.

Another key figure through the cycle has been the local billionaire real estate developer Rick Caruso, a center-right Democrat who lost to Bass in 2022. He declined to run again but has remained a prominent and influential presence in the city’s political conversation. Despite having fiercely criticized Bass during her term, including over her handling of the Palisades disaster (his own Palisades Village luxury shopping center was damaged), he announced in October that he would be voting for her.

Raman’s term will overlap with L.A.’s hosting of the 2028 Summer Olympics, a global spectacle offering the city an opportunity to reboot its tarnished image. The former Lucasfilm head Kathleen Kennedy recently took the baton to oversee the ceremonies after Peter Rice, who had been running the effort, became Netflix’s chief content officer.

Report: Jalen Hurts makes an appearance at team facility

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Eagles quarterback Jalen Hurts made an appearance at the team’s practice facility on Wednesday, Tim McManus of ESPN reports.

It is the first time some players had seen Hurts since he went on injured reserve with blood clots in his lungs.

Hurts sat in on the team meeting and additional meetings, but he did not address the team.

Coach Nick Sirianni did not address a question Wednesday about whether the quarterback had been at the facility.

“I’ve talked to him and am in constant communication with him and know he’s resting and recovering,” Sirianni said. “I miss him like crazy. But nothing new on that front. I know he’s resting and recovering, and we’re all still praying for him and hoping for a speedy recovery.”

Hurts, 28, went on injured reserve on Oct. 17 and is expected to miss the rest of the season.

Tanner McKee went 0-3 in Hurts’ absence before being benched for Joe Milton this week.

Qantas Is Retiring Its Airbus A380s 4 Years Early Because It Trusts Its A350-1000 & Boeing 787 Deliveries

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Airbus A380 should have no place in contemporary aviation's point-to-point model, made possible by advanced twin-jet aircraft. However, some airlines continue to fly the A380. Korean Air has pushed the type's exit back from 2026 to 2031, and Lufthansa won't start retiring the A380 until well into the 2030s.

Qantas has bucked this trend by bringing its scheduled A380 retirement date forward to mid-2028. The reason for this disparity lies in the aircraft that different airlines plan to use to fill the capacity gap left by retiring A380s. For instance, both Lufthansa and Korean Air have substantial Boeing 777X orders.

As such, they have been forced into an A380 extension by the ongoing delays plaguing the program. Meanwhile, Qantas has opted for the already-operational Airbus A350 and Boeing 787 programs. Able to fill capacity gaps with these jets, the airline can retire the A380 on its schedule. Qantas has declined to comment on this.

Qantas had previously planned to keep flying its A380 fleet into 2032. However, this August, it announced a pull-back to mid-2028, as reported by Aerospace Global News. The move has been facilitated by the successful deployment of A350s and the arrival of additional Dreamliners into the fleet.

The A380 retirements will be progressive, meaning that the first of Qantas' ten-aircraft A380 fleet will be retired in March 2028, with the rest following over the subsequent months. Qantas hasn't set any specific dates beyond its first A380 retirement, giving it a high degree of flexibility should circumstances change further.

Qantas Group CEO Vanessa Hudson has said that the need to retire the A380s has been caused by the challenging economics of flying older four-engined aircraft in the second half of the 2020s. “The A380 is an older aircraft, and so the cost of those aircraft over time will increase in terms of maintenance,” she said. She hopes that eliminating this additional cost will contribute to improving the carrier's margins to 10% to 12% by FY2032.

The arrival of A350s and 787s will provide a replacement for Qantas' A380s. The carrier has firm orders for 24 A350s and 12 787s, Aerospace Global News reports. Half of the A350 orders are for the Airbus A350-1000ULR jets, capable of completing the nonstop Project Sunrise flights from Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) to London Heathrow Airport (LHR) and New York JFK Airport(JFK).

Qantas is also in active discussions with Boeing and Airbus to convert 20 existing purchase-right options into firm orders, with the jets arriving in 2030. The table below shows the carrier's current fleet (without the additional 787s and A350s), showing how it operates while the airline still flies A380s, based on data from Planespotters.net. When Qantas made the orders in 2023, Alan Joyce, Qantas Group's CEO at the time, said:

“Both the 787 and A350, and the GE and Rolls-Royce engines fitted to them, are thoroughly proven and extremely capable. (...) These are generational decisions for this company. The aircraft will be part of the fleet for 20 years."

It's important to note that, given the lower capacity of the A350 and the Dreamliner compared with the A380, these jets will not offer like-for-like replacement. However, they will be able to operate every route currently carried out by the A380, while also providing the flexibility needed to open new routes.

Both Qantas and British Airways are reconfiguring their A380s to cut economy seating.

The absence of 777Xs from Qantas' order sheet has provided an option, but that does not mean the decision to retire the A380 wasn't forced. Overshadowing everything has been a reduction in pre-tax profits. This has mainly been the result of the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, which has forced oil and jet fuel prices higher.

Fuel hedging has not been able to offset all of this increase. The table below uses data from Qantas' Investor Centre to show the airline's key results for FY2026. It shows hundreds of millions in profit reductions compared with last financial year.

A$2.064 billion ($1.45 billion) - down A$330m ($232 million)

A$1.29 billion ($907 million) - down A$319m ($224 million)

Qantas Domestic +7 ppts; Qantas International +5 ppts; Jetstar Domestic Flat; Jetstar International +6 ppts

Unpredictable fuel and maintenance costs have hit the A380 particularly hard, given that this enormous jet is unbelievably thirsty. Meanwhile, increasing the number of highly efficient twin-jet planes in the Qantas fleet will reduce its reliance on unhedged fuel supplies, making the airline much more resilient to geopolitical crises that upset the oil market.

Worse than having to retire expensive aircraft early due to poor profitability is being unable to retire these expensive jets despite poor profitability. Lufthansa is a notable example that has become stuck with the A380 while it waits for the 777X to arrive. Lufthansa's 777X delivery expectations remain in the first quarter of 2027, but that does not mean the airline thinks it will begin flying them that year.

Tygart Media reports that the Lufthansa summer 2027 schedule does not include the 777X. Worse than continuing to fly the A380 as a result, Lufthansa is also keeping its Airbus A340-300s flying to deal with the capacity gap. The table below shows the widebody Lufthansa fleet as it exists before the delivery of the 777Xs.

It's important to note the advanced age of these jets, with only the 787 and A350 pulling the average age down. As airlines feel increasing pain without their expected 777X aircraft, Boeing will suffer with them, even as some aircraft have already been built but may be undeliverable. Lufthansa Group CEO Carsten Spohr said:

"Like our friends in Dubai, we are in talks with Boeing about which of these airplanes we will not be accepting for commercial service and which of these aircraft will be able to be modernised. And then, of course, with the financial contribution of Boeing, we would be willing to take."

Time is running out for passengers wanting to fly on one of the Australian flag carrier's superjumbos, especially from Melbourne.

An airline with a slightly less acute A380 problem caused by 777X delays is Korean Air, which has five A380s in service. This airline also has an aging fleet of 15 747s. The original plan set for 2021 was for the A380 to retire by 2026 and the 747 to retire by 2031. This has been pushed back in response to delivery delays and Korean Air's takeover of Asiana (which had a fleet of six A380s), One Mile At A Time reports.

The carrier's A380s continue to selectively serve high-capacity routes. A capacity gap has emerged because of delivery delays. In August 2025, the airline made its largest order for new aircraft ever, as AviTrader reported at the time. The order included 20 Boeing 777-9s, along with 25 787-10s, 50 737-10s, and eight 777-8F.

Walter Cho, the Chairperson and CEO of Korean Air, said that "acquiring these next-generation aircraft is the core of our fleet modernisation strategy, delivering significant gains in fuel efficiency and enhancing the passenger experience across our global network." However, these benefits are meaningless while Korean Air waits for these planes to be delivered.

Lufthansa and Korean Air will find it hard to contemplate the retirement of their A380s until their ordered 777Xs start to arrive. After the official launch in 2013, the 777X was supposed to enter service in 2020. Yet, the program has faced numerous regulatory hurdles and setbacks.

Most recently, FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford has said that the 777X will not be certified this year, as it will follow the certification of the Boeing 737 MAX 7 and MAX 10. This new certification timeline puts the expected first deliveries of the 777X in 2027 at risk. Lufthansa will be uniquely impacted by the change in schedule. It is the launch customer.

Production standard 777X jets have already been built for Lufthansa. In May 2026, the first one built completed a three-hour maiden flight at Everett, Washington. It has also been fully outfitted with Lufthansa's cabin. As the first to receive a 777X, Lufthansa will get its jet into revenue service first, but will have to serve as the aircraft type's pioneer.

Korean Air sits further back in the delivery queue when compared with early customers like Emirates, Qatar Airways and Cathay Pacific. It won't receive its 777Xs as soon as the aircraft enters service, and may have to wait a year or more depending on the speed of production.

BLUETTI’s flexible $1,099 Apex 200 backs up your home – and powers your RV

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

What do you actually need to keep running when the power goes out? For most households, the fridge, wifi, phones, and a few other essentials come first. Buying enough battery storage for every appliance in the house can come later – and that’s the appeal of BLUETTI’s latest launch, the Apex 200 portable power station.

The Apex 200 starts with 2.56kWh of storage and 3.2kW of continuous AC output, with the option to add batteries when you need longer backup. Built-in RV connections also mean it can earn its keep between blackouts.

BLUETTI launches the Apex 200 on November 4, with standalone super early-bird pricing starting at $1,099 through November 18. Electrek readers can get an extra 5% off with code ELECTREK5OFF.

Here’s how to work out which setup is worth buying for your home or RV.

The Apex 200 takes the expandable design of BLUETTI’s flagship Apex series and puts it in a smaller package.

You get a 2,560Wh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery. The 3.2kW of continuous AC output increases to 3.6kW when an expansion battery is connected or the unit is actively charging.

The enclosure holds all that in 36.2 liters and weighs 26.9kg (59.3 lb). You’ll want two hands to move it, but it’s convenient to move it between home and your camper.

BLUETTI’s UltraCell technology delivers an industry-leading energy density of 70.7 Wh/L, yet the Apex 200 is more than 50% smaller by volume than class competitors. For an RV owner, that smaller footprint frees up room for other gear.

There’s also a tilted display and four app-controllable AC outlets. That might not sound like a big deal, but when the battery is on the floor or under your desk and you want to check or redirect its charge, it means you don’t have to crouch.

BLUETTI calls its approach “Start Small. Scale Big. Power More.” BLUETTI says Apex 300 users reported liking the modular design but wanted something easier to handle day to day. The Apex 200 offers them a lower entry point, with room to add capacity rather than replace the main unit.

Before buying a portable power station, you’ll want to know how much power your appliances draw and how much energy they use over time.

For example, a 150W appliance running continuously for 10 hours uses 1,500Wh. A 1,500W appliance gets through that same amount in one hour. Running a coffee maker briefly is a different proposition from keeping a high-draw appliance on all afternoon.

A rough runtime calculation is: Usable battery capacity in watt-hours ÷ average load in watts = hours of operation.

Divide the Apex 200’s nominal 2,560Wh by a steady 150W load, and you get about 17 hours before accounting for conversion losses and the battery system’s own power use. Real runtime will be lower.

Refrigerators complicate that calculation because their compressors cycle on and off. Check measured daily energy use where possible, along with startup demand – motors can briefly draw more power when they kick on.

If your usual blackout lasts four to 12 hours, start by looking at the Apex 200 on its own.

BLUETTI estimates it can run a 150W refrigerator for 10–12 hours, or support low-draw essentials such as CPAPs, wifi routers, and mobile devices for two to three days, depending on the equipment and how it’s used. That doesn’t mean every device on that list will run together for three days. Your combined load determines the runtime.

The Apex 200 also gives you more control over where that energy goes. Four independently controllable Smart AC outlets built right into the unit – no need to buy smart plugs! – let you switch individual devices on and off through the BLUETTI App, set schedules, and apply state-of-charge controls. So if something doesn’t need power overnight, you can turn off its power at the outlet and save the fridge’s battery.

BLUETTI calls the feature an industry first for portable power stations. The system also works with Amazon Alexa, Google Home, and Home Assistant.

A UPS function switches connected equipment to battery power in 15 milliseconds. Check that switching time against your equipment’s requirements, especially for a CPAP or other sensitive device.

With AC output on and the display off, idle consumption drops to just 10.5W.

A winter freeze or severe storm can leave you without grid power for days. That’s when adding extra storage starts to make more sense.

For typical power outages, adding one or two BLUETTI B300K batteries can extend essential home backup to two to three days.

But you can go further. One Apex 200 supports up to six B500K expansion batteries, bringing its maximum capacity to 33.28kWh.

But a bigger battery still needs recharging eventually. The Apex 200 accepts up to 3kW of solar input across two independent channels:

With a correctly configured array of SORA 500 portable solar panels making full use of those inputs, BLUETTI says the base battery can charge from 0 to 80% in 50 minutes, or reach 100% in 1.7 hours using solar alone.

A solar generator setup lets you recharge during daylight. The Apex 200 can also be charged with wall AC, vehicle charging, and compatible gas-generator charging. A suitable 30A generator connection can help replenish the battery when solar production falls short.

The Apex 200 can also form part of a larger home battery backup system through BLUETTI’s Hub A1 parallel box.

BLUETTI specifies up to 6kW of continuous AC output and approximately 100kWh of storage across a parallel system using up to three Apex 200 units.

That’s a significant increase over the standalone battery, but your daily consumption still matters. A steady 1kW load uses 24kWh in 24 hours. Heavy-draw appliances can make a large battery feel much smaller.

BLUETTI positions its larger configurations for three to four days of essential-panel backup. How close you get depends on your batteries, appliances, and ability to recharge.

If you want automatic backup through your home’s electrical panel, confirm the compatible transfer equipment, supported circuits, voltage, and output for the exact bundle. Those details determine what you can actually keep running.

Start with your most likely outage, then decide whether you need more runtime, a charging source, or both.

Extra batteries extend runtime. Solar panels or another charging source recharge them. For outages that stretch into days, both can be worth having.

When shopping for a camper van or travel trailer, choosing a battery comes down to more than just capacity. Among its 10 output ports are two connections RV owners will appreciate having built in.

The native 30A TT-30R RV outlet plugs directly into travel trailer shore power inlets for 120V power.

The 12V/30A Anderson port can directly power compatible DC equipment, including portable fridges and communications gear. This avoids converting battery power to AC and then back to DC, cutting out unnecessary conversion steps.

For charging between campsites, the system can pair with the BLUETTI Charger 2, which combines alternator and solar charging support. It lets you use driving time to replenish the battery through faster vehicle alternator charging, then use solar when you’re parked.

BLUETTI’s recommended mobile bundle is the Apex 200 + SORA 500 solar panel + Charger 2. You get storage for camp, solar charging during your stay, and alternator charging on the road. Compatible campground AC gives you another option.

The Apex 200 is the better fit if you value a smaller enclosure, easier transport, native RV ports, and a lower upfront cost. You can start with 2.56kWh and expand to 33.28kWh on one unit, or approximately 100kWh across a fully expanded parallel system.

The Apex 300 is worth considering if your priority is higher native output and capacity for a system that will mostly stay at home. It offers 3,840W of output and 2,764.8Wh of battery storage, and BLUETTI positions it for more advanced home backup, including compatible 120V/240V configurations.

For someone who wants a battery for both blackouts and RV trips, the Apex 200’s size and connections make a useful combination. You can buy the storage you need now and add more when your power needs change.

The Apex 200 launches November 4, with the standalone unit starting at $1,099 during the November 4–18 super early-bird promotion.

Founded in 2013, BLUETTI allows millions of users across more than 120 countries to stay safe and prepared. Its in-house R&D supports a range of solar generators and home backup systems for outage preparation, RV travel, and off-grid living and recreation.

Shop the Apex 200 and launch bundles, and use ELECTREK5OFF at checkout for an extra 5% savings.

Livestream: Watch Labrinth create a song from scratch tonight using Apple’s new MacBook Pro

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

To promote the new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, Apple is hosting a livestream in which singer-songwriter Labrinth will create a song from scratch overnight at the company’s Tower Theatre store in Downtown Los Angeles. Watch the livestream below.

Last week, Apple announced the new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display at a special event in Los Angeles, where it invited members of the press and content creators to get an early look at the redesigned laptop.

Now, Apple is continuing its Los Angeles-centered promotional campaign with another event, this time involving singer-songwriter Labrinth.

Starting at 8 p.m. PT / 11 p.m. ET tonight, Labrinth will spend the night at Apple’s Tower Theatre store in Downtown LA, creating an entire song from scratch using the new MacBook Pro and Logic Pro.

The entire process will be livestreamed on his YouTube channel, allowing viewers to follow along in real time and ask questions as he works. Apple says the finished song will be released next week.

Tonight’s livestream comes ahead of the new MacBook Pro’s launch this Friday, November 6.

During last week’s demos, Apple showcased the laptop’s music production capabilities, including the ability to play virtual instruments directly on its touchscreen using Logic Pro.

Day 16: Villafranca del Bierzo to La Faba

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Day 16: Villafranca del Bierzo to La Faba

Today’s terrain is bimodal: either a brutal, agonizing road walk along hard asphalt, or a peaceful wander through quiet forests and open fields, interspersed with stretches of ancient Roman roads that have withstood the erosion of the ages.

Today we enter Galicia, where we put away our C’s to replace them with Th’s as the locals do—replathing them. One doesn’t just enter Galicia; one ascends, through the famous ascent of O Cebreiro. First along a paved walk on the road and later by forest path. I thought I remembered this section as being mostly forest and field, but until the last few hours, it has been all asphalt. That is the thing about false memories or expectations: the confrontation with reality rarely goes as expected.

Finally, The Way parts from the road and we begin the section I remembered

Finally, The Way parts from the road and we begin the section I remembered—the other peak of the bimodal distribution. It is beautiful and peaceful, with steep earthen walls rising on either side of The Way. It smells like Galicia too: a farmer’s dirt track for cattle (watch your step). It is an old Roman road that weaves through the forest, gaining elevation and crossing mountain streams over large stone bridges. It doesn’t take much imagination to feel like we are walking this hundreds of years ago—and indeed, centuries of pilgrims have trodden these exact stones. No signs of civilization appear until we finally reached La Faba, the midpoint of the ascent to O Cebreiro.

the beautiful forests of the way to O’ Cebreiro

It had been a grueling climb, and now the sky tips its hand. No longer a mere sprinkle here and there, it is fixin’ to dump on us just as the entrance to the Albergue para Peregrinos La Faba comes into view, clearly signboarded. I had stopped here in 2017 for a quick nap and ended up staying the night. We had no illusions of going any further today, especially since we were carrying freeze-dried meals purchased earlier in the day—Europe’s answer to Mountain House.

Like other donativos, there were no advance reservations. We arrive relatively early, so the line ahead of us is only twenty people deep. We knew we’d get a bed, but would it be a lower bunk? There is always a subtle anxiety in the pilgrim check-in process.

Solveig, the mid-50s volunteer checking us in, is kind, thoughtful, smiling, and—being German—exceedingly thorough. Karl, the other volunteer, is in his late 70s and wonderfully jovial. He escorts us to the dorm in groups of five to eight, after several batches of us have checked in, cheerfully ordering the “young bucks” to take the upper bunks while assigning us the lower ones. I love donativos!

A 3-D printed relief map of the French Way, and us pointing to what we had walked so far.

After the initial check-in rush dies down—though wet, weary pilgrims are still trickling in from the rain—Karl and I chat in between his bed assignments. Like most donativo hosts, Karl and Solveig serve two-week stints, and they are four days into theirs. He has served so many times he’s lost count, but one thing he knows for sure: this would be his last year. That has been a recurring theme I’ve heard at nearly every donativo this trip: veteran volunteers quietly saying “no más.” Yet Karl clearly loves this work and understands its deeper value: “Better to serve for two weeks than to hear a hundred Masses.” His German way of storytelling reminds me of my father whenever his own family heritage came through.

“Why do you keep coming back?” I ask him.

He smiles and shares a story. “There once was a villager who ran into a neighbor out in the woods. ‘What are you doing here?’ he asked.

‘I’m searching for God in the forest,’ the neighbor replied.

‘But God is everywhere the same!’ the villager said.” Karl paused, speaking English but phrasing it with a distinct German syntax. “And the neighbor answered: ‘Yes, God is everywhere the same. But me—no.’

That’s why I return,” Karl said gently. “God is the same everywhere. But I am different here.”

La Faba is known for its evening Pilgrim’s Blessing, so when I see a guitar sitting in the common area, I let Solveig know that I play and offer to lead some music from the Taizé Community or traditional hymns during the Blessing. Back in 2017, it was the foot-washing ceremony I remembered most from this sanctuary—pitying the poor soul who washed my feet back then!

As people gather, I start fingerpicking Taizé melodies and lead them in “Amazing Grace”—though in a slow, soulful way they perhaps aren’t used to hearing. Solveig follows with a short, thoughtful reflection. I could see she was still refining her message, and I feel for her; it must be so hard to deliver a spiritual message in a second language. Four down, and ten more chances, I think. At the end of the service, we read a pilgrim’s prayer together—each person reading in their native tongue, yet every voice uniting on the final line: Ultreia et Suseia!

It’s the ancient call-and-response we’ve heard all along The Way: someone calls out “Ultreia!” and we respond “et Suseia!”—Further on, and higher up.

That is us tomorrow: further on, and higher up to O Cebreiro.

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

Venture and Ms.Venture finished the Camino de Santiago via the Portuguese Way summer of '24. And Feb 2025, Japan's Kumano Kodō, and later that Sunday the first third of the Oregon Coast Trail. I'm 3026 we walked The French Way from Burgos. We are a couple of 60+ Texpats living in Colorado slowly walking as we contemplate what our next season of life might look like. Venture's interest in thru-hiking came while they were in lockdown in Hanoi, Vietnam during the pandemic. He read AWOL on the Appalachian Trail and was never the same. He has walked The French Camino, 10% of the AT, The Lone Star Trail, and 50% of The Colorado Trail. Thanks for joining the adventure.

Pregnant Jen Affleck Celebrates Daughter’s Birthday Amid Divorce

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star Jen Affleck shared that she was celebrating daughter Nora's 5th birthday just one day after filing for divorce from Zac Affleck.

Jen Affleck is keeping the focus on her kids.

One day after filing for divorce from husband Zac Affleck, the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star took a moment to celebrate daughter Nora’s 5th birthday with a sweet Instagram message.

Jen posted a photo of Nora to her Instagram Story Nov. 4, showing her posing with a bicycle while wearing a pink shorts and shirt set covered with Disney princesses. The room was filled with balloons, including a large balloon garland that read, “Happy Birthday.” Jen captioned the sweet picture, “I can't believe she's already 5.”

The celebration comes shortly after news broke that the 27-year-old had filed for divorce from Zac, 30, on Nov. 3 after seven years of marriage, according to Utah court records obtained by E! News.

The two first met in 2018 on the Mormon dating app Mutual and married in June 2019, six months after getting engaged. In addition to Nora, Jen and Zac are also parents to kids Luca, 3, and Penelope, 16 months, and are currently expecting their fourth child together.

Still, despite Jen’s pregnancy, the pair had been candid about the struggles they faced throughout the marriage, many of which were documented on SLOMW and its upcoming spinoff series The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, which followed Zac and Jen as they moved to Southern California.

"It’s been a lot the past couple of months, I’ll be honest," Jen admitted to People in August. "But I do feel like I’m seeing the light at the end of the tunnel and just trying to count my blessings every single day."

She’d also shared how Zac had been there for her and their kids when there was “a lot that we’ve been navigating behind closed doors,” including her struggle with prenatal depression during her first trimester.

“I could not be there for my kids, and so Zac really had to show up,” she said. “I mean, he didn’t really have a choice.”

The reality star added, “I wouldn’t have survived the first trimester without him.”

While Jen and Zac have called it quits, keep reading to see where the rest of MomTok’s relationships stand.

Taylor Frankie Paul & Dakota Mortensen: Broken Up

While Taylor Frankie Paul's romance with Dakota Mortensen—they welcomed son Ever in 2024—bloomed on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, they called it quits due to Dakota's infidelity.

"Right after Ever was born, Dakota and I were living together and in bliss with our new baby, and I was even considering engagement," Taylor—who began dating the real estate agent in July 2022—revealed during the season two premiere of the Hulu series. "And then I learned that he was lying this entire time."

Taylor was told by a woman named Jenna that she and Dakota had been intimate and he had "spent the night more than once." (He has denied the claims, writing in a September 2024 TikTok that he "never cheated.")

On the series, Taylor said that Dakota accused Jenna of instigating their affair.

"No, it was him," she emphasized in the confessional. "He said he had just kissed her on the couch. No, he went on the couch, they hooked up on the couch, and then they went to the room and they hooked up."

Since ending her romance with the Basin Tiling founder, the mom of three (she shares two kids with ex-husband Tate Paul) was set to find love once again on season 22 of The Bachelorette, but things got a bit thorny when news broke in March 2026 of an alleged confrontation between the exes weeks earlier.

A spokesperson for the Draper City Police Department confirmed to People that there was an open "domestic assault investigation," telling the outlet "allegations have been made in both directions.”

Days later, a video of a previous 2023 physical altercation between Taylor and Dakota surfaced online, prompting ABC to cancel the MomToker's season of the dating franchise.

As for Dakota, he has kicked off a romance with show guest star Shinia Powell, though he previously apologized to Taylor for hooking up with her during a season four episode.

Whitney Leavitt & Connor Leavitt: Married

Whitney Leavitt found the perfect dance partner in Connor Leavitt, whom she wed in 2016. But it wasn't always smooth sailing for the couple—who share kids Sedona, Liam and Billy—as they navigated life in the spotlight.

For instance, the Dancing With the Stars alum, who announced during her Broadway run in May 2026 that she'd be leaving Mormon Wives, had to shut down rumors about her husband's sexuality.

"Do I need to put out a sex tape, or something? Like, trust me," she said on the Off the Vine podcast in October 2024. "We're good. Everything's fine."

"I think it's because when we were going through our personal stuff—and I know you haven't seen it—but he struggled through a porn addiction," she explained. "There's a scene where one of my friends says something along the lines of how they could see how he was confused sexually."

Through the ups and downs, the reality star emphasized that she and Connor—who were 23 and 22, respectively, when they got married—are stronger than ever. In fact, they even renewed their vows in February 2026 for their 10th wedding anniversary.

"We got married really young," Conner told Brides after their ceremony. "It felt like our wedding day, as much as it was for us, it really felt like it was for our families. So we really want to do something to make it ours, and this felt like a really fun way to do it."

Married in 2019, Jen Affleck and Zac Affleck—no relation to Ben Affleck, despite jokes to the contrary—have been at the center of breakup rumors since SLOMW's debut.

In fact, their fight after Jen attended a Chippendales striptease show in Las Vegas became a focal point of the first season. The couple—who share kids Nora, Penelope and Lucas—made up, however, and the Dancing With the Stars contestant later defended their relationship.

"It can be tiring and draining, just because it gets old having to hear the same things," Jen told E! News of the speculations in November 2025. "For me, I'm like, 'People will always talk and people will always have their opinions.' I think it is frustrating for him when people don't see what's happening behind closed doors and how much work and effort he's putting in."

But while Jen announced in June 2026 that she'd be adding to their dance troupe with baby No. 4, the MomToker filed for divorce that November after seven years of marriage.

Jessi Draper & Jordan Ngatikaura: Divorced

Bumps in Jessi Draper and Jordan Ngatikaura's five-year marriage emerged after the hairstylist admitted to having an emotional affair with Vanderpump Villa's Marciano Brunette, though initially it appeared they were working towards smoother roads.

"We are back together," Jessi—who shares kids Jagger and Jovi with Jordan—told E! News in November 2025. "We're trying to work on it. Relationships are not perfect. I think a situation like ours—it's going to take time. It's not going to something that's just amazing overnight."

Sadly, the reconciliation appeared to be short-lived, with Jordan also dad to daughter Peyton from a previous relationship, filing for divorce in March 2026 after five years of marriage.

However, days later, Jessi said she was “blindsided” by his filing and accused him of rushing to submit the paperwork because “he wanted the headline first.”

Admitting that her “heart sunk” seeing the news, she explained, “because we agreed to tell our children together before filing and we haven’t done that.” (E! reached out to attorneys for Jordan for comment but haven’t heard back.)

Though Jessi told E! News that she was "just casually dating and living life," when she brought Marciano as her date to the 2026 American Music Awards in May 2026, he revealed a tattoo dedicated to her that August.

Jessi and Jordan finalized their divorce in September 2026.

Mayci didn't expect to find love with Jacob Neeley. In fact, after the unexpected death of her boyfriend Arik Mack in 2015, when she was just 14 weeks pregnant with their son Hudson, she was prepared to be a single mom.

But it all changed when she met Jacob in 2016 at age 21, whom she would marry two years later.

"After everything I'd been through, I was very guarded, but Jacob was kind and we got on really well," Mayci said in her memoir Told You So. "For a long time, we remained just friends and because we weren't really dating, I was OK with him meeting my son."

"Jacob was amazing with him, which made him appear even more attractive," she continued. "I was terrified of getting hurt again but, after four months, I couldn't deny my feelings any longer. He was kind and funny and made me feel safe."

Mayci and Jacob went on to welcome daughters Harlow in 2021 and Charli in 2025.

The truth behind the joke gift wasn't revealed until Jessi shared on Instagram in November 2025 that "grandpa Bret likes to drink her piss like it's dirty soda and he gulps it down like water."

But Demi and Bret aren't crying over spilled secrets—or pee. In fact, she told Entertainment Tonight that December that she and Bret "have nothing else left to do other than own it and have fun with it."

Although their sex life raised eyebrows, the couple has been embracing life with their blended family. While Demi shares daughter Maude with her ex, Bret is dad to his and The Real Housewives of Salt Lake City alum Angie Harrington's sons Rome and Cole.

"I've never seen a dad so involved or love their kids harder, the most devoted step-dad to Maude, and future father to our own children one day," Demi wrote on Instagram in June 2024. "Your ability to remain consistently loyal to your role as father/step-father while navigating all that life throws your way, will never cease to amaze me!"

While Mikayla and Jace have been loving life with their four kids, Mikayla admitted that she hopes to grow their family even further.

"When people ask me how many kids I want, I'm like, 'I've always wanted three or four, but I could see myself having six,'" she told People in February 2025. "I'm excited to hopefully move into a new house. I want a big lot for my herd of kids."

As Mikayla began unpacking her trauma surrounding years of sexual abuse as a teen, as well as her chronic illness—which causes flare-ups due to heightened stress—their marriage had been deeply affected.

"I think it's hard anytime you bring up the stuff with the sex or the intimacy," she admitted to Jace. "I feel like it's hard on me 'cause I feel like, again, my nervous system's already in fight or flight and I'm already maxed out. My bucket is full."

Their relationship was a bit complicated on paper. Hang with us: Mason's brother is Chase McWhorter, the ex-husband of Layla's costar Miranda McWhorter. But the pair had been willing to put their romance to the test.

Unfortunately, the mom of two confirmed that she and Mason had split in February 2026.

Critical Role's upcoming series with Hololive gave Matt Mercer 'a deeper understanding and appreciation' for vtubers

Shattered Legends is a six-episode miniseries that will debut later this month.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Neither Critical Role diehards nor Hololive vtuber fans could've predicted that they'd work together to film a new tabletop roleplaying series. Shattered Legends is the product of a collaboration that might sound bizarre at first but makes perfect sense when you consider vtubers are basically just voice actors, like just about everyone else in Critical Role's orbit.

Matt Mercer certainly sees them that way. He tells PC Gamer that filming Shattered Legends with Hololive vtubers gave him a "deeper understanding and appreciation for the skill and talent that each of these performers has." The only challenge, he says, was the technical logistics of filming a show with a mix of humans and 2D anime girls.

The original idea for Shattered Legends, before it even had a name, was a pitch from Nerissa Ravencroft, Hololive's "Demon of Sound" vtuber. A long-time fan of Critical Role, Ravencroft says she could barely believe it when her manager told her they were up for it: "I was in awe for multiple days."

"Growing up, I never had friends or knew anybody who ran a TTRPG campaign, so it’s been a heavy desire to play one for a long time," she says. "I just so happen to be one of the luckiest girls in the world, as I get to play with not only my fellow [Hololive vtubers], but Critical Role!"

Ravencroft took the lead on Hololive's side, creating her own character, working with artists on the visuals and unique vtuber models based on everyone's characters, and helping produce an original song.

The story of Shattered Legends was inspired by Berserk and Soulsborne games. All the vtubers—which includes Ravencroft, Mori Calliope, Gigi Murin, and Shiori Novella—wanted to tell a darker story than fans might've expected. Mercer and Marisha Ray, who played alongside the vtubers, helped them fit it into Critical Role's existing setting Exandria as a self-contained "canon-ish" campaign.

The group will be playing Daggerheart, Critical Role's own fantasy TTRPG. For those unfamiliar with it, Daggerheart eschews D&D's d20 rolling system for two 12-sided dice, one representing your 'hope' and the other your 'fear'. If your hope roll is higher, you can spend a point of it to do something beneficial, but if fear is higher, the GM can use it whenever they want to challenge the players.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Mercer prefers Daggerheart when playing with new players and likes how the narrative dynamics "allow for baked-in tension and release, while also inviting players to collaborate in extremely creative ways."

Ravencroft, who is one of those new players, says she was "a bit intimidated" as someone who normally plays videogames, but found it easy once things got going, especially with Mercer and Ray at the table. "Having Matt as a GM for my first Daggerheart campaign is already crazy enough," she says, "but with an incredibly experienced and talented roleplayer and actress like Marisha, we were truly in the best hands possible."

Although the story fits neatly into six episodes, Mercer says there's "much promise of a wider world for these characters," and that "there's always room to pick up these seeds for future adventures" if people want it.

Shattered Legends will premiere on Ravencroft's YouTube channel in mid-November.

India warns AI-powered hackers targeting crypto exchanges at ‘machine speed’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

India’s cybersecurity agency says a threat actor is escalating an AI-assisted hacking campaign targeting crypto exchanges and other financial firms.

India’s national cybersecurity agency warned that an AI-assisted threat actor has escalated its attacks on cryptocurrency exchanges and other financial businesses, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) on Wednesday designated the threat actor as “CRAFTY FALCON,” in an update to a campaign it first warned about in October. The latest advisory has now explicitly identified crypto exchanges among affected businesses and detailed the use of AI attack tools.

“The threat is active and ongoing. CSIRT-Fin continues to receive reports of successful attacks, including from NBFCs and fintech entities that had already received the earlier restricted advisories and had undertaken remediation,” said CERT-In.

The warning adds to evidence of hackers using AI tools against financial institutions. In October, cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike reported that an unidentified attacker used ARTEX, an AI penetration-testing tool also named in CERT-In’s advisory, alongside Claude Code and other AI models to breach South Korean financial firms and steal data.

CERT-In said the AI-assisted financial threat actor “surveys its targets with machine intelligence, operates at machine speed and strikes by invoking the victim’s own payment and disbursement APIs.”

The campaign has targeted nonbank lenders, digital lending platforms, fintech companies, payment aggregators and service providers, as well as crypto exchanges.

According to CERT-In, attackers exploited vulnerable applications and exposed testing environments, using shared credentials and access tokens to move into production systems. They also targeted cloud infrastructure to deploy malicious files.

AI tools helped attackers rapidly generate and refine attack scripts. The attackers also reverse-engineered application code to develop tailored exploits, while some attacks were timed for weekends and holidays to delay detection.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

In some incidents, attackers used small test transactions to validate unauthorized transfers before moving larger sums. CERT-In also reported stolen cryptocurrency wallet keys and funds drained from wallets.

The agency did not name any affected crypto exchanges or quantify losses.

Cointelegraph reached out to CERT-In, CoinDCX, CoinSwitch, WazirX, ZebPay and Mudrex for comment.

CERT-In has advised entities to “treat this campaign as active and escalating,” urging at-risk companies to heighten security monitoring around the clock, with increased staffing and lowered alert thresholds during holidays, weekends and other low-coverage windows.

Solana Foundation chief information security officer Michael Coates previously told Cointelegraph that legitimate security teams need access to the strongest AI tools.

“We need to make sure that the best models we can get are in the hands of defenders because attackers will have something capable enough,” he said.

TRM Labs head of policy Ari Redbord similarly told Cointelegraph in July that “attack techniques are moving faster than a single audit from launch day can account for,” urging continuous security reviews.

Investing $10,000 – Fill a Roth IRA, Then Buy a Broad Index Fund

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

With $10,000 to invest, use accounts with tax breaks before a regular one. Take any 401(k) match you’re missing, pay off high-interest debt, and top up your emergency fund. Then fill this year’s Roth IRA, and put the rest in a broad, low-cost index fund in a brokerage account.

The account you choose matters more than the fund you buy.

Accounts with tax breaks have yearly limits you can’t make up later, while a brokerage account takes any amount, any time. Work down this list until the money runs out.

Say you get the match, owe nothing on cards, and have a full emergency fund. If you’re under 50, this year’s limit, $7,500, goes in the Roth and the rest in a total-market index fund.

Invest it all at once, since Vanguard’s research found a lump sum beat splitting it into three monthly purchases about two-thirds of the time from 1976 to 2022. If a drop right after would rattle you, dollar-cost averaging, spreading it over a few months, is fine too.

Skip hot stocks, and don’t pay an advisor 1% a year to manage it. At an assumed 7% yearly return, that fee leaves you 6%, so $10,000 grows to about $57,400 in 30 years instead of $76,100.

Buy the index fund the day each deposit arrives, since the money sits in cash until you choose an investment.

Pick a brokerage for your Roth from the best places to open an IRA.

Phaze raises $8.75M to give AI agents remote access to powerful workstations

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If autonomous artificial intelligence agents are really going to become the future of the enterprise then they’re going to need to have a computer of their own so they can get on with their work without disturbing anyone else.

The founders of the remote desktop firm Parsec Technologies Inc. have come up with a new offering that’s designed to give those agents the computer access they need. Their new startup is called Phaze Inc., and it has just launched its offering after raising $8.75 million in seed funding from Andreessen Horowitz. Today’s round also saw participation from Index, NextView, Makers and Lerer Hippeau.

Phaze has developed a high-performance remote desktop platform that’s designed to accommodate both humans and AI agent workers, giving them access to powerful computers, including high-end workstations. With its platform, those users can control a distant workstation with the same low-latency responsiveness and visual fidelity they’d get if they were accessing hardware locally.

The startup says it’s trying to solve a couple of glaring efficiency problems at many enterprises. One issue is the emergence of computer-use agents that like to hijack worker’s screens while automating mundane business tasks, reducing human productivity.

Another challenge is that many enterprises invest in powerful workstations capable of rendering complex 3D graphics and compiling massive codebases that can cost upwards of $10,000. Those machines are needed, but many information technology departments tend to have a policy of assigning one machine to each employee, which means that expensive hardware is often left sitting idle if that person goes out for lunch, sits in a meeting or takes a few days off.

Phaze’s solution is to transform those workstations into a shared pool of elastic compute, allowing anyone at an organization to access it via its proprietary low-latency streaming technology when they need it. This streaming tech is backed by a specially developed Model Context Protocol server that allows computer use agents to log into and operate expensive workstations the same way a human employee would.

The result: Instead of working exclusively through expensive and brittle application programming interfaces, agents can access a computer directly and navigate its operating system, launch applications and get work done.

Phaze co-founder and Chief Executive Benjy Boxer said the platform also supports human-to-agent collaboration, so they can work together to get more complex tasks done. For instance, an automated IT support bot might work to diagnose problems when new tickets are created, and then pass the session to a human engineer if the solution is more nuanced than what it’s authorized to do by itself.

“The computer sitting on your desk shouldn’t determine what compute you can access,” Boxer said. “People need powerful workstations to do their jobs, and now AI agents need computers they can actually operate. We’re building the access layer that lets either one connect to the machine they need, wherever it happens to be.”

By transforming expensive workstations into shared infrastructure, administrators can assign their computing resources dynamically to whoever needs access as people’s and agent’s workflows fluctuate. The workstations themselves can be sat on a desk, installed in a data center, or locked away in a cupboard. Wherever they’re located, organizations will be able to get maximum efficiency out of them.

Phaze’s job now is to convince enterprises of the benefits of its platform, and Boxer said the money from today’s round will be used to accelerate its go-to-market plans and onboard new customers. The startup will also look to expand its engineering team and develop more features.

Andreessen Horowitz General Partner Martin Casado think Phaze is likely to see a lot of interest in its remote access platform, because workstations are too expensive to be left sitting idle for hours at a time. “Phaze is turning those machines into shared infrastructure for people and AI agents,” he said. “Benjy and the team have been building in this space for more than a decade and they bring that experience to a new era of remote access and machine management.”

Uber says it alone can make robotaxis pay, hours after Waymo quit its app

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Google-owned autonomous cab company Waymo has revealed it will stop allowing Uber users to book its robo-cabs, hours before the OG rideshare giant made a case for all autonomous vehicle fleet operators to use its platform instead of creating their own.

This slow-motion fender bender played out across Wednesday, starting with Waymo Co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana telling Bloomberg that her company would end a partnership that saw it work exclusively with Uber in Austin and Atlanta.

Mawakana said Waymo wasn’t happy about the fact that when users of Uber’s app requested a ride in one of its robo-cabs, they weren’t guaranteed to get one.

News of that break-up came on the same day that Uber announced its quarterly results. As is common on such occasions, the company published remarks [PDF] its execs intend to make during an earnings call.

CEO Dara Khosrowshahi used those remarks to promote the idea that Uber is the natural home for robotaxi fleet operators, because it already delivers around 30 percent higher trips per vehicle per day compared to apps run by autonomous cab companies. He said that higher utilization rate, plus use of shared charging and maintenance infrastructure, can deliver “higher fleet profitability even after paying Uber’s marketplace fee.”

“As autonomous technology becomes more widely available, we believe this ability to commercialize vehicles profitably will become an increasingly important source of competitive advantage and value capture.”

The two events are deliciously ironic, because Uber is arguing the scale of its platform makes it the ideal host for third-party players – just as Google does with its search, app store, and YouTube services. Left unsaid is that the two companies hold concerningly dominant positions in their respective markets.

Khosrowshahi also outlined more ways Uber intends to strengthen its position, including by putting around 2,000 robo-cabs on the road in 2027, before “a much larger commercial ramp beginning in 2028.” That plan means Uber will run some cars itself and rely on third-party robo-cab operators who allow bookings on its app.

“Importantly, building a broad ecosystem does not mean spreading our resources evenly,” the CEO explained. “We intend to maintain an open platform while concentrating capital, integration resources, and hands-on deployment support behind a smaller group of partners with the clearest paths to commercial scale and attractive economics at maturity.”

Or in other words, as Uber uses more robo-cabs, it will try to maximize profits.

Uber users booked $59.8 billion worth of services in its third quarter, up 20 percent year-over-year. The company recognized $14.75 billion of that as revenue, a ten percent improvement.

Rideshare accounted for $29.5 billion of bookings and $7.5 billion of revenue. Delivery services brought in $5.5 billion of revenue on $28.5 billion of bookings.

No wonder Khosrowshahi wants more robo-cabs: they’ll boost Uber’s more lucrative business.

The company might need that boost because Australia’s High Court yesterday decided [PDF] that the company must pay AU$81 million ($57 million) in State payroll taxes because the fees paid to rideshare drivers are effectively wages. While the case considered Uber’s activities in one of Australia’s six States, all of them charge payroll tax. The case is one of many around the world in which courts have found gig workers should be treated as employees for legal and tax purposes. The High Court is Australia’s highest judicial authority and like-minded jurisdictions around the world are aware of its decisions, potentially making more trouble for Uber further down the road. ®

Zillow CEO’s reality check on AI agents: ‘Finding a home isn’t a chore to hand off to a robot’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 4, 2026 at 5:41 pmNovember 4, 2026 at 5:54 pm

People will let AI agents cancel subscriptions and book appointments, but most won’t want one to do their house hunting, predicts Zillow Group CEO Jeremy Wacksman.

Wacksman made the case Wednesday afternoon in prepared remarks on Zillow’s quarterly earnings call, addressing a big issue for the online real estate company: whether AI agents will shop on people’s behalf, bypassing sites such as Zillow.

“No one wants an AI agent to watch a movie or play a game for them, because the enjoyment is the point,” he said. “Dreaming and searching for a home is similar. It’s the fun part of the moving process that people want to do.”

Few decisions are as high-stakes as choosing a home, he said. “Finding a home isn’t a chore to hand off to a robot; it’s a milestone people want to live themselves.”

It’s a key issue for Zillow, which makes most of its money from the home-buying process, including fees from partner real estate agents and its mortgage lending business.

That depends on people browsing Zillow’s listings and connecting with those (human) agents and loan officers. An AI agent that does the searching could change those dynamics and potentially cut Zillow out of the process.

The issue has become more pressing with the rise of personal AI agents such as Meta’s Muse, which launched in September and can book travel and make purchases on people’s behalf, among other everyday tasks.

Amazon blocked Muse from shopping on its site less than two weeks after launch, saying it never agreed to let Muse shop there and that the agent doesn’t identify itself. Amazon is also suing Perplexity over the shopping agent in its Comet browser. A federal appeals court in August overturned an injunction Amazon had won in that case.

So far, Wacksman said, Zillow isn’t seeing meaningful traffic from AI platforms. The company has integrated with OpenAI and Google’s Gemini, and is now working with Anthropic. About 80% of its traffic comes directly to Zillow.

For the third quarter, Zillow’s revenue rose 7% to $724 million, short of its own forecast of $745 million to $760 million, as a sharp rise in mortgage rates slowed home sales.

The company posted a $158 million net loss for the quarter, mostly from agreeing to settle a shareholder lawsuit over Zillow Offers, its defunct home-flipping business.

Kyndryl shares surge 30% on hyperscaler-related revenue growth

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Shares of Kyndryl Holdings Inc. jumped 30% today after it reported better-than-expected earnings for the second quarter of its 2027 fiscal year.

Kyndryl generated $3.9 billion in sales during the three months ended Sept. 30. That represents a 1.9% decrease from a year earlier, but the company still managed to top the consensus estimate by about $50 million. It attributed the top-line surprise to its Kyndryl Consult business.

Kyndryl, a former IBM Corp. unit, once generated nearly all its revenue from managing other companies’ technology infrastructure. In 2022, it launched Kyndryl Consult to take on more strategic, higher-margin professional services deals.

The unit’s revenue grew 4.2% year-over-year in the second quarter. Kyndryl didn’t share a specific quarterly revenue number, but divulged that the unit earned a total of $3.6 billion over the past 12 months.

Kyndryl Consult’s signings, a measure of future revenue, jumped 19% over the past year. That momentum may have contributed to the company’s stock rally today.

Many of the projects that Kyndryl Consult takes on involve artificial intelligence. The unit can help customers build AI agent ensembles and set up training data generation pipelines. Additionally, it can advise clients about business decisions such as how they should allocate their AI budgets.

Kyndryl offers a software platform called Bridge to companies that buy its AI services. It enables AI agents to access business data from an organization’s internal systems. Additionally, Bridge monitors those agents to ensure they adhere to safety requirements.

The public cloud is another major focus of Kyndryl’s growth efforts. The company’s hyperscale-related revenue topped $500 million in the second quarter, which amounts to a 27% year-over-year increase.

“Strong signings and continued growth in Kyndryl Consult and hyperscaler-related revenue reinforce our confidence in our fiscal 2027 outlook,” said Kyndryl Chief Executive Officer Martin Schroeter. “Our performance reflects demand for Kyndryl’s AI-led modernization capabilities and deep mission-critical expertise that help customers evolve their complex technology environments, strengthen cybersecurity and achieve measurable business outcomes.”

The company delivered an adjusted net income of $92 million, which breaks down to 42 cents per share. Analysts expected 41 cents per share. The sum excludes a $38 million workforce reduction charge the company took in the second quarter. It’s part of a broader cost-cutting initiative that is set to save up to $500 million annually starting from Kyndryl’s 2028 fiscal year.

For the current fiscal year, the company is forecasting an adjusted pretax income of $600 million to $700 million. Kyndryl expects its revenue to be flat or increase by up to 2%.

Matthew Stafford has full participation with elbow injury; Myles Garrett limited in return

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Rams coach Sean McVay wouldn’t allow much about the ice pack on quarterback Matthew Stafford’s arm after Sunday’s win over the Chargers other than to say it was soreness. The injury report said it isn’t serious, and Stafford agreed.

Stafford was a full participant, listed with an elbow injury.

“I’m 38 years old, trying to keep up with these young bucks. Little bit of soreness and stuff that is not uncommon this time of year,” Stafford said, via Gary Klein of the Los Angeles Times. “Haven’t missed a rep. Feel good. So I’m not too worried about it.”

Stafford underwent surgery on his elbow during the 2022 offseason.

The Rams got more good news when outside linebacker Myles Garrett (knee) returned to practice as a limited participant. It was the first practice Garrett and Aaron Donald have had together.

Garrett underwent arthroscopic knee surgery in mid-September after playing 40 snaps in the season opener. He was given a six-week prognosis and has missed seven games.

The Rams practiced without cornerback Jaylen Watson (shoulder), long snapper Joe Cardona (ankle), cornerback Trent McDuffie (back), tight end Colby Parkinson (ribs) and safety Kam Curl (foot) on Wednesday.

Inside linebacker Grant Stuard (hamstring), offensive lineman Steve Avila (hamstring), wide receiver Jordan Whittington (back) and tight end Terrance Ferguson (ankle) were limited.

In Your 40s, Check Your Retirement Progress – Then Max Your 401(k) & IRA

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Your 40s still give new savings time to compound, so check now whether you’re on track for retirement. Fidelity’s guideline is to have at least three times your salary saved by 40 and six times by 50. Push your 401(k) contributions toward the yearly limit, fund an IRA, and start adding some bonds to your investments.

Find a retirement shortfall at 45, and you have about 20 years to close it before 65. Find it at 60, and you have five.

At a hypothetical 7% average annual return, $150,000 saved at 45 plus $1,000 a month grows to about $1.1 million by 65. Starting the $1,000 a month at 45 rather than 50 means five more years of deposits, $60,000 in all, and about $200,000 more at 65.

So divide your retirement savings by your salary now and compare the result with Fidelity’s guideline. It assumes you’ve saved 15% of your pay since age 25, any employer match included, and will retire at 67. The total to aim for is your retirement number, about 25 times the yearly spending Social Security won’t cover.

Whatever your numbers show, make these moves this decade:

If you’re behind, also plan to work a few years longer. Each extra year of work adds a year of saving and leaves one less year of retirement for your savings to pay for.

Log in to your 401(k) this week and raise your contribution by two percentage points, then add a point with every raise until you hit the limit.

To choose a provider for your IRA, compare the best places to open an IRA.

High gas prices are pumping up used EV sales: these are FLYING off the lot

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A new car affordability crisis and punishing gas prices are driving an unprecedented number of used car buyers to consider going electric, and that increased demand without a corresponding increase in supply means these used EVs are flying off dealer lots.

Days on the lot. That’s dealer-speak for the average number of days a given vehicle typically sits in inventory before finding a buyer. That’s critically important to dealers, since the vehicles on their lots are commonly financed through something called floorplan credit, and every additional day a car spends on a dealer’s lot can mean another day of interest payments and another day closer to a curtailment. Days on the lot can be even more important to dealers when it comes to cars owned outright, as well, as the dealers can have significant cash tied up in vehicles they’ve purchased outright, and every day those cars sit is another day that money isn’t working for them elsewhere.

That’s what makes this month’s fastest-selling used EVs particularly interesting, because these used EVs aren’t just selling fast for electric vehicles – they’re selling fast, period.

Dealer news sources Automotive News and Cox Automotive report that the average new car spent 82 days on the lot in October, with a 75-day target being an industry-wide benchmark. At the same time, the Audi Q4 Sportback e-tron Quattro with its 258-mile EPA-estimated range is turning in just 29.8 days. (!)

That Audi’s not alone. In a recently-released vehicle pricing study, iSeeCars reported that the Toyota BZ4x, Lexus RZ300e, Honda Prologue, and Borrás-family favorite Polestar 2 are all turning in an average of 35 days or less, with the BMW i4, iX, Kia EV6, and Chevrolet Equinox EV just a day or two behind.

“The results reveal a market where EVs are appreciating in average asking price much faster than other drivetrains, while also spending less time waiting for buyers,” said iSeeCars Executive Analyst Karl Brauer, who found that average transaction prices for EVs rose 9.8% last month as ICE-powered vehicles stayed steady, rising just 0.3%. “Hybrids are experiencing moderate improvements in price and selling speed, while gasoline-powered vehicles have seen their selling times increase,” he adds, “despite relatively stable prices.”

Used electric vehicles averaged a price of $32,533 in September 2026, up $2,896 from the $29,637 ATP recorded a year earlier.

Judge Torn on Lil Durk Bail Question After Murder-for-Hire Verdict: ‘A Question of Psychology’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The rapper's lawyers want him out of jail ahead of a second racketeering trial. Prosecutors say he'll flee.

A judge is struggling with whether to let out Lil Durk on bail following his acquittal on murder-for-hire charges but before a second racketeering trial.

The “All My Life” hitmaker (Durk Banks) was found not guilty in September of taking out a hit on rival rapper Quando Rondo. Durk’s lawyers are now pushing for his release after more than two years in jail, offering to put up all his future music royalties as bond and even getting letters of support from Jay-Z and J. Cole.

Judge Michael W. Fitzgerald weighed the request during a Wednesday (Nov. 4) court hearing, attended by Billboard. The judge was torn, given that Durk attempted to flee to Dubai before his original 2024 arrest and still faces another trial next summer for allegedly operating his Only the Family (OTF) label as a violent gang.

On the one hand, Judge Fitzgerald said “not that much has changed” for Durk and that “even an innocent person would be tempted to flee” at this point. On the other, however, he noted that Durk “has a very strong incentive now to let the legal system play out, so the acquittals really do change the calculation a great deal.”

“It’s a question of psychology,” said the judge. “Would he flee?”

Durk’s team says the answer is no. His attorney Drew Findling argued Wednesday that the rapper “understands how the system works” and wants a second jury to find him not guilty again. Fellow defense lawyer Christy O’Connor also pointed out that the proposed bond package truly encompasses all his future earnings on his music, so he would be left destitute if he went on the lam.

Prosecutors want Durk to stay in jail. Assistant U.S. Attorney Daniel Weiner said Durk has “absolutely no respect for authority” and would “run amok” if let out. Weiner also pointed out that Durk could simply flee to a non-extradition country and make new music. “He could accumulate more wealth,” said the prosecutor.

Judge Fitzgerald ultimately took the bail motion under submission and will issue a written ruling in due course.

Durk was acquitted of murder-for-hire following a two-week trial, in which prosecutors accused him of putting a bounty on Rondo in retaliation for the 2020 killing of his close friend and collaborator King Von. Rondo was shot at a Los Angeles gas station in 2022 and survived, but his cousin Saviay’a Robinson, also known as Lul Pab, was killed in the crossfire.

The remaining count faced by Durk is under the federal Violent Crimes in Aid of Racketeering Activity (VICAR) statute, which broadly criminalizes gang violence. Prosecutors want to argue at next year’s second trial that Durk used his leadership role in OTF to orchestrate the Rondo incident, as well as a 2019 shooting in Atlanta and another 2022 killing in Chicago.

Durk’s attorneys say the VICAR count is “incredibly weak” and that everything related to the Rondo shooting should be excluded under double-jeopardy rules. This is a complex legal question that the attorneys will litigate in court papers over the next several months.

Milcho Manchevski’s ‘Sister Brother Manhole Cover’ Wins Top Prize at Tokyo Film Fest

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Macedonian filmmaker examination of families and the wealth gap takes Grand Prix and brings 10 days of festival to a close.

Milcho Manchevski’s Sister Brother Manhole Cover took the Grand Prix at Tokyo International Film Festival (TIFF), as the event closed as it had opened, on a rainy day in the Japanese capital.

The Macedonian filmmaker’s winner tells of fraternal and maternal love on the edges of society, in his first feature since Kaymak, which was also in competition in Tokyo, in 2022. The prize comes with the Governor of Tokyo Award’s $19,000 (3 million yen). The two leads Sara Klimoska and Filip Trajkovikj were in attendance, along with producer Kortoshev, who accepted the award in place of Manchevski, who had left Tokyo but sent a video message. Related Stories TV Tony Awards to Air on CBS Through 2032 Movies AFM: Dark Sky Takes 'American Dollhouse' for U.S. (Exclusive)

The best director award went to Argentina’s Lisandro Alonso for Double Freedom. The special jury prize was won by Magic Atlas by Sun Xun, which took more than a decade to complete, and became the first animation selected for the main competition in Tokyo. Hu Ling won best actress for The Night That Binds Us, a France-Hong Kong co-production, while Japan’s only win came in the form of Narita Ryo’s best actor for Two Cigarettes, Three Nights.

The jury was headed by Australian filmmaker Rolf de Heer, alongside producers Alex Lo and Oh Jungwan, actor Watanabe Makiko and colorist Yov Moor.

Heysel 85, set against the backdrop of the Heysel Stadium disaster that killed 39 supporters during the 1985 European Cup Final between Liverpool and Juventus, won the audience award for director Teodora Ana Mihai.

Serife Tavsan, by Afghani director Tareq Daoud, won the Asian Future competition for emerging regional filmmakers and a $6,325 (1 million yen) cash prize.

More than 69,000 people attended screenings of 204 films during the 10 days of the 39th edition of the festival across the Hibiya-Marunouchi-Yurakucho-Ginza area, up slightly on last year.
