No Problem with Recognizing Islamic Talaq Divorce from Dubai, When Wife Didn't Want to Stay Married

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

From Schultz v. Stevenson, decided today by California Court of Appeal Judges Peter Krause, Shama Hakim Mesiwala, and Stacy Boulware Eurie:

In 2004, [the parties] purchased a residential property in Grass Valley, California, taking title as "husband and wife as joint tenants." In 2020, [husband] Schultz obtained a judgment of divorce from [wife] Stevenson in Dubai, United Arab Emirates (UAE). The Dubai divorce decree addressed marital status and child custody only; it made no findings related to division of property or spousal support.

Years later, Schultz commenced an action … to partition the Grass Valley property [by ordering it sold and the proceeds split -EV]. Stevenson opposed partition, arguing that the Dubai divorce decree is not enforceable in California and therefore the property is community property and not subject to partition [since California law does not allow partition of community property among married parties -EV]. The trial court ultimately … [decided] in favor of Schultz, finding that the parties are divorced and that the property is not community property….

Stevenson … argues that the trial court abused its discretion by extending comity to the Dubai divorce decree because it was based on Islamic (Sharia) laws that violate state public policy and constitutional principles of equal protection and due process…. [S]he argues that the Dubai divorce is not entitled to comity because it is based on a talaq pronouncement of divorce, which violates California's public policy favoring gender equality and constitutional protections against gender-based disparate treatment. [Generally, a talaq divorce can only be initiated by a husband. -EV] Stevenson contends therefore that she and Schultz are still married, and that the Nevada County property is community property, which is not subject to partition.

We conclude that Stevenson has failed to show that the trial court erred in extending comity to the Dubai divorce decree.

Under the doctrine of comity, courts of this nation generally will give effect to the judgment of a foreign nation if the foreign court had proper jurisdiction and enforcement of the judgment will not prejudice the rights of United States citizens or violate domestic public policy…. Even if we agree that parts of Sharia law—such as a talaq pronouncement of divorce—are contrary to domestic public policy favoring gender equality and constitutional protections against gender-based disparate treatment, the mere fact that some laws of the foreign nation are inconsistent with those of the sovereign does not preclude application of comity.

The basic principle of judicial comity is that "'a court should not assume to disturb another court's disposition of a controversy unless there are good reasons for doing so.'" The standard for refusing to enforce a foreign judgment on public policy grounds is high and infrequently met. The standard "'is not simply that the law is contrary to our public policy, but that it is so offensive to our public policy as to be "'prejudicial to recognized standards of morality and to the general interests of the citizens….'"' [E]ven when a foreign law offends public policy, 'it may still be applied in a limited context where the potential harm is minimal….'" (See In re Dalip Singh Bir's Estate (Cal. App. 1948) [in context of intestate succession, foreign law permitting polygamy may be applied under principles of comity].)

Here, we are not persuaded that extending comity to the Dubai divorce decree impermissibly prejudiced Stevenson's rights or flouted state public policy favoring gender equality. It is important to remember that the Dubai judgment only adjudicated two issues—marital status and child custody—and that the trial court extended comity to the judgment as it related to the status of marriage only. {Stevenson contends that issues related to child custody and support have been rendered moot as the children have reached the age of majority.} Since Stevenson plainly did not wish to remain married to Schultz, we cannot say that it was an abuse of discretion for the trial court to recognize the Dubai judgment as dissolving marital status.

The premise underlying Stevenson's claim of prejudice appears to be that recognizing the foreign divorce precludes her from obtaining spousal support and an equitable division of the marital property. We disagree with this premise…. As noted, the Dubai judgment only adjudicated the issues of marital status and child custody. It did not address, and therefore has no effect upon, spousal support or the division of community property in California….

In reaching this conclusion, we note that the trial court has ordered the partition and sale of property but has not yet determined how the proceeds will be disbursed, reserving jurisdiction "to order the manner in which the sale proceeds are to be disbursed, and to order equitable adjustments that the Court considers just and appropriate." It is therefore premature for us to consider whether the distribution of proceeds amounts to an abuse of discretion….

Robin D. Shofner (Scale LLP) represents Schultz.

NEXT: "The 'Birds Aren't Real' Guy Was Just a Little Ahead of the Curve"

Eugene Volokh is the Thomas M. Siebel Senior Fellow at the Hoover Institution at Stanford, and the Gary T. Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. Naturally, his posts here (like the opinions of the other bloggers) are his own, and not endorsed by any institution. He is also the co-host of the Free Speech Unmuted podcast.

French blood banks hacked amid ‘deeply concerning’ wave of fridge and freezer cyberattacks in Europe

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

In a recent spate of cyberattacks in Europe against fridges and freezers, the latest assault hit France’s national blood service, where hackers gained control of cold storage systems at medical facilities and turned up the temperature.

The French Blood Establishment, EFS, told media that the facilities managed to save the blood supplies and there has been “no loss of blood products.” But the attacks come after supermarkets in several European countries experienced hacks of their fridges and freezers leading to the loss of millions of Euros in products.

Last month, hackers hit hundreds of supermarkets across France where temperatures were forced up to 50 degrees C overnight in what officials called an “unprecedented” attack. Food losses and fires were reported after “connected refrigerators ⁠in several ​major food retailers across France.” What followed was a wave of similar attacks across the continent where fridges and freezers at stores in the U.K., Italy and Germany were recently hacked, following attacks in Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden.

“They are a deliberate attempt to disrupt our food supply chain and undermine ​consumers’ confidence in it – a warning that we must take seriously,” said EU Commissioner for Agriculture Christophe Hansen. “The repeated occurrence of these cyberattacks is deeply concerning.”

Connected fridges can now be controlled and monitored remotely through internet-connected software but with this comes vulnerabilities. Hackers can exploit publicly reachable, legacy or unpatched remote refrigeration management controllers that may use default passwords or weak date-based administrative bypasses.

“Retail is an easy target because retail chains run enormous estates of refrigeration controllers, building management systems, door access panels and point-of-sale gear,” Tom Hegel of the cybersecurity firm SentinelOne, told Reuters.

Cybersecurity experts so far have not figured out who’s behind the attacks. Russian hacking groups are a possibility as well as Iranian hacktivists or nation-state hackers. It might also be kids pulling off what have so far been very expensive pranks. Cybersecurity researchers at some firms have installed fake software as honeypots in an attempt to gather information about the hackers.

After the midterms, a bigger clash over data centers could be coming

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

The November 3 midterm results gave Democrats full control of Congress, setting the stage for what could be one of the most divided U.S. governments this decade, with Republicans in the White House.

Tuesday’s results suggest that anger over data centers, the large facilities that house the computers powering AI, is becoming a political force. With Democrats now in control of Congress, that could lead to new limits on where data centers can be built and how much say local communities have.

Data centers became a ballot-box issue in various states. An NBC exit poll showed how concerned voters are about AI: More than half of respondents believe that AI will do more harm than good, while almost 80% are against the building of data centers in their neighborhoods. Almost 60%, meanwhile, said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their vote. Nadine Smith, president and CEO of the racial justice organization Color of Change, called Tuesday’s election a referendum on “who this economy is built for.”

“National security matters,” Smith said, referring to a common argument from data center supporters that the infrastructure is essential for the U.S. to stay ahead in war and in its rivalry with China. “And so do the families in South Memphis breathing exhaust from the gas turbines powering an AI supercomputer.” (She was referring to xAI’s data center in Memphis, Tennessee, which uses gas turbines for power.)

Voters in several states pushed back against the AI buildout, even as pro-AI super PACs spent millions hoping to sway them this midterm. The anti-data center backlash was especially felt in states like Ohio, where OpenAI has eyed a $500 billion data center, backed by more than $100 billion in financing from Nvidia. In fact, the Ohio county where Meta had planned a 700,000-square-foot facility just passed a ban on data centers, and local communities across the state overwhelmingly voted against data center buildout measures.

Analysts at Morgan Stanley predict that Democratic governor-elect Amy Acton will also pose a major risk to Ohio’s data center buildout. Elsewhere, candidates in Texas, Maine, Michigan, Nevada, Georgia, and South Carolina all won seats after taking a stance against the rapid buildout of data centers. “It is clear that money does not buy outcomes,” said Anshi Bhatt, a tech and policy researcher, adding that in modern politics, creating a “local villain” is one way to build campaign momentum.

Likely next steps include more construction moratoriums or requiring data centers to pay for their own power grid upgrades instead of passing those costs on to nearby communities. Goldman Sachs, in fact, predicts data center construction delays in states like Georgia and Texas because of Democratic gains.

Democratic control of Congress could also improve the odds for bills like the one Sen. Bernie Sanders and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez introduced earlier this year, though any such bill would also face a possible veto. It proposes a nationwide moratorium on new data centers until Congress adds new guardrails and ensures that the buildout does not raise electricity costs, harm the environment, or proceed without community approval.

There are also bills like the GRID Savings Act, proposed by New Mexico Sen. Martin Heinrich, which would prevent grid infrastructure costs from being passed on to communities during large AI data center buildouts.

The environment is also a major concern, especially at xAI’s data center in Memphis and at the data centers throughout Virginia, known as the world capital of data centers, where gas turbines are used for power. Maia Woluchem, program director at Data & Society, a nonprofit research group, believes that changes to energy regulations, land rights, and other environmental policies could affect the data center buildout, especially as congressional Democrats look further into the Trump administration’s rollback of certain energy protections, as the New York Times reported they might.

“Regardless of federal action, I could imagine that we will see additional movement at the state and local level, given the momentum that’s been building from the ground up,” Woluchem continued, citing governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, who supports a statewide moratorium on data centers, as an example. Both Hinojosa and Gov. Greg Abbott called for some limits, but Hinojosa centered much of her campaign on opposing data centers, a notable choice in Texas, whose electrical grid has proved vulnerable over the past decade.

But the hard part is still to come. Smith, for example, is now expecting a fight, or even more executive orders, to bypass what Congress is now likely to block. “Even among Democrats, a blanket freeze is contested,” she said, referring to some Democrats’ worry that a moratorium could slow AI progress and hurt national security.

She also expects AI lobbyists to start calling lawmakers. “Democrats ran on making Big Tech pay,” Smith said. “Our job is to make sure they remember it once the cameras leave.”

The backlash runs deeper than the buildings themselves, which aren’t new. One private equity executive told me recently that people don’t necessarily hate data centers, but rather AI, and that data centers have become a stand-in for people’s fears and anxieties about the technology. “It does look like the results are pointing to a real frustration with tech power at minimum,” Woluchem said, noting that this is also a bipartisan issue.

For the past month, people have told me they are worried about data centers’ effect on the environment and about their lack of say in whether they’re built.

Amazon, for example, built a data center in Gilroy, California, without many residents knowing, by satisfying a 45-year-old zoning law. Vague or outdated zoning laws, along with requirements that local officials sign nondisclosure agreements (NDAs) barring them from discussing data center projects, have only fueled people’s outrage about the buildout. Data centers have also received billions in tax breaks, and although many tech companies have touted the benefits, such as jobs and community programs, for many people, that simply wasn’t enough.

People want to know they still have a say in what happens in their communities. Many also fear that AI buildouts will mainly enrich a few wealthy people while their communities suffer and living costs rise.

“If AI infrastructure is truly a national priority, then the companies building it can afford to pay for their own power, meet clean air standards, disclose what they use, and build with the consent of the people who live next door,” Smith said. “Families should never be subsidizing the richest corporations in history through their utility bills.”

Meet Colleen Allen, the Future of American Fashion

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This is the November 4, 2026 edition of Fit Check, your front row seat to the power players, peacocks, and deep pockets of fashion—and how they influence the rest of the world.

The United States has been overtaken by a blue wave following last night’s midterm elections. James Talarico flipped Texas to make it to the Senate, Chris Pappas of New Hampshire became the first out gay man to be elected to the Senate, and Keisha Lance Bottoms of Georgia is now the first Black woman to be elected governor in the history of the US. There were great memes about what this could mean for the country—largely, the comeback of woke—and some fashion moments worth discussing. Scroll down for a midterm-fashion verdict, including notes on the curious case of a seemingly AI-generated picture featuring Chanel and Valentino products supposedly spotted at a Michigan Democratic Party election night event in support of Abdul El-Sayed.

In other news, Ye is staging a comeback Yeezy show in Los Angeles tonight at the brand’s flagship—keep an eye out tomorrow for a report from my Angeleno colleague Maxwell Adler. Congratulations are in order for Schiaparelli’s Daniel Roseberry, who is having a great week after running the New York City Marathon on Sunday, posting a much-discussed thirst trap on Monday morning, and receiving the Leader in the Arts Award at the Smithsonian’s annual Hirshhorn New York Gala on Monday night. Congratulations as well to the winners and honorees at the 2026 CFDA Awards. More on that very curious evening below, including an interview with Colleen Allen, certified fashion darling and Emerging Designer of the Year winner.

Mentioned in this issue: Jon Ossoff and his tight suit, Colleen Allen, Ralph Lauren, Jill Biden, Melania Trump, Hasan Piker, Zohran Mamdani and Rama Duwaji, and Tim Burton and his new Loewe collaboration.

The CFDA Fashion Awards on Monday night brought some star power to the American Museum of Natural History: Keke Palmer hosted, and presenters included Matt Damon, Cole Escola, Lupita Nyong’o, Laufey, and Demi Moore. And yet the mood of the evening was somewhat tentative. The Council of Fashion Designers of America is under something of a renovation: Steven Kolb, its former CEO, resigned in September following an altercation with PETA protesters at New York Fashion Week. Serving in his stead is Hildy Kuryk, a comms expert who spent four years at Vogue as its executive director of communications following a career in politics. Kuryk is serving as interim CEO and heading up the search for Kolb’s permanent replacement. On Monday night, she stayed out of the spotlight, leaving most of the audience wondering—and gossiping about—who the new face of the CFDA could and should be.

Rachel Scott of Diotima took the main award of the night as womenswear designer of the year, and Anthony Vaccarello, who just left Saint Laurent, was presented with the International Designer of the Year Award by muses Zoë Kravitz, Hailey Bieber, and Rosé. Ralph Lauren was named menswear designer of the year, securing his place as the most awarded designer in CFDA history. Mr. Lauren himself made an appearance—a crowd-pleaser that also left me wondering if the American industry often puts too much emphasis on its past and not enough on its future. In that spirit, I called up Colleen Allen, who took the prize for emerging designer of the year, and asked her to introduce herself to those of you who haven’t met her yet.

Colleen Allen received her award from designer Gabriela Hearst and actor and writer Cole Escola.

José Criales-Unzueta: What would you say is the most important thing for people to know about your background?

Colleen Allen: I often lead with that I always was a menswear designer and was never intending on doing womenswear. That point of view has really guided my work and who I’ve become. I just love the process of making. I did menswear because there wasn’t an identity of women that I saw that I connected with, really, with brands when I was first going to school. And I just loved the craft of tailoring and learning the physical process of cutting a pattern. I think there’s such an ephemerality in making womenswear, in a way. Draping on a mannequin is quite abstract to me. A huge part of my identity is thinking like a tailor.

Where did you go to school, and where did you work before starting your brand?

I did half at Parsons, half at Central Saint Martins. I consider Parsons to be quite technical, and Saint Martins sort of opened up the world conceptually for me and gave me a lot of freedom and thinking about world-building. I was at The Row doing menswear for three years, and then I also interned at Vogue for a minute [and] Calvin Klein 205W39NYC was a very formative experience. When I was an intern there, I was full-time. I was there for eight months, but it was just so cool because it was all in the same room with Raf [Simons, now at Prada], Pieter [Mulier, now at Versace], Matthieu Blazy [now at Chanel]. You knew at that moment how special it was and how crazy it was. I think the world didn’t process it, but when you were in the room, you knew these people were all so important. And so I think being around that every day and absorbing the way that they worked together and they’re such world-builders, all three of them are very conceptual. I think that was a huge informative experience for me.

I think that people know me for color. The feedback that I get from people who buy the clothes is that when you’re wearing a big velvet coat in a color, or even just a dress or a top, people get stopped on the street just because it’s so visually impactful. Velvet is also a signature. That duality of color and texture, and also a bit of a historical Victorian sensibility.

Where do you think that interest in Victorian garments comes from?

There’s an emotional, aesthetic love for it. There’s a bit of a groundedness and a weight to it, and a bit of that period of time was quite spiritual thinking about the afterlife and death, but there was a sense of romance around that that I think really seeped into the clothes in a way that I don’t know if other periods of time really reflect. On the other side of it, it isn’t Victorian specifically, but a lot of historical dress for women is incredible tailoring—thinking about those bodice jackets that have a lot of tiny little panels with lots of little grosgrain finishing on the seams on the inside—and it’s all about molding to the female body. As a menswear designer, I love wearing men’s clothes and wearing a tailored jacket, but I think that the idea of having that sensibility and also reflecting femininity is the perfect storm for me. A lot of that just got lost in a certain period of time once women were able to wear pants, and in the transition of women being able to access menswear, women’s tailoring never got back to that sort of technical level of fitting the body or the female form.

You’ve never done a runway show, which is something that many young designers feel signals their arrival. Why is that?

I love fashion and wanted to work in fashion because I loved watching fashion shows. And specifically, I loved shows that were immersive. I always go back to [Alexander] McQueen [that signaled] fashion is more than product. It is this world-building, cinematic performance art, and you can take it just so much further emotionally. If I’m going to do a show, I want it to carry that level of weight. For me, at my scale, it just feels like the most manageable way to encapsulate a sense of emotion is to do it through a [photo] shoot and showroom appointments for now, just in truly a practical sense. But it is my dream, and I’m working towards having a show. But when I do it, I want it to feel special.

It’s a bit surreal, in a way. It’s especially cool that it’s voted by the industry, and it sounds maybe kind of cheesy, but I do just feel like that is the best reward. That all these people that I’ve worked with are acknowledging [me]. It feels solidifying, grounding. It feels like I’ve been doing this forever, and it’s also going by so fast at the same exact time. It lets me be present and acknowledge myself for how much work has been put into it and that I did it and it’s real and it’s something I dreamed about for so long.

Do you feel like a fashion-industry darling?

[Laugh] Oh my God, how does one answer that question? Gosh, I think that three years ago I was entering these rooms knowing absolutely no one... I think the showroom has actually really fostered real relationships and friendships in the industry, and a sense of community that just feels really good. I work as hard as I can to support whatever the stylists are doing or the editors, I’m always physically running around to make sure that they get the clothes because I love what they do. I love that I know the photographer and the stylist and all the people, and know that if my clothes are in it, it will be amazing for me and they made the effort to reach out to me, to include me in it. The word darling is such an ick word [laugh]. I feel like the word I want to say is camaraderie, because everyone just works so hard. Nobody is flouncing around being a darling. We are all hustling together, and if I can support your hustle and you support mine, that’s the best thing. And when it works, it’s just so satisfying.

Tim Burton, iconoclast filmmaker extraordinaire, has a new capsule with Loewe, which has recreated characters and vignettes from The Nightmare Before Christmas as bags and charms. These kinds of collabs were a staple when Jonathan Anderson was at the brand, and Jack McCollough and Lazaro Hernandez seem to be game to keep them coming with the same endearing whimsy.

In the age of artificial intelligence, the film has become a symbol of human craft and ingenuity. “The more it keeps going that way,” Burton told me yesterday, calling from London and answering a question about the proliferation of AI in film, “the more I appreciate [the human] side of it. I think it’s something that’s very important to keep in the world, the tactile, not being on a screen all the time and doing all that sort of thing. To me, [humanity] is very important to life and art and anything, really.” We also spoke about his collaboration with A$AP Rocky and whether he’d ever do a live-action remake of 2005’s Corpse Bride.

Prediction: Nottingham Forest vs Manchester City

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Turning their attention back to Premier League action on Saturday evening, Manchester City will travel to the City Ground when they meet Nottingham Forest.

With England’s elite turning their attention back to domestic duties this weekend, there are plenty of eye-catching Premier League betting promotions available.

Ahead of Saturday’s showdown from the City Ground, you can find all of the key Nottingham Forest vs Manchester City H2H stats here.

Verdict: Erling Haaland over 1.5 shots on target

Starting with the hosts, while there might have been plenty of excitement around the City Ground following Oliver Glasner’s shock arrival over the summer, Nottingham Forest have drastically failed to find their feet under the ex-Crystal Palace boss. Last seen putting in another concerning display as they slumped to a 2-0 defeat away at Brentford, the Austrian tactician already finds the pressure on his shoulders at a boiling point. Opening this weekend’s fixtures sat in the relegation zone and rightfully coming under a huge amount of scrutiny, Nottingham Forest are bracing themselves for another heavyweight challenge on Saturday evening. Marking their return from September’s extended international break with a 3-1 drumming at Selhurst Park, Glasner’s struggling squad have registered just a sole point from any of their previous five consecutive Premier League appearances.

In what could be a real double injury boost for Nottingham Forest this weekend, Glasner’s side are hopeful that both Nicolas Dominguez and Brazilian marksman Igor Jesus could make their respective returns at the City Ground. However, former Juventus defender Nicolo Savona remains on the sidelines with a long-term knee issue.

Veteran striker Chris Wood could be handed a maiden Premier League start of the season on Saturday evening. Despite notching just a sole top-flight strike since opening up the 2026/27 campaign, England international Morgan Gibbs-White will lead Forest out here.

As for the visitors, while Manchester City might have sacrificed their spot at the top of the Premier League table, Enzo Maresca’s men did claim a much-welcomed European boost in midweek. Dragging themselves to a thrilling 3-2 victory away at German outfit RB Leipzig, the Sky Blues will be looking to get their domestic charge back on track. Now sat three points adrift of reigning Premier League champions Arsenal, the former Chelsea boss will certainly be smelling a golden opportunity on Saturday evening. Landing a 2-1 win when they last travelled to the City Ground for a top-flight showdown back in December 2025, Man City will now be searching for some much-welcomed defensive grit. Including their shock 1-0 loss at home against a high-flying Brighton last weekend, Maresca’s superstar squad have failed to muster a single clean sheet from any of their previous seven consecutive appearances across all competitions.

Sustaining a knee injury last month, Manchester City have confirmed that former Bournemouth star Antoine Semenyo will miss the next couple of months. However, aside from the Ghana international, Maresca’s camp have reported a clean bill of health ahead of Saturday’s trip to Nottingham.

Once again finding the net away in Germany on Wednesday night and already notching nine goals in as many Premier League appearances so far this season, attacking talisman Erling Haaland will spearhead City’s charge. Portuguese international Ruben Dias should be handed an immediate full recall at the City Ground.

Claiming a much-welcomed Champions League boost on Wednesday night as they clinched a thrilling 3-2 victory away at RB Leipzig, Manchester City will now be desperate to get their domestic charge back on track. Landing a 2-1 win when they last travelled to the City Ground 11 months ago, Maresca’s men will be looking to exploit a Nottingham Forest squad who find themselves at the heart of a public nightmare. Failing to win any of their first five showdowns in front of their own supporters (four defeats, one draw), City should be smelling a golden opportunity this weekend.

Verdict: Erling Haaland over 1.5 shots on target

The Sound Shore Fund Q3 2026 Letter To Shareholders

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Thanakorn Lappattaranan/iStock via Getty Images

Thanakorn Lappattaranan/iStock via Getty Images

The Sound Shore Fund Investor Class (SSHFX) and Institutional Class (SSHVX) declined 1.37% and 1.34%, respectively, in the third quarter of 2026, trailing the Russell 1000 Value Index (Russell Value) which advanced 2.61%, and the Standard & Poorâs 500 Index (S&P 500) which advanced 2.30%. The three-year annualized gains for SSHFX of 19.67% and for SSHVX of 19.91% were behind of the Russell Valueâs 20.07% and the S&P 500âs 22.89%. As long-term investors, we highlight that Sound Shoreâs 35-year annualized returns of 10.44% and 10.70%, for SSHFX and SSHVX, respectively, as of September 30, 2026, were ahead of the Russell Value at 10.38% and trailed the S&P 500 at 11.02%. For the Fund's most recent standardized performance information, click here.

The stock market demonstrated continued resilience in the third quarter of 2026, with the capitalization- weighted S&P 500 gaining 2.3% despite broad macroeconomic headwinds. Steady earnings growth and sturdy economic fundamentals provided a solid backdrop for equities during an otherwise volatile quarter marked by rising interest rates, higher oil prices, and geopolitical uncertainty. Although mega-cap technology and artificial intelligence enthusiasm continued to drive overall market performance, breadth weakened, as demonstrated by a 2% decline in the Equal Weight S&P 500 and a 7% drop in the Russell 2000 Index for the quarter.

Last quarter we touched on the concentration that has crept into the Russell Value. In the first half of 2026, a handful of semiconductor stocks drove performance. Then at the end of June, the Russell went through a reconstitution and technology became the largest sector at over 20% of the index, with three companies each accounting for a 5% weighting. Not exactly the return to value that many investors had hoped for. Below, we break down the year-to-date performance of the Russell Value and illustrate the outsized impact these technology weightings have hadâ¦the most telling datapoint is that the median stock in the Russell 1000 Value Index is up less than 1% YTD.

U.S. Treasury yields rose across the maturity spectrum during the third quarter, with the 10-year Treasury yield reaching 5.29% - its highest quarter-end level since June 2007. As a result, interest rate-sensitive stocks came under pressure, driven by algorithmic program trading that executes baskets of similar stocks with little regard for fundamental differentiation. One such stock was building products distributor QXO (QXO), which proved to be a detractor for the quarter. We initiated our position in the fourth quarter of 2025, focused on the track record of QXOâs CEO, Brad Jacobs. Jacobs is a serial entrepreneur, with proven success in previous roll-ups at United Waste Systems, United Rentals (URI), and XPO (XPO). He leverages scale and technology investments to outperform peers and we expect the same outcome at QXO. The company is currently rolling out critical technology investments, such as new warehouse management systems, which should drive double-digit organic earnings growth over multiple years. Despite the positive momentum, the stock trades at a discount to its distribution peers and we have been adding to the position.

Concerns over higher oil prices - much like rising interest rates â also rippled through the market. Domestic carrier Southwest Airlines (LUV) gave back a portion of its previous quarter's gains as fears of a protracted war pressured consumer discretionary and travel stocks. Despite these challenges, Southwest maintains a robust balance sheet and is benefiting from a multiyear restructuring designed to drive higher earnings and returns on capital. To boost margins, management is implementing key structural changes, including premium legroom, preferred seating, and checked bag fees. These initiatives enhance product pricing power and position the airline to restore returns to historical standards. With estimated earnings power exceeding $6 per share, we believe Southwest remains a compelling holding in the portfolio.

Sometimes it can be helpful to step back and analyze multi-year trends to identify an opportunity set. The chart below tells the story of the haves and have-nots over the last two years. As referenced earlier, technology has ripped and been the engine driving the indices higher, while the health care sector underperformed. Although always selective, our portfolio does have exposure to health care. Weâve been adding on the weakness and our patience paid off this quarter as a number of our health care holdings led our contributors list.

A new addition to the portfolio and our top performer for the quarter was Thermo Fisher Scientific (TMO) (Thermo), a leading life sciences tool and lab equipment supplier that we have owned successfully in the past. We purchased the stock in June 2026, when it traded below its historical average P/E multiple despite expected double-digit earnings growth and a 5% free cash flow yield. Following the post-pandemic boom, life science and biotech companies aggressively destocked inventory, while academic research funding faced short-term headwinds. We believe this cyclical slowdown has bottomed and that Thermoâs end-market demand is re-accelerating toward historical growth rates. Additionally, the company benefits from the pharma industryâs reshoring through its contract development and manufacturing division, which provides high-margin sterile manufacturing and packaging services. The position is off to a good start and we see upside from here.

Global oil super-major BP was also a strong third quarter contributor as it benefitted from both internal restructuring efforts and higher oil prices. Our research returned us to the stock - previously a successful holding - when it was trading at a very attractive 4 times cash flow with a strong balance sheet and solid dividend. BPâs new and externally hired CEO, Meg OâNeill, is simplifying its portfolio by exiting low return assets, cutting capital spending, and re-focusing on its high-quality legacy energy businesses. The company plans to improve returns on capital employed by 4% with no help from oil prices, which should yield better than consensus earnings growth and an improved valuation against any commodity backdrop.

Equity markets continue to navigate a complex framework of geopolitical tension and persistent macroeconomic uncertainty. The upcoming U.S. midterm elections are likely to drive headline volatility as the market weighs potential shifts in fiscal, regulatory, and tax policy. However, historical precedent shows that market clarity generally improves once election outcomes are resolved. We are sometimes asked what kind of market does Sound Shore tend to lag, and our answer is always the same. When a select few stocks (often in one industry) gather momentum and reach unprecedented levels of valuation, our disciplined investment process means we are unlikely to fully participate. Throughout these cycles, we have remained true to our strategy, looking past short-term noise to focus on attractive risk-reward opportunities elsewhere. And right now, we see plenty.

Sound Shore remains focused in our approach â concentrating on cash-flow-resilient businesses with strong balance sheets, led by disciplined management teams capable of compounding value through elevated capital costs and margin pressures. Currently, our portfolio is attractively valued at an average twelve-month forward P/E ratio of 12.7 times versus the S&P of 18.7 times and the Russell 1000 Value of 16.4 times. We appreciate your investment alongside ours and encourage you to reach out with any questions or comments.

Pixar Promotes Jonathan Garson to President, Taking Reins from Retiring Executive Jim Morris

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Jonathan Garson has been promoted to the role of president at Pixar Animation Studios, while standing executive Jim Morris moves into the role of president emeritus as he prepares for retirement. The Walt Disney Company, which owns Pixar, announced the leadership transition Wednesday afternoon.

Garson, who has notched 30 years at Disney and most recently served as Pixar’s general manager after leading its marketing and franchise team, will become president on January 4. The role will see him oversee production and operations at the animation giant, partnering with chief creative officer Pete Docter.

Disney states that Morris, who has been with Pixar for 21 years and served as president for the past 12, will remain involved with Pixar through an executive producer role on “Incredibles 3” (due out in June 2028), along with talent development, mentorship and technology initiatives. Both Garson and Morris will report to Alan Bergman, chairman of Disney Entertainment and Studios for The Walt Disney Company. Related Stories IndieWire and Disney to Celebrate Emmy Rossum and the Cast of ‘Furious’ at Special Pass the Remote Screening After ‘Avengers Endgame: Encore,’ Infinity Vision Might Turn Out to Be a Thing

“Having worked closely with Jim Morris for more than 20 years, I have enormous respect for his leadership and unwavering commitment to Pixar and its people,” Bergman wrote in a statement leading the press release on the leadership changes. “He’s not only a tremendous partner in business but a great friend. As we prepare for his eventual retirement, this transition will ensure a smooth succession for the team.”

Bergman continues, “With a deep background at both Pixar and Disney, Jonathan Garson has proven to be an exceptional leader with a strong sense of what makes Pixar unique and how its storytelling comes to life in so many ways for fans, and Pete Docter and I are excited for him to step into this role. In addition, Jonas Rivera brings a wealth of creative expertise that will serve the team well as he works even more closely with Pete.”

Morris’ statement follows: “Leading Pixar has been the privilege of a lifetime, and I’m honored to have been a steward of its legacy alongside extraordinary artists, storytellers, and technologists who keep redefining what animation can do and expanding the art of cinema. After 52 years in the business, and almost 40 at Lucasfilm and Pixar, I’ll be stepping back from the day-to-day, but I’m thrilled to move into handling a number of special projects, working alongside my friends Pete, Jonathan, and Jonas at Pixar, and of course Alan Bergman, who has been such a supporter of Pixar throughout the years. Looking ahead, I can’t think of anyone better than Jonathan to carry the legacy of this beloved studio forward. He’s a Pixar person through and through, a fierce champion of our films, our people, and our culture. Pixar is in very good hands.”

Garson then writes, “I still remember seeing ‘Toy Story’ for the first time – never imagining I’d one day work at Pixar, let alone step into a leadership role such as this. Every day I walk in the door here, I’m reminded just how special this place is and how lucky I am to be a part of it. Jim has shown me what it looks like to safeguard this culture of quality, innovation, and creative ambition, and I’m extremely grateful to both him and Alan for their trust and support. It’s a humbling opportunity, and I look forward to working with Pete, Jonas, the production leadership team led by Katherine Sarafian and Lindsey Collins, and all of the extraordinary talent here at Pixar as we continue to push boundaries and lead our studio into the future.”

Along with Garson, Pixar will also bump Jonas Rivera to executive vice president of creative, overseeing directors, creative vice presidents, development, casting, and content advisement departments, in partnership with Docter. The two will serve as executive producers on all Pixar films.“Ever since I walked through these doors as Pixar’s first production intern, this place, its people, and its stories have been my life’s work,” said Rivera. “I am deeply honored to step into this new role alongside Pete, partnering with him and Jonathan to support our incredible filmmakers and artists. Jim has been an extraordinary mentor and leader, and I intend to build on the lessons I learned from him as we look toward our next chapter.”

Pixar is coming off of a sequel success in “Toy Story 5,” which earned over $1.1 billion at the global box office to rank as the 10th-highest-grossing animated feature of all time. Up next are two original features — “Gatto” in March and “Ghost Market” in March 2028 — followed by “Incredibles 3.”

Nick Bosa returns to practice with limited work

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

49ers defensive end Nick Bosa returned to practice Wednesday after missing the team’s past five games with a calf injury. Bosa was a limited participant.

“It’s a long year, and I kind of just reminded myself that I need to be ready for the long haul,” Bosa said, via Harrison Rich of the San Francisco Chronicle.

Bosa tore his right ACL in a Sept. 21, 2025, game against the Cardinals and underwent surgery five days later. As he was coming back this summer, Bosa experienced soreness and tendinitis in his right knee, and the 49ers backed him down until Sept. 2 when he returned to practice.

He injured his left calf while warming up in practice for Week 3.

“I must have had some type of compensation going on, especially in that first week back,” Bosa said. “I was kind of trying to push it back with the [right knee] tendinitis, so I may have been favoring [the left calf] a little bit. I was just doing my normal warm-up drills one day, and I tweaked it. Calves are a pretty long process. I’ve never had one, so it took a little longer than I expected, but I don’t even think about it anymore.”

Wide receiver Mike Evans (ribs/Achilles) remained out of practice, and running back Christian McCaffrey (hamstring) also didn’t practice.

Wide receiver Deebo Samuel (hamstring), safety Siran Neal (knee/foot) and offensive tackle Trent Williams (rest) were the other players who sat out Wednesday’s session.

Linebacker Luke Gifford (groin), cornerback Nate Hobbs (adductor), wide receiver Christian Kirk (knee), wide receiver Demarcus Robinson (ankle), safety Marques Sigle (ankle), cornerback Upton Stout (shoulder) and linebacker Garret Wallow (hamstring) were limited.

Meta brings Conductor on board its model train

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Agent orchestration startup gets its exit

Meta has acquired AI coding startup Conductor, signaling interest in applying AI to software engineering tasks.

Charlie Holtz, the startup's co-founder and CEO, made the announcement in a blog post. He also revealed that the company's hosted Conductor Cloud, a sandbox for hosted agents, will go read-only on November 13 and shut down on November 20.

Conductor, the eponymous company's macOS app for running multiple coding agents simultaneously in isolated copies of Git projects, will continue working for the next few months, and the biz expects to provide a way to transfer Conductor Cloud workspaces to local machines.

But in early 2027, Conductor will no longer receive new models or critical bug fixes.

A Meta spokesperson confirmed that the acquisition will strengthen the company's developer-oriented AI capabilities but declined to elaborate further. Perhaps Conductor will play nicely with Muse Code, Meta's recently introduced coding agent.

Having spent heavily on strengthening its AI research lab, Meta has moderated its appetite for big acquisitions since April when China blocked its $2 billion offer for Manus AI, a developer of software agents.

Meta's other acquisitions this year, Moltbook, Assured Robot Intelligence, and Stilla, are also AI-oriented but more modest in scope.

The Conductor deal comes without an announced price, and follows in the footsteps of Meta regaining credibility as a model provider: Llama 4 disappointed but Meta's recent Muse Wonder and Spark models rank respectably among the top LLMs at the moment.

In Meta's most recent conference call for investors [PDF], CEO Mark Zuckerberg said his company aims to provide AI agents that non-technical people can deploy for business tasks.

"What we see is a lot of people are actually using Muse today to help run especially small businesses," he said. "And we think that there's both an opportunity to help create an agent that people use to really enable them to automate and do a lot of the parts of spinning up a small business that are just kind of difficult and time-consuming but aren't the core thing that a person is trying to do."

Meta's AI strategy, as Zuckerberg describes it, is focused on collecting transaction fees from businesses for enabling agentic commerce, selling advanced AI capabilities to developers in the form of model access or tokens, charging for Muse to automate business tasks, and applying AI to personalize and improve results from ads.

It's a good time to run an AI-related startup, as leading model companies seek to add value beyond offering AI models. Models themselves have become commoditized to the point that only a few leading model makers can get away with charging a premium. In an effort to maintain that lead, top model makers have been purchasing innovation (and talent) while they are still flush with investor funds.

On Wednesday, OpenAI said it plans to acquire Pierre Computer Company, maker of distributed storage service Code Storage⁠ and open-source developer tools. And a few months earlier, the ChatGPT maker snapped up Ona, which makes cloud development environments for agents.

Anthropic's most recent developer tools deal involved Stainless back in May. Its purchase of Opaque Systems, which offers confidential computing technology, could also be squeezed into the developer tool category.

At some point, when it becomes clearer what kinds of AI services people will pay for, startup acquisitions may become more focused. But for the moment, with software development being the most successful use of AI, makers of AI-oriented developer tooling have a shot at a glorious exit. ®

Arsenal have ‘a lot to learn’ after nervy Slavia win, says Arteta

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Mikel Arteta believes Arsenal still have "a lot to learn" after they edged to a 2-1 win over Slavia Prague in the Champions League.

It looked like Arsenal were set to cruise to victory after Noni Madueke and Viktor Gyokeres both scored in the first half to put them in control.

Mikel Merino had also missed a number of chances before the break, and they could have proved costly as Arsenal put in a sluggish performance in the second half.

Wiktor Nowak scored Slavia's consolation in the 90th minute, but Arsenal had ridden their luck before that as Mojmir Chytil had an effort cleared off the line and David Raya made a stunning save to tip Oskar Kubiak's header onto the post.

Slavia had 17 shots in the second half – the joint-most in a single half against Arteta's Arsenal in a Champions League match (also 17 in the second half for Sporting CP in November 2024), while their 2.11 expected goals (xG) was the most any side has managed against the Gunners since 2023-24 in one half.

Arsenal still came out with three points to maintain their unbeaten start to the Champions League, while they have already won 13 matches in all competitions this season, the joint-most of any side in Europe's big-five leagues, along with Barcelona and Real Madrid.

But Arteta was far from happy with his side's second-half performance and is determined to learn from that.

"Always we need to value winning away in the Champions League," Arteta told TNT Sports. "It is extremely difficult.

"Two very different halves. We should have had three or four goals in the first half, but we didn't do it. Right at the start of the second half, we were on the back foot, very sloppy and gave balls away. Then we had to suffer. A lot to learn.

"We should have scored more in the first half, and if we didn't do that, we had to go and do that in the second half.

"We made six changes [to the starting line-up from their win over Liverpool], but we know the quality of the players we have. Whether we were lacking something, I don't know, but I would have done the same thing [again].

"I value what the team has done now; it is 11 away games we played."

Job done in Prague https://t.co/qTzno2ZLWv

Since the 2024-25 season, only Paris Saint-Germain (25) have won more Champions League games than Arsenal (23).

And while the Gunners have played seven games against sides from Czechia (three versus Sparta Prague, four against Slavia) without losing (W6 D1), this is the first time they have conceded on Czech soil.

Raya played a big part in ensuring Arsenal walked away with the victory, and like Arteta, pointed to the huge differences between the two halves.

"First half, we played really, really well," he told TNT Sports.

"We created a lot of chances and could have scored more, but the second half was completely different. They stepped up a gear and made it difficult for us, and we have to learn from it. Delighted with the win, but a lot of things to improve going forward.

"Maybe they started pressing a bit higher with more intensity. We had too many giveaways, especially in the final third. There was too much running from box to box, and we have to manage those reactions and go for the third and fourth goal.

"At the end of the day, it is three points."

Mourinho: There isn’t a player like Bellingham in the world of football today

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Jose Mourinho believes "there isn't a player like Jude Bellingham in the world today" after the midfielder impressed in Real Madrid's win over AEK Athens.

Los Blancos defeated the Greek side 3-2 in the Champions League on Wednesday after a 92nd-minute winner from captain Federico Valverde.

AEK twice took the lead, first through Aboubakary Koita and then Stavros Pilios, but Bellingham and Kylian Mbappe were on hand to restore parity on both occasions.

Madrid have now scored more 90th-minute winning goals than any other team in the Champions League, with Valverde's their ninth in the competition.

Bellingham's goal came after 32 minutes, as he expertly flicked Marc Cucurella's low cross into the bottom-right corner.

Bellingham has now been directly involved in 30 goals in the Champions League (16 goals, 14 assists).

He is the sixth-youngest player to reach 30 in the competition (23 years 128 days) and the third-youngest European, only behind Mbappe (20y 356d) and Erling Haaland (22y 76d).

Bellingham also led in final-third entries (14) and passes in the final third (26 – level with Eduardo Camavinga), while winning seven of his nine duels and winning 100% of his tackles (2/2).

"Since I arrived, he's been doing well," Mourinho said on Bellingham.

"He's a unique player, I think. There isn't a player like Jude in the world of football today. He's complete in every way.

"I don't have many words, because for me, he's an absolutely extraordinary player. And even when he doesn't have a great game, one of those spectacular ones… even when he doesn't, he always shows hard work, leadership, ambition, quality, attacking prowess, goals.

"He's an extraordinary player. I repeat: I'm very lucky to have a squad of this calibre."

The name's Jude. Jude Bellingham  https://t.co/ugc980JzIH

On the win itself, Mourinho added: "Yes, very satisfied, obviously. We played against a very difficult team. A team that played very well, better than ever.

"[Luka] Jovic had an extraordinary game as a reference point. Players with legs, with the intensity to get forward from deep.

"They're the reason we didn't play a perfect game. It's them. They played a great game."

PP-2 turntable reads vinyls without tonearm and inspires glass listening room in tokyo

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

engraved floor hatches reveal a hidden record collection inside a conceptual listening room by paris-based studio waiting for ideas.

Paris-based studio Waiting For Ideas has introduced the PP-2, a second-generation aluminum turntable that uses light to read vinyl, alongside [Pause], a conceptual listening room envisioned for Tokyo. Imagined as a glass cube suspended above water, the space places the turntable at its center, with a record collection concealed beneath the floor. Together, the two designs consider how the physical experience of listening to music can extend from an object into architecture.

When the studio introduced the PP-1 in 2025, its appeal was immediately visible: a solid square of machined aluminum with no exposed tonearm. Records sit upside down, with a concealed mechanism reading them from below. The PP-2 preserves that familiar appearance, including the minimal controls, while introducing significant changes to the hardware inside.

The new PP-2 uses an optical stylus system that detects the needle’s movement through light as it follows the record’s grooves. The needle still makes physical contact with the vinyl, but its motion is translated into an audio signal without the magnets and coils found in conventional cartridges. According to Waiting For Ideas, this reduces the moving mass of the stylus, allowing it to follow the grooves more precisely and retrieve finer details from the recording.

Inside the CNC-machined aluminum body, an axial-flux direct-drive motor adjusts its rotational speed 1,000 times per second, working alongside a Harmony analog preamplifier developed for the optical pickup. New RCA outputs allow the PP-2 to connect directly to hi-fi systems, while a redesigned door protects the stylus from dust between uses. The turntable retains automatic speed detection for 33⅓ and 45 rpm records.

Alongside the new turntable, Waiting For Ideas introduces [Pause], an architectural concept that takes the physical experience of vinyl listening beyond the tabletop. Renderings envision a glass cube rising from a concrete platform suspended above water in central Tokyo. The PP-2 occupies the center of the enclosure, surrounded by an otherwise sparse interior. Transparent walls open the room toward the surrounding architecture, while a grid of square panels interrupts the floor.

Each panel conceals part of a vinyl record collection, with artists’ names engraved into the surface. Visitors would lift a hatch to retrieve their chosen album before bringing it to the turntable. It’s a curious reversal of the conventional listening room, where records typically line shelves along the walls. Here, the music library becomes part of the architecture itself, and choosing an album involves physically opening the floor.

The concept draws on the same thinking behind the PP-2, where familiar actions are simplified while the physical character of vinyl remains. Inside [Pause], the process of finding a record becomes an experience of its own. The individual hatches give each album a designated place, while the turntable sits in the middle of an open room. The surrounding water offers a visual separation from the city beyond the glass.

For now, [Pause] exists only as a rendered proposal, with images rendered by Julia Ippolito. The PP-2, meanwhile, is available to order, with an assembly lead time of three to four weeks. While the new turntable refines the technology of the original PP-1, the accompanying architectural concept explores what happens when an entire room is designed around the simple act of putting on a record.

studio: Waiting for Ideas | @waiting.for.ideas

photography: © Mathilde Hiley | @mathildehiley

visualizations: © Julia Ippolito | @juliaippolito

Cinematography Legend Robert Richardson Answered a Film Student’s Email — Their Conversation Became ‘The White Devil’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Robert Richardson has spent decades answering questions about how he makes movies look the way they do. The legendary cinematographer is a regular collaborator of Martin Scorsese, Oliver Stone, and Quentin Tarantino, with three Oscars to his name — for “JFK,” “The Aviator,” and “Hugo.” But something caught his attention when a film student from the Czech Republic emailed him in 2016: Jana Hojdová wanted to know about everything else.

“I get a number of requests for this kind of work and I am not really interested,” Richardson told IndieWire. “There wasn’t one that was technical. It was who are you? Where do you come from? What’s your life like?” Related Stories ‘The Ballad of Lieutenant Dan,’ Documentary About Gary Sinise’s Work with Veterans, Acquired by Freestyle Digital Media ‘Wildwood’ Director Travis Knight on How Live-Action Filmmaking Shaped His Stop-Motion Animated Adventure

Richardson started writing Hojdová back, and a decade later, their correspondence has grown into a friendship and Hojdová’s feature directorial debut, “Robert Richardson: The White Devil.” They’ve also got an ongoing book project, which aims to organize and share even more of the film’s underlying research: an intellectual arsenal backed by the vast archives from Richardson’s house in Massachusetts.

“350 pages and we are not done,” Hojdová said, sitting next to Richardson for a Q&A after a private screening of her film at Soho House West Hollywood on October 29. “This is our homework in Los Angeles,” she said. “That we need to finish our book.” Richardson smiled.

“No is not in her vocabulary. It’s a lifetime achievement here.”

Hojdová originally approached Richardson in support of her master’s thesis at Prague’s FAMU film school. The daughter of a director and cinematographer herself, she told IndieWire she also grew up watching Richardson’s films with her family in the Czech Republic. Richardson’s first response felt “life-changing” for Hojdová and, by the time she traveled to New Zealand to meet him in 2017 on the production of Baltasar Kormákur’s “Adrift,” she said she thought she really knew the man behind the movies. In person, it became apparent just how much Hojdová still had to ask.

“I started to capture everything that he’s doing,” she said. “He’s driving [his] car to the set, his eating, everything.” Watching Richardson work and go about his daily life felt like an obvious film to Hojdová, but Richardson was less convinced. “My life is not interesting,” she recalled him saying.

Still, Hojdová kept shooting, and after six months of discussion, Richardson agreed to the project on the condition that its director would be shown, too. At first, that presented a problem for the young filmmaker who, like Richardson, greatly prefers the other side of the camera.

“I don’t like my voice, I don’t like watching myself,” she said. “It was like, ‘OK, I will say yes, but maybe I will not really be in there.’” Then, the pandemic brought her and Richardson even closer.

In 2020, what began as a short visit to look through photos and records at Richardson’s place in Cape Cod expanded as Hojdová realized just how much material the artist had kept in his basement — and lockdown forced her to extend her stay. The two ultimately wound up living together for months.

“If we didn’t have COVID, it wouldn’t be a movie,” Richardson said. “It wouldn’t be the same movie in any way at all.”

Four years after first contacting her hero, Hojdová said she knew many of Richardson’s personal stories but had previously lacked the material to put them on-screen. Suddenly, she was surrounded by countless scenes that could go with his memories, and observing Richardson, effectively in captivity, for weeks on end gave Hojdová even more to ask.

“I said to Bob that he has to be happy because we are filming at his house and he doesn’t need to go somewhere to shoot,” Hojdová said. “We are there.”

“It was really hell,” Richardson joked. “I mean seriously, you were ready to shoot every day. I had an assignment every day.” Later in the conversation, he offered another description: “She’s a spy. She convinced me that it was cool to do this, bit by bit.”

Eventually, Hojdová faced 650 hours of footage and the overwhelming task of finding the feature documentary inside it. Shaping all that material meant deciding how much of her own relationship with Richardson actually belonged on screen. It wasn’t until Hojdová reached the editing room that she fully appreciated her subject’s original condition. Viewers needed someone to accompany them into Richardson’s world — and the curious cinematographer trying to understand him? She was the way in.

“I had to fight for me being in the film, which was very hard for me because I don’t like it,” Hojdová said. “But I realized it was a great choice because I think people can connect more with me than with Bob.”

The filmmaker’s plan to bring Richardson’s personal life into conversation with the all-timer movies he helped create presented another challenge for Hojdová in post. Working extensively with executive producer Matej Chlupácek, she had to secure legal permission to use dozens of iconic images. Finishing “The White Devil” edit meant beginning an extensive licensing process, with roughly 390 entries to clear.

“It was really a nightmare, but we did it,” she said.

Of course, Richardson — whom Hojdová warmly described as “impatient” — wanted to see results as soon as possible. Still, she said he trusted the process and, more importantly, his director’s judgment.

“She was very smart,” Richardson said. “Her choices were good. She was persistent and I thought working with her was a pleasure.” Really watching the film he was asking about? That’s another matter.

Sprawled out in the front row of Soho House’s plush private theater, Richardson said the October 29 evening in L.A. felt like his first real viewing. “The White Devil” had its world premiere at the Czech Republic’s Karlovy Vary International Film Festival on July 5 and will make its North American premiere at DOC NYC on November 14. But at Karlovy Vary, where Richardson also received an award, he said he was too overwhelmed to absorb the portrait.

“I was not in body — because, then, you’re sitting in front of 1500 people and they’re playing this film,” he said. “I don’t remember half of it.”

A thrilling and sensitive consideration of cinematic genius, “The White Devil” explores Richardson’s sometimes contentious relationships with crews, his evolving collaborations with elite directors, a deeply troubled personal life, and even his experiences with psychedelic drugs. But the difficulty of living with such beautiful and raw honesty became apparent during a sweet exchange between Richardson and Hojdová in front of the Soho House audience.

Asked about watching the film, Richardson said, “When I was sitting here watching it, I was like, ‘Oh, this isn’t bad. This isn’t so bad. We could cut that down a little bit.’”

“But how was it for you to see?” Hojdová pressed in response. Richardson paused.

“I don’t like watching this movie because it’s a very transparent approach to who I am,” he said. “Most times you would think the documentary wouldn’t necessarily have as much of the shadow side.” Hojdová reminded him that exposure was part of the understanding they’d reached together: “You told me that if this film can tell someone something, it needs to be transparent.”

Speaking to the audience, Richardson turned to the subject of his five children and, as he does in “The White Devil,” took responsibility for the personal connections he sees as damaged by his deep commitment to a demanding artistic career.

“Looking back on that, it’s very hard,” he said. “I haven’t been able to fall back into a good relationship with the first two. The third has become very close.” He also reflected: “I’m trying. It’s a long time when you’re addicted to one form of drug or another. You’ve got to wean yourself off that particular methodology.”

Richardson’s youngest gave him something lighter to discuss: “I’m pretty close to the three year old, but he’s a monster,” he joked. “He is worse than I was as a child. He needs to be sent away to school now.”

Hojdová herself has a three-year-old daughter, and the adult friends’ children were born only a month apart. Still, “The White Devil” director sometimes played the parent steering Richardson onstage.

“Did you learn something?” Hojdová asked.

“I learned pain and I learned that now I should try to work harder, try to make the family work better,” Richardson said. After 10 years, a student’s shot-in-the-dark inquiry had become a genuine discovery on both sides — with each artist maturing and educating the other along the way.

“She started in her 20s in this film. Now she’s in her 30s. It’s a long time that you grow together,” Richardson said of Hojdová. “She’s seen all the worst sides of me anybody could see. She’s probably been the second longest relationship with a female in my life.”

“Robert Richardson: The White Devil” screens at DOC NYC on November 14 and 16 at IFC Center. The film is also streaming in the U.S. through November 29 with the purchase of an online screening ticket.

Man United transfer news: Scouts 'intensify' efforts to sign £38m defender as Michael Carrick endures crisis in the backline

By Lewis Nolan | 05 Nov 2026 00:27

Manchester United are in the race for Como defender Jacobo Ramon Naveros, the newest report has revealed.

It is understandable that the Red Devils are already looking forward to the next few transfer windows considering the club are 17th in the Premier League table with just seven points.

Michael Carrick's side are only above the relegation zone because of goal difference, and part of the reason they are so low is because of their defence.

United have conceded at least two goals five times in the Premier League so far this season, and the squad are in desperate need of reinforcements at the back.

A new report from TEAMtalk claims that United's scouting of Como defender Ramon is set to intensify, especially following his game against the Red Devils in the Champions League in October.

Ramon has primarily operated as a centre-back during his career, and the defender has shown that he can contribute at both ends of the pitch.

The 21-year-old managed to score against United after he rotated into midfield and decided to make an unchallenged run into the penalty area, before heading home from close range.

There are no question marks about the Spaniard's ability on the ball, but he also routinely wins his individual battles at the other end of the pitch, with the centre-back coming out on top in 67% of his duels in Serie A this season.

Though Ramon is valued by Transfermarkt at €45m (£37.51m), United will almost certainly have to pay significantly more than that price, but his potential is high.

It may sound hyperbolic, but United have one of the Premier League's weakest defences, with the likes of Luke Shaw and Harry Maguire far beyond their best.

Lisandro Martinez's mentality has been questioned, while many fans are keen for right-back Diogo Dalot to be replaced.

The need to strengthen at the back is urgent, and given United arguably find themselves in a relegation battle, they cannot afford to ignore their defence any longer.

Crocodylians rather than mammals were the top hunters in their ancient habitat

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Fossil bite marks reveal that giant crocodylians were likely the leading predators of huge mammals about 12 million years ago

South America looked very different during the Middle Miocene, roughly 11 to 13 million years ago. In what is now Colombia, rivers and wetlands were home to giant turtles, fish, snakes, huge plant-eating mammals, flightless terror birds, carnivorous mammals, and many kinds of crocodylians.

Crocodylians include living crocodiles, alligators, and caimans, along with their extinct relatives.

New fossil evidence suggests that these reptiles were likely the leading predators of the largest land mammals in this tropical community.

The fossils came from La Venta in Colombia’s Tatacoa Desert.

Mammalian predators lived there too, but there were not many of them, so other large predators, like crocodylians, played an important role.

Oscar Wilson, a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Helsinki, worked with colleagues to examine this puzzle using direct physical evidence.

Earlier work had predicted a major role for giant crocodylians by comparing the combined body masses of predators and prey.

“There was so much prey available that mammalian predators couldn’t have eaten it all,” Wilson said.

“Our new study shows that crocodylians in particular were the region’s most important predators, especially when it came to hunting large prey.”

The team studied four fossils from three types of large plant-eating mammals that once lived in South America. These animals were similar in some ways to the large hoofed mammals that live today.

The main evidence came from marks that other animals left on the bones. These included deep tooth marks, small dents, grooves, cracks, and crushed areas.

The researchers photographed each fossil and created 3D digital models of several specimens. They also used a CT scan on one jaw to see inside the fossil without cutting or damaging it.

They measured the bite marks and compared them with fossil teeth.

The researchers also estimated how much force was needed to make the marks in fresh bone. This information helped them identify the most likely predators.

One Pericotoxodon skull carried a nearly circular puncture about 1.15 inches (29.2 millimeters) wide, along with smaller pits and grooves.

A leg bone from the same species held five deep punctures and four shallow pits, showing that several teeth had contacted the bone.

A Xenastrapotherium lower jaw had two large punctures surrounded by fractures and heavily crushed areas. The marks passed through the hard outer layer of bone, pointing to a powerful bite delivered while the bone was fresh.

A young Granastrapotherium jaw also showed punctures, crushed bone, and drag marks from teeth.

One sharply curved groove may record violent movement, such as shaking, thrashing, or the rolling motion used by living crocodiles during feeding.

The animals’ estimated weights ranged from about 1,360 to 4,020 pounds (616 to 1,822 kilograms). However, some body mass estimates came from tooth measurements, a method that may overestimate the size of these unusual extinct mammals.

The strongest match was Purussaurus neivensis, a giant relative of modern caimans that may have reached about 23 feet in length and weighed close to 3,950 pounds.

Its front teeth were thick and conical, with some measured adult teeth reaching about 1.7 inches (43.7 millimeters) wide at the base.

Their size and rounded shape closely matched the largest punctures, while ridges along some tooth edges could account for marks that split into two points.

Other local crocodylians had teeth that were too small, too oval, or too flat and serrated to fit the main wounds.

Combined estimates from several punctures exceeded 9,000 pounds of force (40,000 newtons) and sometimes approached 15,700 pounds of force (70,000 newtons).

The estimate from the skull was probably too high because the tooth passed through thinner bone than that found in the jaws and leg bone.

“The frequency of marks likely made by Purussaurus suggests that it was an important predator in these tropical ecosystems,” Wilson said.

Fossils rarely reveal whether a predator killed an animal or scavenged it after death, and these bones cannot answer that question with certainty.

None showed signs of healing, which rules out wounds that had begun to repair. However, that fact alone does not distinguish hunting from scavenging.

Three damaged fossils came from the head, while the fourth came from the lower leg near the knee.

Living crocodiles often seize prey by the skull or legs during an ambush, and experiments with pig carcasses have produced skull damage similar to the marks on the Pericotoxodon fossil.

The curved mark on the Granastrapotherium jaw suggests that a predator fed on it using strong, violent movements. Missing pieces from two fossils may also have been torn away during feeding.

However, weathering, pressure during burial, or damage before collection could have destroyed those pieces. The Xenastrapotherium jaw may show signs of another feeding event.

Ten small, parallel grooves on the other side were probably made later by a smaller animal with serrated teeth. The researchers think Langstonia may have scavenged the remains.

Purussaurus may have waited near freshwater and attacked large mammals as they came to drink, much as large crocodiles hunt today.

Still, no living crocodylian reaches the same size, so modern species offer useful comparisons rather than perfect models.

As large predators, these reptiles may have carried nutrients from land into the water and left parts of their prey for turtles, fish, birds, and other animals to eat.

They may also have affected large herbivore populations, although the fossils do not prove that they controlled those populations.

The evidence comes from only four specimens, and the team found the marks by chance rather than through a systematic search of all La Venta collections.

A systematic survey could show whether crocodylian bites were common and whether different predators left recognizable patterns.

For now, these damaged bones offer the first direct evidence that giant crocodylians fed on some of the largest mammals at La Venta.

Purussaurus was likely an important predator, although some of the marks may have resulted from scavenging.

The study is published in the Journal of Vertebrate Paleontology.

X is moving signed-in users to its new web app one country at a time, but it’s already using the rewrite for X’s logged-out web experience.

X is moving signed-in users to its new web app one country at a time, but it’s already using the rewrite for X’s logged-out web experience.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

X is introducing a new version of its web app that it says is much faster than before, and it’s now rolling out the updated app “one country at a time” for signed-in users.

“Open a profile on X.com over a slow connection,” principal engineer Yagiz Nizipli said in a blog post about the changes. “On the old web app, you’d stare at the X logo for more than 22 seconds before the first post appeared. On the new one, it shows up in 2.7.” The post also goes into technical detail about how the updated app “replaces the three servers we used to run for people, search crawlers, and link previews with one.”

Security researcher Jane Manchun Wong posted that she had access to the updated app, and said it “feels snappy, loads fast and many parts are polished.”

The new app is already available for the logged-out version of X.com and for the pages that are crawled by search engines, Nizipli said.
