Jon Ossoff and Abdul El-Sayed Cinched a Blue Wave With Snug Tailoring

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

On Monday night, the eve of the 2026 midterm elections, the fashion crowd celebrated at the 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards, colloquially referred to as the Oscars of American fashion. Tory Burch was honored with the Positive Change Award, which was presented to her by Anna Wintour, the global editorial director of Vogue and chief content officer of Condé Nast. In her remarks, Wintour made the evening’s only direct reference to the midterms, reminiscing about attending a fundraising event for James Talarico, who successfully flipped Texas in the race for the Senate last night, alongside Burch. After applause from the audience, Wintour, a prolific Democratic fundraiser herself, looked up and addressed the room: “Let’s hope all of Texas feels the way you do,” she said.

The American fashion crowd has largely distanced itself from politics since the 2024 election. During their time in the White House as first ladies, Michelle Obama and Jill Biden were staunch supporters of the American fashion industry, often wearing American designers for public appearances. Melania Trump, in contrast, has more often preferred European labels. It’s an MO that trickles down into the political parties they represent.

There were many headlines that came out of last night’s midterms: Talarico flipped Texas, Annie Andrews flipped South Carolina, New Hampshire’s Chris Pappas became the first out gay man to be elected to the Senate, and Keisha Lance Bottoms of Georgia and Deb Haaland of New Mexico became the first Black and Native American women, respectively, to be elected governor in the history of the US.

And yet it wasn’t just good news (and many great memes) that circulated in my group chats last night; there were also some fashion choices worth noting. First, the most pressing: Where were Steve Kornacki’s khakis? And what was the reasoning behind Haley Stevens’s ludicrously capacious hat? Some questions in life simply go unanswered.

Kornacki, a national political correspondent for NBC News, does not make his political affiliations public. But men in his age range (he is 47) and in the generations that follow—millennials and Gen Z—have been contending with what formalwear means and signals today.

Democratic men have been hard at work on their public image since the 2024 election, largely distancing themselves from the MAGA look and its too-large suits and extra-wide ties. In separating themselves from the manosphere, men like Abdul El-Sayed, who won the Senate race in Michigan, and Jon Ossoff, who was reelected to the Senate in Georgia, have been dressing more like the less buttoned up, progressive men they represent and less like the old establishment politicians on both the right and the left.

On the campaign trail, El-Sayed often sported tees and sweaters rather than button-downs and ties, frequently wearing taut sweaters and henleys under his suit jacket, while Ossoff largely opted for rolled cuffs and no jacket. As the two delivered victory speeches last night, they each wore a tailored suit—a little too tight in the arms; these are men who seem to eat their fair share of protein—and ditched their tie.

It’s a familiar image of a more laid-back look, but one in stark contrast to that of the faces of the right: Donald Trump, JD Vance, et al. Ossoff and El-Sayed, similarly to Kamala Harris during her presidential bid, also leaned into what was happening on the internet. These days, netizens are happy to objectify men and christen a new internet boyfriend every week. Ossoff’s and El-Sayed’s campaigns seemed to understand the power of thirst—that their suits were too tight was most likely not completely intentional, but it did help underscore a simple fact: that they are fit, and that their virility can be both attractive and nonthreatening all at once.

Zohran Mamdani speaking at an event on election night. Mamdani also ditched his tie on Tuesday, in line with some of his fellow Democrats.

It is worth noting, however, that there are some men further to the left, both in politics and around it, who have reembraced the suit: Zohran Mamdani and Hasan Piker. On one hand, this has served a similar purpose as Ossoff’s and El-Sayed’s choices: It has distanced them from looksmaxxers and other conservative-leaning young men. On the other hand, a suit for Mamdani and Piker signifies what suits have always represented: that these are grown men doing important things.

The men are not the full story, however. Bottoms and Haaland came as they are, nodding to the communities they represent. The former wore a bright fuchsia suit and matching blouse, a callback to the lively and monochromatic dressing that is a deeply embedded and celebrated tradition in Black church culture. Haaland’s jewelry—firefly earrings and a beaded necklace—nodded at the style of accessories that is representative of Native style and fashion. If there is something that fashion clearly signifies, it’s community and belonging.

It is a running punch line that the left is more closely connected to fashion—it’s the wine-and-cheese crowd, the elites. (Hillary Clinton, during her bid for the presidency in 2016, was dragged online and by the press for wearing an Armani jacket that reportedly retailed for some $12,000 while delivering a speech that touched on income inequality.) While it’s true that fashion designers are often more enthusiastic about dressing progressives than they are about dressing conservatives, in the era of Trump and the MAGA look, this has become a half-baked story.

Olivia Reingold, a staff writer at The Free Press, made a post on X last night while reporting from an event for the Michigan Democratic Party. “So far at Abdul El-Sayed’s election night event I’ve spotted, Valentino heels, a Chanel bag, a Moncler jacket, Rolex watches, and a YSL bag among other designer items,” Reingold wrote, captioning an image of a woman holding a gold quilted Chanel handbag and a pair of Valentino Rockstud leather pumps.

Read Fit Check: Your front row seat to the power players, peacocks, and deep pockets of fashion—and how they influence the rest of the world.

When one takes a closer look, however, the image has several discrepancies. The style and make of the Chanel Flap bag is inconsistent with others the brand has sold, and the Valentino shoes in question appear askew in proportion; their straps have several punched holes that the authentic Valentino style does not. This could mean that the products in the image are not authentic, but there is another suggestive tell: The bottom strap on the left shoe appears to cut through its connecting strap rather than around, a classic mistake made when artificial intelligence is used to create or alter an image. A run of the image through several AI detectors, including SynthID, a digital watermarking approach created by Google DeepMind that detects media generated by OpenAI, Google, Nvidia, and Kakao, suggests the use of AI. SynthID detected that “this media was made or edited with OpenAI.” (Vanity Fair has reached out to Chanel and Valentino to advise on the authenticity of the products and to Reingold regarding the potential use of AI in the image.)

It’s become too easy to try to make a point about the left’s fashion choices: that Rama Duwaji, wife of Mamdani, should not wear pricey shoes, even if borrowed, because of her political beliefs; or that Democratic women should not carry Valentino or Chanel and vote for a candidate seeking to tax the wealthy. Therein lies the crux of the argument: Does this mean that luxury shoppers should align themselves with MAGA because it’s a party associated with billionaires like Elon Musk and Jeff Bezos and logo-clad celebs like Nicki Minaj, who wore Chanel to the polls in New Hampshire? The answer is simple: no.

Our fashion choices say much about our values, identity, and aspirations. And while politicians have oftentimes utilized fashion to align themselves with specific groups or appear friendly to voters, they, in broad strokes, do not define our political affiliations. A Chanel bag does not make a Democrat, just as a red tie does not make a Republican.

I Was Shocked By The Greatest's Gold Medal Scene, Until I Learned The True Story

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A Lot Of Elizabeth Holmes' Former Co-Workers Have Now Seen You Can See Everything: 'He Fell For It Too'

The Scene In You Can See Everything That Showed EVERYTHING Might NOT Have Been Billy Evans' Idea

A Former Theranos Employee Watched You Can See Everything. What He Saw Made Him Want To Scream At The Screen

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Part of being a legend is also having a few myths.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Warning! The following contains spoilers for The Greatest. Stream the series with a Prime Video subscription, and read at your own risk!

One of the latest sports series to hit the small screen, The Greatest runs through the early years of Cassius Clay's career in boxing and his eventual transformation into the icon we all know as Muhammad Ali. The first episode highlights his time immediately after the Olympics back in Louisville, Kentucky, and how the frustrations between his treatment there and back in Rome led him to launch his gold medal into the Ohio River.

To be quite honest, that scene is one of the most shocking things I've seen on the 2026 TV schedule. I've watched a lot about Muhammad Ali in my life, and I wasn't familiar with this moment. It was wild to think one of the most famous gold medals in United States history was sitting at the bottom of a river, but it turns out the truth behind that story is widely disputed.

It didn't take long for me to discover some information on this situation. Apparently, while the story of Cassius Clay launching his gold medal into the river after being denied service at a diner because of his race is a well-known legend, it is often disputed by historians. Even though Ali famously recalled the story in his 1975 autobiography, some within his inner circle hinted that while the restaurant story happened, Clay had lost the medal in a move a year later.

American television producer Dick Ebersol later wrote in Sports Illustrated that Ali had told him that he "simply lost" the medal at some point. It made for a great dramatic moment in The Greatest, but there's more evidence than not that the event itself simply never happened.

As mentioned, The Greatest is all about the life of Cassius Clay, specifically the early years of his boxing career after his Olympic Gold Medal win in Italy. The story is largely about what happened between those big bouts as well as the issues Clay and his family experienced in their quick rise to fame.

Boxing Trainer Shares How Will Smith ‘Fell To His Knees’ After Particularly Brutal Training For Muhammed Ali

The show -- which is executive-produced by Michael B. Jordan -- also focuses on Clay's friendship with Malcolm X and how he became a major advocate for the treatment of Black Americans after witnessing equality while overseas in Italy. The premiere is a powerful one, showing how Cassius slowly witnesses just how different life is back home in Louisville, and the number of people quickly coming into his life to potentially take advantage of him as much as to help him.

As someone who loves some of the best sports movies as much as the next person, I'm really enjoying The Greatest. While there hasn't been a ton of boxing-oriented action thus far, there's plenty to learn about the fighters' lives, including that of one of Ali's most memorable opponents, Sonny Liston. Sure, some creative liberties are taken, but so far, there's more fact than fiction I'm spotting in this riveting series. You might even say it deserves a gold medal of its own.

Watch The Greatest on Prime Video right now. I've often argued that November is the best time to watch boxing shows and movies, and this series is proving me right!

Weekend stock tokens predict Monday open, but guessing up scores 55%

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bitquery’s pre-futures weekend test found no clear directional edge, while Nasdaq’s proposed weekday expansion remains conditional.

Stock tokens kept trading through weekends, but their prices did not reliably guide the next stock-market open in a new study of 121 stocks.

Blockchain data provider Bitquery's Nov. 3 analysis found that Sunday token moves measured before equity-index futures reopened correctly called 54% of qualifying openings, so simply predicting that every stock would open higher scored 55%.

More hours can give investors another opportunity to transact, but they do not show that the resulting prices predict the next opening better.

Bitquery followed 17 weekends beginning July 10 through Oct. 30, with the final stock opening on Nov. 2. It recorded 791 stock-opening observations across those stocks, and its direction test kept the 533 openings that moved at least 0.5% from Friday's close.

Token moves pointed the right way on 286 of those observations, compared with 291 correct calls for always predicting a rise. Because more openings rose than fell, a 50% coin-toss benchmark alone would flatter the tokens' result.

The study also used balanced accuracy, which averages success on rises and falls. The tokens scored 53%, with a reported 95% interval of 44% to 62%.

Across all 791 observations, including smaller moves, the median error was 1.2% using token moves versus 0.9% assuming the stock opened at Friday's close. Bitquery put the excess error at 0.3%, with a 95% range of 0.2% to 0.5%.

A weekend price is not yet a forecasting edge

Bitquery's study asks different questions about direction and forecast size. The later Sunday reading also changes the information and the sample.

Trading access and useful forecasting information remain separate claims.

Vendor research, not independent raw-data replication. No futures-price benchmark or cost-adjusted strategy. The later cutoff, volume floor and movement filter change together.

Method: Reported study figures only. Main test: 17 weekends beginning July 10–October 30, openings through November 2. Direction filters stock moves below 0.5%; size uses all 791 observations. Panel intervals are reported 95% ranges. Point and interval geometry maps linearly onto the same 40%–70% balanced-accuracy scale; this is an interval plot, not a zero-based bar chart.

The main test read token prices just before 6 p.m. Sunday ET, the futures reopening cutoff used by Bitquery. That timing is important because a later token price could incorporate information already available from equity-index futures.A separate check using prices just before 8 p.m. returned 59% balanced accuracy, with a 95% range of 53% to 65%, across 220 openings. Different instruments, liquidity filters and observation times prevent that result from serving as a direct rebuttal to this test. The main test read token prices just before 6 p.m. Sunday ET, the futures reopening cutoff used by Bitquery. That timing is important because a later token price could incorporate information already available from equity-index futures.

A separate check using prices just before 8 p.m. returned 59% balanced accuracy, with a 95% range of 53% to 65%, across 220 openings. But it also raised the weekend-volume threshold to $1 million and changed the movement filter.

Listings needed at least $10,000 of weekend trading and a trade in the hour before each cutoff. The study excluded openings affected by stock splits or ex-dividend dates and crypto-sensitive stocks such as Strategy and Coinbase, then combined issuers using weekend-volume weights.

Earlier perpetual-market research reported improved forecasts from final weekend prices in a different sample. Different instruments, liquidity filters and observation times prevent that result from serving as a direct rebuttal to this test.

Nasdaq's Oct. 30 update described Dec. 6 as the expected transition to 23-hour, five-day trading, subject to clearing and market-data infrastructure being ready. Its related market-data proposals also remain subject to regulatory approval.

Its member implementation notice describes an added 9 p.m. to 4 a.m. ET session requiring new member ports.

If the rollout proceeds, wider conventional weekday hours would narrow one stock-token advantage for investors whose brokers and jurisdictions permit both routes. Weekend trading and the ability to hold tokens in a blockchain wallet remain separate benefits to assess.

For example, Kraken's xStocks disclosure excludes the US, Canada, UK and Australia, permits compatible-wallet withdrawals and says holders lack ownership and voting rights in the underlying shares.

The next useful evidence would hold timing and eligibility constant, compare against available market benchmarks, and measure executable results after costs. Until then, an available weekend price should not be mistaken for a demonstrated forecasting advantage.

Theories that Bethesda is hinting at an earlier launch are already surfacing, and a few of them are pretty good.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Yesterday, after eight years of waiting, Bethesda finally gave The Elder Scrolls 6 a title: Sentinel. It then warned that, after eight years of waiting, more waiting is in store because "it'll be some time before we're ready to share more." Then Bloomberg's Jason Schreier, one of the industry's best-known and most reliable reporters, weighed in, saying the game is "still more than two years away."

Schreier's 'two more years' statement, echoed by Jez Corden of Windows Central, attracted quite a range of comments, not all of them of the polite sort. That led Schreier to Reddit, where he could elaborate on his statement.

Pointing out his record of accurate calls on previous Bethesda releases Fallout 76 and Starfield, Schreier wrote, "I'm a reporter. I am not telling you this because I enjoy watching people get mad about having to wait for sequels to their favorite video games. I am telling you this because it is true, and because my job is to report accurate facts and clear up misinformation. TES6: Sentinel is more than two years away."

Did this clarification calm the roiling waters? Well...

Many of the replies are understanding, grateful, and sympathetic to Schreier's plight, but there are plenty that express doubt or distrust, suggesting that the two year window was pure speculation. Others want to know why Bethesda would drop the title announcement yesterday if the game really is that far out, given that Todd Howard has previously expressed regret about revealing The Elder Scrolls 6 so prematurely in the first place. Why repeat that mistake?

A few optimistic souls are holding onto the dream of a 2027 release that emerged from the reveal trailer for Skyrim on the Nintendo Switch 2:

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

And then there are the Elder Scrolls fans operating on a whole different level from the rest of us. Redditor Reacryoum, for instance, thinks Bethesda is leading up to a surprise trailer drop on November 11—that's actually one of the more grounded theories to emerge over the past 24 hours. Casting back to one of Bethesda's old Twitter posts, Jayehemcee speculated that the theme of the game is "time," and that this is all part of the game's marketing. I'm not sure how that one works in practice, frankly, but I respect the ambition.

Minimum-Act-7571 put forth the idea that Bethesda is actually messing with Schreier by intentionally feeding him false information, because it's super mad at him spilling the beans all the time—although they also admitted that it's a "new copium theory," and so perhaps not meant to be taken entirely seriously.

I think the best of the bunch, though, is this one from Any-West7160, who believes the Sentinel title reveal contains the actual release date: November 11, 2027. I don't believe it, but I like it.

Here's the video in question—what do you see in the stars?

The best videogame conspiracy theories are always the most bonkers, but what complicates the situation just a wee little bit for Sentinel is that one such theory was actually correct: The Sentinel subtitle was stashed away in a Starfield easter egg for years before anyone noticed. Maybe it was just a coincidence, sure, but the numbers don't lie, as the saying goes—and those numbers said Sentinel. And they were right!

So there's precedent there, pretty certainly, but it's also a lot easier to drop in a reference to a locked-in-stone game title than it is to do the same with a relatively far-off launch date that's subject to disruption and delays. And as others have pointed out, including Schreier, it's extremely unlikely that anyone is just lying about the far-off release window for a little bit of PR pop: Evasion and word games are one thing, but straight-up dishonesty will leave your credibility in shreds pretty much instantly.

What it all adds up to, ultimately, is this: Two more years of waiting and maybe more, plenty of hopium and copium along the way, and a wise piece of advice from Vanquisher_84 which will almost certainly go unheeded:

A couple walking their dog spotted a rusty can and found 1,427 buried gold coins

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A California couple discovered 1,427 gold coins buried in eight rusty cans on their property in 2013, uncovering a treasure worth over $10 million.

In 2013, a Northern California couple known only as John and Mary were walking their dog on their property when they noticed a rusty can poking out of the ground. It was the first of eight cans that together held 1,427 gold coins.

The coins were struck between 1847 and 1894 and had a face value of $27,980. Kagin’s, Inc., the coin firm that marketed the find, estimated the coin hoard‘s value at more than $10 million.

“We found the coins buried on a little area of our property that we call the Saddle Ridge,” Mary said in an interview with the couple. The treasure became known as the Saddle Ridge Hoard.

“I saw an old can sticking out of the ground on a trail that we had walked almost every day for many, many years,” John recalled.

“John used a stick to dig up the first can,” Mary explained.

Carrying the can toward the house, the couple stopped for a breather because it was so heavy. John joked that it must be full of lead paint.

“Right after making the comment about it possibly being paint, the lid cracked off and exposed a rib of a single gold coin,” he recounted.

“Of course, it was a very surreal moment,” John noted of his first reaction.

Back at the trail, about a foot (30 centimeters) to the left of the first can, the couple broke into a second one with a small hand shovel.

In all, the coins lay in eight decaying metal canisters, all less than a foot (30 centimeters) beneath the surface.

The cans were numbered and had been placed in a curious alignment with the North Star, the American Numismatic Association reported.

“Years ago, on our first hike, we noticed an old tree growing into the hill,” John recalled in the association’s account. An unusual angular rock also sat up the hill from where the coins were buried.

“Starting at the rock, if you walk 10 paces towards the North Star, you wind up smack in the middle of the coins!” Mary pointed out.

For safekeeping at first, John hid the coins at home. “I dug a hole under the wood pile and got a slab of green board to cover it,” he remembered.

Most of the gold coins were $20 Liberty double eagles struck at the San Francisco Mint from 1855 to 1894, according to the Professional Coin Grading Service (PCGS), which certified each coin.

Kagin’s counted almost 1,400 $20 gold pieces, 50 $10 gold pieces and four $5 gold pieces in the hoard.

The $20 coin dates to the California Gold Rush. An 1849 example was the first $20 coin struck by the U.S. Mint during that rush, according to the Smithsonian Institution.

David Hall, co-founder of PCGS, called the hoard “a literal time capsule” of coins that circulated in the late 19th century. In 2014, more than a dozen of its coins ranked as the finest known, or tied for finest known, in the PCGS Population Report.

One of them, an 1866-S “No Motto” double eagle, was valued at around $1 million.

David McCarthy, senior numismatist at Kagin’s, evaluated the coins after the couple contacted him. “Many pieces were finer than anything known in major collections or museums,” he observed.

Based on the coins’ dates and condition, along with the decay of the cans, Kagin’s concluded that the hoard was buried over a significant period of time in the late 19th century.

“Unlike other hoards and treasures, this one includes a great variety of dates, many of which are in pristine condition,” noted Donald Kagin, president of Kagin’s.

Who did the burying remains unknown. One idea links the coins to a robbery of the San Francisco Mint in 1901, but the wide range of coin conditions has put that theory to rest.

Other suggestions include a secret society or a landowner hiding gold around the time of Franklin D. Roosevelt’s gold confiscation order. Neither idea has been confirmed.

About 90 percent of the coins were to be sold through Amazon’s collectibles store, in what Kagin’s called the first major numismatic treasure sold there. Others were sold through Kagin’s own platform.

Before the sale, a sample of the coins went on display at the American Numismatic Association’s National Money Show in Atlanta, from February 27 to March 1, 2014.

With the proceeds, John and Mary paid off personal debt and donated to local charities.

“We love our lives as they are – I hope we can help our family members and our community and give back some,” Mary told the interviewer.

Before Saddle Ridge, the largest reported buried gold find in the U.S. had a face value of $4,500. Construction workers found it in Jackson, Tennessee, in 1985, and it later sold for around $1 million.

The information was obtained from the American Numismatic Association.

A $6.52 Million Beverlywood Home on a Lavish Golf Course Just Set the Record for the Priciest Teardown Sale

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A Los Angeles property in Beverlywood has been sold in an off-market transaction, and at $6.52 million, it has set the record for the most expensive home to be purchased with plans to be torn down in the upscale neighborhood.

The dwelling is certainly in a coveted location, perched directly on the Hillcrest Country Club—a private club with an 18-hole golf course that has attracted celebrity members for more than a century.

According to Town and Country Magazine, the club’s initiation fee is a reported $185,000. Comedian Adam Sandler, singer-songwriter Neil Diamond, and Paramount co-founder Adolph Zukor are among the Hillcrest’s most notable members.

The home currently standing at 1807 Roxbury Drive is a three-bedroom, 3.5-bathroom abode spanning 2,874 square feet. It was built in 1953.

According to Micah Hiller of Prime Rock Realty, who represented the buyer of 1807 Roxbury Drive, the new residents are looking to build a new house with a luxury construction company called Drexel Luxury Homes. They’d like a home with a much larger footprint.

The lot itself is 8,660 square feet, and Hiller estimates the buyers will be able to build a home that’s “at least” 4,500 square feet without a basement. Should they decide to add a lower ground floor, they’d likely be able to add an additional 2,000 or 3,000 square feet.

“This is the highest land-value sale in Beverlywood’s history and the neighborhood’s second-highest residential sale overall,” Hiller tells Realtor.com®. “The only higher seller was a recently built, three-story home on Duxbury Circle that sold for $8 million.”

Hiller explains that Beverlywood has become a highly sought-after enclave, which, “despite the current market, continues to show incredible strength.”

“From what I’m seeing, demand for the neighborhood has only grown, and in some ways, it has become even more desirable than Beverly Hills,” he continues. “Buyers appreciate the community, location, and opportunity to create a home here.”

Beverlywood has seen some year-over-year softness in the housing market with the median listing price, price per square foot, and median days on the market all showing decreases from this time last year, according to Realtor.com data.

Still, each of these metrics is outperforming nationwide stats. In Beverlywood, the average listing is just under $2.4 million, while that number is just under $420,000 for the country as a whole.

Homes here also spend less than half the time on the market than they do nationwide, with Beverlywood listings being snapped up in an average of 29 days. The U.S. median is 61.

For the Los Angeles neighborhood, this number is down 21.62% year over year, signaling faster turnover and a more competitive environment for sellers.

As of October 2026, Beverlywood had an impressive sale-to-list price ratio of 99%, indicating a strong seller’s market for this desirable spot. There are also fewer opportunities for a buyer to find exactly what they’re looking for, as there are only a dozen active listings for sale.

Beverlywood is not the only corner of the country with a competitive market setting records.

In Provincetown, MA—where the price per square foot is six times the national median—the record for most-expensive condo sold was broken twice in a two-week period.

Thomas Andrews of Gibson Sotheby’s International Realty, who was the listing agent on both pricey transactions, said the vibrant community is the reason the local market is so coveted—which is quite similar to what Hiller says of Beverlywood.

“Provincetown is such a magical place. You have your Martha’s Vineyard, you have your Nantucket, but there’s so much soul in the creative arts in Provincetown that just makes it super vibrant and super sought-after,” he said.

Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones explains that the town has effectively reached its limit for new housing, which creates a tense battle for anyone who wants to find a home there.

"Provincetown sits at the very tip of Cape Cod, which means there is essentially no more space to build," she explains. "The housing stock is therefore relatively fixed, and any new supply has to come off from redevelopment or small-scale infill, both of which tend to be expensive and difficult to pull off."

Hannah Chambers is a senior celebrity and lifestyle reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2026. She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

Whenever I see one of Matt Damon’s daughters in an interview with him, I just wait for them to roast him about something. He shares four daughters (Alexia, Isabella, Gia, and Stella) with his wife, Luciana Barroso, and they never fail to tell their dad EXACTLY what they think about his work, his clothes, and even the way he stands. So naturally, when he took his daughter, Isabella, to the 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards, she wouldn’t be able to resist roasting her dad’s style (and I can’t get over his reaction).

The actor has often shared stories about his daughters’ opinions, so he should know what to expect by now. We already know they have no problem telling their dad the honest truth, even calling out his ‘bad’ movies. So, I knew what Isabella was going to say when an Elle reporter asked for her opinion on her dad’s style. Sure enough, she was quick to throw her dad to the fashion police by assuring she has to veto his outfit choices “every day” and credits a house full of women for giving him a helping hand. Check out the funny moment:

Now, we can’t discount her sisters when it comes to keeping Damon on his toes. Her sister, Gia, has critiqued not just Damon but his BFF Ben Affleck for their red carpet poses when the duo was promoting their movie The Rip. In this case, given the way he laughs (and instantly agrees), he knew what was coming when a reporter asked his daughter if she has vetoed any of his looks. It’s sweet to see how quickly Damon agrees with her, claiming that without her (plus, her mom and sisters) he’s “pretty hopeless.” (Though I wonder if his fashion sense is something that annoys Affleck, since we already know what annoys Damon.)

However, the most surprising part of the interview was that Odysseus received the best dressed award out of Damon’s characters. (Perhaps because it’s the most recent movie Damon has done, having hit the 2026 movie schedule over the summer?) The shout-out is ironic given the cold and wet conditions the actor often found himself in during his ‘odyssey.’ While he doesn’t disagree that his epic hero looks his best (and the costuming is fantastic), he does have a different opinion on which of his characters has the best style:

Though Tom Ripley has pretty great style. After -- once he met Dickie Greenleaf. Once Dickie came into his life, he had good style.

Tom Ripley is the titular con artist from The Talented Mr. Ripley (1999), and it is set in the 1950s. Now, I don’t knock the fun costuming in the movie, but it wasn’t the character I expected. I would have thought Jason Bourne would at least get a shout-out for his attire. After all, it is one of the few roles Damon has done multiple times. Maybe it’s the ‘50s aesthetic that won Damon’s favor.

Anyway, getting back to the actor's personal style, he did look very nice at this event...and I bet his daughters and wife had something to do with that. Now, as award season kicks into high gear, I'll be curious to hear about any fashion vetoes they throw their dad's way.

APOD: 2026 November 5 – Enceladus in Silhouette

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: One of our Solar System's most tantalizing worlds, Enceladus is backlit by the Sun in this Cassini spacecraft image from November 1, 2009. The dramatic illumination reveals the plumes that continuously spew into space from the south pole of Saturn's 500 kilometer diameter moon. Discovered by Cassini in 2005, the icy plumes are likely connected to an ocean beneath the ice shell of Enceladus. They supply material directly to Saturn's outer, tenuous E ring and make the surface of Enceladus as reflective as snow. Across the scene, Saturn's icy rings scatter sunlight toward Cassini's cameras. Beyond the rings (lower right), the night side of 80 kilometer diameter moon Pandora is faintly lit by Saturnlight.Tomorrow's picture: pixels in space

Wildlife experts rescued over 90 venomous primates that were supposed to be extinct for nearly a century

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The critically endangered Bangka slow loris has been rediscovered in Indonesia after decades without scientific records, but its habitat remains at risk.

The Bangka slow loris, a small venomous primate that had not appeared in the scientific literature since the 1930s, still lives on Bangka Island in Indonesia. The species is listed as Critically Endangered, and possibly extinct, on the IUCN Red List.

Between 2015 and 2025, the Alobi Wildlife Rescue Center on Bangka rescued more than 90 of the animals. It released 86 of them back into forests on the island.

Staff at the center published those records in October 2026, in a study with partners from zoos in Austria and Indonesia. The conservation group Re:wild announced the findings.

The Bangka slow loris, Nycticebus bancanus, was described in 1906 from Kelabat Bay on Bangka. For decades it was treated as a subspecies of other slow lorises, until a 2013 study raised it to a species of its own.

The IUCN Red List gives 1937 as the year the loris was last recorded, a date the authors trace to a 1937 publication describing three adult males. Museum material suggests those specimens may date from 1930 to 1932, so the last record could be older still.

Re:wild lists the Bangka slow loris among the most wanted primates in its search for lost species.

Benji Barca, the exploration program manager at Re:wild, credited the rescue center’s work.

“The team at Alobi has spent more than a decade quietly rescuing, healing and releasing these animals, and their careful records are what have finally now brought this primate back into scientific view,” Barca said.

“A species can disappear from the scientific record without disappearing from the lives of the people who share its home,” he noted.

The center kept detailed records for 43 lorises rescued from 2021 to August 2025. Sixteen were found in houses or gardens and six on roads, and at least three of the 43 died.

Seven had injuries suspected to come from electric shocks, and five came from captivity or the wildlife trade. Three turned up on plantations, one was caught in a fence and one was attacked by a dog.

Alobi rescued Bangka slow lorises in all four regencies of Bangka Island and in the city of Pangkal Pinang. That spread suggests the primate may still live across much of Bangka.

Timon Glaw of the Haus des Meeres Aqua Terra Zoo in Vienna, Austria, is the study’s corresponding author.

“We now know the species has survived in several parts of Bangka, and we urgently need a long-term conservation strategy built on population surveys, genetic studies and, above all, protection of its remaining habitat,” Glaw said.

“The next challenge is to make sure it does not actually become extinct,” he added.

The 86 released animals went to seven sites. Bukit Mangkol forest park received 37, Permisan nature park received 22 and the Mount Menumbing area received 15.

The four other sites took six, three, two and one animal. They include Gunung Maras National Park and the BIO protected forest.

How many of the released lorises survived over the long term is not yet known, because no follow-up data exist.

The authors call for future releases of rescued slow lorises to be monitored, as part of an in situ conservation program that protects suitable habitat.

Slow lorises are the only primates known to be venomous. They lick a gland on the inside of the upper arm and mix its secretion with saliva, producing a bite that can be painful and cause swelling.

The venom’s main role in nature seems to be defense against parasites and against other lorises, a 2013 review of slow loris venom concluded.

In extreme cases, a bite can send a person into anaphylactic shock, which has sometimes proved fatal.

In a preliminary test, leeches placed in contact with a mix of the gland oil and saliva all died.

Langka Sani, who leads the Alobi Wildlife Rescue Center and co-authored the study, points to habitat loss as the main danger.

“We are glad to contribute to the scientific record to show that the Bangka slow loris is not lost, but the species is still very close to extinction,” Sani explained.

“Its natural habitat on Bangka Island has shrunk significantly because of illegal tin mining and massive land clearing for oil palm plantations.”

Forest habitat on Bangka fell by 62 percent between 2000 and 2012, according to a 2017 analysis by Supriatna and colleagues cited in the paper.

Settlements, roads, power lines and the pet trade also threaten the loris, Re:wild noted. Five of the rescued animals had been kept in captivity or caught up in the wildlife trade, even though slow lorises are protected by law.

The authors also recommend a conservation breeding program, molecular studies to confirm its identity and targeted surveys on Bangka, Belitung and Borneo. They ask the IUCN to reassess the loris, especially its “Possibly Extinct” tag.

The study was published in the journal Spixiana.

Priceline Faces $1 Billion Fine After Hotel Guests Were Tricked Into Paying Up To 85% More For Rooms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

You search for a hotel, click what looks like its reservation page, and book a room—only to discover you’ve gone through a middleman charging hundreds of dollars more per night. That’s the deception alleged against Guest Reservations, a Priceline partner whose advertised rate could be up to 85% higher than booking directly. Now Priceline’s parent has recorded a $1 billion accounting charge toward a possible Federal Trade Commission settlement involving the partner’s practices, with the potential penalties and consumer refunds still being negotiated. A refundable room at San Francisco’s Palace Hotel was $419 on Marriott’s website. Guest Reservations wanted $775 for a comparable room with the same cancellation deadline. And the city’s lawsuit says the booking site was designed to make travelers think they were dealing with the hotel directly. The company supplying the rooms was Priceline, and its parent Booking Holdings which also owns Booking.com, has taken a $1 billion accounting hit while negotiating a possible regulatory settlement for its involvement in intercept guests who had already chosen a hotel, making them think they’d reached the hotel’s own site, and charging them more – and making it difficult to undo the purchase when they discover what happened. San Francisco’s suit includes a Google search for “palace hotel sf” on an iPhone. The top sponsored result displayed palacesanfrancisco.guestreservations.com, above Marriott’s own hotel website. Clicking led to a page devoted to the Palace Hotel. The city says the small “An independent travel network” on the site was easy to miss and ambiguous. “Guest reservations” sounds like a department of the hotel you’re trying to book. Put the property’s name in front of it, add hotel photos, and a traveler can think they’ve arrived at the hotel’s guest reservations department. The city compared prices within minutes of each other on July 1 for a July 8 stay. These were Marriott’s public nonmember rates, including taxes: King Room Cancellation Terms Marriott Direct Guest Reservations Markup Refundable for one day after booking $381 $595 56% Refundable until July 5 $419 $775 85% A separate investigation found a two-night stay at the Westin Denver Airport cost $237 more through Guest Reservations than booking the same stay directly. Guest Reservations said it clearly identifies itself as an independent platform, complies with Google’s advertising rules, and discloses its taxes and service fees before purchase. Customers also describe trouble getting refunds after discovering the intermediary. And booking through a third party means giving up hotel points and elite benefits, along with the ability to deal directly with the property when plans change. Guest Reservations isn’t owned or operated by Priceline. Priceline Partner Solutions was reportedly its exclusive hotel inventory supplier. Their operation furnished the hotel information, booking technology and payment processing, and earned revenue from completed reservations. The city’s case also targets BookOnline.com. It alleges Booking knew how the sites were acquiring customers and continued helping them sell rooms. Booking responded that it takes consumer protection seriously and believes the lawsuit lacks merit. One example of Priceline’s involvement comes from Seth Kugel’s New York Times investigation. An 85-year-old woman searching for Bologna’s Grand Hotel Majestic reached Guest Reservations and requested an eight night standard room stay. She was charged $16,559 for a suite, including $4,276 in taxes and fees. When she disputed the charge with American Express, Priceline Partner Solutions was the merchant of record and successfully sought to have the charge reinstated. Guest Reservations later told the paper it had issued her a refund. Priceline told the Times that fewer than 0.09% of Guest Reservations transactions generated complaints, that partners must comply with laws and contractual obligations, and that it takes action when it identifies violations. A low complaint rate doesn’t tell us how many people were misled. Some never discover it. Others blame the hotel. Many may think they have no recourse. Most consumers do not complain when they’re unhappy (or complain to friends and family). The odds that a complaint reaches Priceline as supplier of the inventory seem low. And they’re saying they’re getting complaints… not what they did with those! In its latest quarterly filing, Booking recorded: A $900 million reduction in revenue. A separate $100 million loss in general and administrative expenses. Booking says the FTC could seek even more. A negotiated agreement would likely include payments to the agency, money back to U.S. consumers, and changes to Priceline’s practices. There are also civil penalties for qualifying rule violations, with a current ceiling of $53,088 per violation. The government has a straightforward argument about the alleged sales process: a reasonable customer gets misled by the appearance of an ad and booking page, while the booking site says guests should have read the disclaimer more carefully. The site apparently was literally designed to create the wrong impression among customers. The FTC’s business impersonation rule covers falsely posing as another company or misrepresenting an affiliation, including by implication. The government likely needs to show that Priceline knew about the deception and participated in it or had authority to control it. But there are several defenses: Charging more isn’t illegal. Travel agencies can charge service fees, and customers can buy expensive rooms. Guest Reservations says it discloses its role and charges. The actual booking screens and phone calls have to establish deception. It doesn’t appear that Guest Reservations websites offer any value add of any kind. The question is going to be whether they were in fact actively trying to generate bookings through deception. Supplying rooms alone doesn’t make Priceline responsible for every advertisement of them. It can argue that it provided ordinary distribution services and didn’t design or control the misleading sales pitch. The FTC declined to adopt a broad provision making suppliers liable for furnishing the tools of impersonation. So the FTC needs to demonstrate Priceline’s complicity. The FTC has to establish its authority to collect the money. The Supreme Court limited the agency’s general route to obtaining refunds in 2021. The amount of harm is unclear. Travelers could have gotten their rooms for less but generally did receive the stay they booked, at a price they were willing to pay. I wouldn’t make every wholesaler liable whenever a reseller behaves badly. It’s not yet clear exactly what Priceline knew and when they knew it. But I infer from taking a billion dollar accounting charge that the evidence against them is significant. Online travel agencies can earn their commissions by helping customers find the hotel they want, comparing options and searching for better prices (despite rate parity rules). I’ve written about the financial incentive to make comparison shopping harder. Here, it seems the traveler had already chosen the hotel, was looking for the hotel site to go ahead and book it, and the middlemen got paid by confusing them into thinking that’s what they were doing. The company supplying the rooms was Priceline, and its parent Booking Holdings which also owns Booking.com, has taken a $1 billion accounting hit while negotiating a possible regulatory settlement for its involvement in intercept guests who had already chosen a hotel, making them think they’d reached the hotel’s own site, and charging them more – and making it difficult to undo the purchase when they discover what happened.

Clicking led to a page devoted to the Palace Hotel. The city says the small “An independent travel network” on the site was easy to miss and ambiguous.

“Guest reservations” sounds like a department of the hotel you’re trying to book. Put the property’s name in front of it, add hotel photos, and a traveler can think they’ve arrived at the hotel’s guest reservations department. The city compared prices within minutes of each other on July 1 for a July 8 stay. These were Marriott’s public nonmember rates, including taxes:

A separate investigation found a two-night stay at the Westin Denver Airport cost $237 more through Guest Reservations than booking the same stay directly. Guest Reservations said it clearly identifies itself as an independent platform, complies with Google’s advertising rules, and discloses its taxes and service fees before purchase.

Customers also describe trouble getting refunds after discovering the intermediary. And booking through a third party means giving up hotel points and elite benefits, along with the ability to deal directly with the property when plans change.

Guest Reservations isn’t owned or operated by Priceline. Priceline Partner Solutions was reportedly its exclusive hotel inventory supplier. Their operation furnished the hotel information, booking technology and payment processing, and earned revenue from completed reservations.

The city’s case also targets BookOnline.com. It alleges Booking knew how the sites were acquiring customers and continued helping them sell rooms. Booking responded that it takes consumer protection seriously and believes the lawsuit lacks merit.

One example of Priceline’s involvement comes from Seth Kugel’s New York Times investigation. An 85-year-old woman searching for Bologna’s Grand Hotel Majestic reached Guest Reservations and requested an eight night standard room stay. She was charged $16,559 for a suite, including $4,276 in taxes and fees.

When she disputed the charge with American Express, Priceline Partner Solutions was the merchant of record and successfully sought to have the charge reinstated. Guest Reservations later told the paper it had issued her a refund.

Priceline told the Times that fewer than 0.09% of Guest Reservations transactions generated complaints, that partners must comply with laws and contractual obligations, and that it takes action when it identifies violations.

A low complaint rate doesn’t tell us how many people were misled. Some never discover it. Others blame the hotel. Many may think they have no recourse. Most consumers do not complain when they’re unhappy (or complain to friends and family). The odds that a complaint reaches Priceline as supplier of the inventory seem low. And they’re saying they’re getting complaints… not what they did with those!

In its latest quarterly filing, Booking recorded:

Booking says the FTC could seek even more. A negotiated agreement would likely include payments to the agency, money back to U.S. consumers, and changes to Priceline’s practices. There are also civil penalties for qualifying rule violations, with a current ceiling of $53,088 per violation.

The government has a straightforward argument about the alleged sales process: a reasonable customer gets misled by the appearance of an ad and booking page, while the booking site says guests should have read the disclaimer more carefully. The site apparently was literally designed to create the wrong impression among customers.

The FTC’s business impersonation rule covers falsely posing as another company or misrepresenting an affiliation, including by implication. The government likely needs to show that Priceline knew about the deception and participated in it or had authority to control it. But there are several defenses:

Supplying rooms alone doesn’t make Priceline responsible for every advertisement of them. It can argue that it provided ordinary distribution services and didn’t design or control the misleading sales pitch. The FTC declined to adopt a broad provision making suppliers liable for furnishing the tools of impersonation. So the FTC needs to demonstrate Priceline’s complicity.

The FTC has to establish its authority to collect the money. The Supreme Court limited the agency’s general route to obtaining refunds in 2021.

The amount of harm is unclear. Travelers could have gotten their rooms for less but generally did receive the stay they booked, at a price they were willing to pay.

I wouldn’t make every wholesaler liable whenever a reseller behaves badly. It’s not yet clear exactly what Priceline knew and when they knew it. But I infer from taking a billion dollar accounting charge that the evidence against them is significant.

Online travel agencies can earn their commissions by helping customers find the hotel they want, comparing options and searching for better prices (despite rate parity rules). I’ve written about the financial incentive to make comparison shopping harder. Here, it seems the traveler had already chosen the hotel, was looking for the hotel site to go ahead and book it, and the middlemen got paid by confusing them into thinking that’s what they were doing.

The Remarkable Election Night Story We Should All Be Talking About

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Don’t let the sound of the blue tsunami drown out what happened in Wisconsin.

Drowned out by the sound of the blue tsunami making landfall everywhere, a remarkable story took place in Wisconsin. The state's Democratic party, almost gerrymandered out of existence a few years ago and helpless, succeeded not only in electing a governor, but also majorities in both houses of the state legislature.

The Democrats already enjoy a majority on the state supreme court, so I feel confident in saying that the state is now completely free of the dead hand of the administration of Scott Walker, the goggle-eyed homunculus hired by Koch Industries to manage their subsidiary once known as the state of Wisconsin. They also flipped two congressional seats, one of them Paul Ryan's old seat now held by one of his old aides, and that of the inexcusable Derrick Van Orden, famous corrector of lounging congressional pages.

(What is truly delicious is that Van Orden was dimed out on that rampage by his fellow Wisconsin congressman Mark Pocan, who was reelected Tuesday night, though he ran unopposed.)

Wisconsin's new governor made a visit to the shebeen a few years back when he was Milwaukee county executive, which is a position of some importance in state politics. (It also served as Walker's springboard.) His pathway to the governor's office was, shall we say, eccentric. From the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel:

But his campaign for governor was something of a rollercoaster.

(Hong, it should be noted, turned out to be the anti-Haley Stevens, campaigning vigorously for Crowley throughout the general election campaign. Good on her.)

It was political activist Ben Wikler who turned the shebeen on to David Crowley, and Wikler is going to go down in Wisconsin political history as the guy who rebuilt the state's Democratic Party almost from scratch. And he did so in keeping with the state's proud progressive history and with the state's one-word motto: Forward.

Building AI factories at enterprise scale

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

SPONSORED FEATURE: ASUS wants to scale AI everywhere in the enterprise

AI has become a juggernaut, driving technical strategy in the tech sector and outside of it. With the worldwide server market growing 30 percent in a single year to hit $122.6bn in the first quarter of 2025, IDC now feels safe recategorizing AI-driven investment "from cyclical to durable".

The analyst company expects inference to account for over 70 percent of computing demand by next year, adding that it's growing fastest at the edge.

All this investment in AI infrastructure might once have come mainly for hyperscalers, but that's changing quickly. Adoption is spreading quickly beyond public cloud service providers into enterprise environments, IDC adds. They're eager to run agentic AI in-house.

There are plenty of reasons for companies to own their own AI infrastructure rather than offloading it to the cloud. One of these is data control, especially in regulated sectors such as finance, healthcare, manufacturing, and the public sector that have sovereign data requirements. Another revolves around the need for predictable costing from tokens delivered on tunable in-house systems.

So what should companies look for in a supplier? Delivery speed will be a powerful driver for many who are under the gun to deliver AI initiatives for the business, especially during periods of constrained memory and CPU supply. Life cycle services will also come near the top of the list, for companies that need help managing everything from the power through to the reasoning LLM models and the workloads that use them. That need for expertise is unlikely to stop; companies will undergo changes in AI strategy as they get to grips with a still-evolving technology.

ASUS expects this AI surge to continue in the datacenter, but elsewhere too, specifically in edge locations (especially industrial use cases) and at developers' desks, as the applications that will power the enterprise get made. It wants to be top of peoples' list for hardware and services at all these places along the AI continuum.

At NVIDIA's GTC show in Berlin on Oct 21-22, it announced a full-stack AI offering that it's calling its Enterprise Agentic AI Solution. It's  three-layer affair, featuring:

ASUS AI Server and Infrastructure: Its selection of AI servers, including several new units.

ASUS AI Software Stack: Its software for integrating and controlling AI workflows.

ASUS AI HUB: A services operation that ties it all together.

These bring together some existing assets that ASUS had for enterprise AI users, along with some new ones. The time is right for an end-to-end offering with new hardware because of the way the market is shifting, say ASUS execs.

"ASUS servers help enterprises build high-performance agentic AI infrastructure capable of supporting both complex task execution and large-scale AI agent deployment, accelerating enterprise adoption of agentic AI," says Paul Ju, senior vice president and head of infrastructure solution BG at ASUS.

While generative AI initially spiked demand, the advent of agentic AI has sent compute demand into overdrive. Ju explains that the focus is expanding from the GPU.

"Historically, AI servers have been optimized primarily around GPU compute. But as enterprise AI shifts toward agentic AI, workloads increasingly revolve around a continuous ‘agent loop’ that is heavily CPU-dependent," he says. That loop takes in task planning, code execution, external tool invocation, and reflection or correction work.

The CPU's increased role in this execution work is creating bottlenecks in conventional AI servers, he adds.

The first such sticking point is single-thread performance, which leaves GPUs idling while they wait for CPUs to complete. The second lies in memory bandwidth. When this is managed on a per-core basis, it can slow things down when many agents run at once.

The multi-die design of many CPUs also penalizes them in AI environments, because accessing data across dies introduces latency, Ju argues. CPUs with cores and cache on different dies suffer latency when data has to cross between them. Maintaining cache coherency exacerbates that problem.

Finally, there's a security risk: a lack of hardware level sandbox isolation on some CPUs make it possible for prompt injection to reach core systems, he warns.

Customers also bring their own challenges as they grapple with different applications for AI. Enterprises evaluating AI server options have to match their server platforms to the projected AI workload profiles, Ju adds. Training a supervised learning model to batch-classify insurance claims takes different technical resources to running a low-latency reasoning employee chatbot.

ASUS wants to help customers solve problems like these wherever they come up. With this in mind, the company had plenty of goodies for onlookers at GTC.

Starting at the datacenter, the company announced both single and full-rack systems built around the NVIDIA HGX Rubin NVL8 eight-GPU server board. It has a 3U hybrid-cooled system, the XA NR11-E12L, along with the 2U 100 percent liquid-cooled XA NR11-E12, both targeting large AI reasoning workloads.

The two Rubin units target different use cases. The 3U hybrid liquid cooling system uses direct-to-chip liquid cooling on the 8GPU baseboard, along with air-cooled dual Xeon 6 chips on a sled. The company pegs this as the easy transition path into AI factory hardware. The 2U unit, with its 100 percent liquid cooling, is tailored for maximum PUE and compute density. It also uses a cable-free board-to-board design to keep its footprint low.

The kit will be speedy, too, with NVIDIA HGX Rubin offering 800Gbit of integrated bandwidth per GPU.

ASUS is promoting the serviceability of these units through their use of tool list CPU and GPU sleds. It claims up to a 50 percent time saving on maintenance operations for these units, meaning less downtime per service and ultimately a lower total cost of ownership.

ASUS is structuring its hardware proposition around speedy delivery so that customers can get hardware up and running to support their AI initiatives. That speed comes from validation carried out before delivery with the help of a pre-validated rack reference architecture.

In keeping with its focus on multiple designs for the right AI workload, ASUS also supports NVIDIA's modular MGX Blueprint for server builds. The selling point here is flexibility with a mixture of GPUs, CPUs, and networking options for enterprise inference, agents, and visual computing. This is the modular route for workloads that don't need the power of an 8-GPU HGX baseboard, and it covers a large gamut of enterprise inference and agentic use cases.

ASUS showcased MGX servers including the 6U XA P8A-E14AXL with its eight liquid-cooled NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPUs. A 2U sibling, the XA P4N-E2, sports NVIDIA Vera processors for agentic work.

The ASUS booth also featured the ExpertCenter Pro ET900N G3, powered by the GB300 Grace Blackwell Superchip developer desktop with 748GB of coherent memory, and the Ascent GX10 based on NVIDIA DGX Spark  for agentic work. Also on show were the ProArt GR1X Mini PC and ProArt P16  and P14 laptop - the first laptops powered by NVIDIA RTX Spark - aimed at both developers and creators.

For edge installations, it showed the PE3000N, its rugged NVIDIA Jetson T5000-based edge-AI computer aimed at use cases including industrial environments. Announced a year ago, this delivers up to 2,070 FP4 TFLOPS of peak AI performance with 128GB of memory. Targeted at industrial AI environments was the PE6000G, a workstation-grade AI and simulation platform.

Equipment only supports an AI factory when there is power to run it. ASUS has been taking steps with NVIDIA as a partner here, adopting its DSX AI Factory platform with digital twin system for laying out hardware and power in AI factory environments. DSX supports equipment using the NVIDIA Vera Rubin processor line, which supports dry coolers and a lower PUE with a 45 degree centigrade warm water inlet.

ASUS configured racks become simulation-ready assets inside DSX, adopting its OpenUSD shared file format. That covers engineering data alongside the geometry for measuring real power draw and cooling requirements.

ASUS wanted to target cooling as part of the original design plan for the AI Factory, not as a facility afterthought. So, the DSX partnership allows implementation teams to model water temperature, flow, pressure, CDU placement, and piping within the digital twin model. They translate information from the planning resources that go around this, such as the NVIDIA Omniverse DSX Blueprint framework, using the ASUS Infrastructure Deployment Center.

The digital twin system isn't just for design and setup. It's supposed to keep working after go-live so that AI factory operators can simulate changes before touching hardware. The ASUS Control Center monitors the infrastructure while DSX exchange links IT and operational technology data. Operators can validate changes in the simulation before flipping the switch to go live.

ASUS' goal here is clear: to assist enterprise customers as they tackle in-house AI opportunities while acknowledging that the performance profile and physical locations of their workloads will vary. As companies continue to evolve their understanding of AI and where it fits within the organization, they will need to be agile in how they plan and nurture their infrastructure from the datacenter out to the edge, and to the developer's desk.

The Berlin announcements tie it together in a neat package for organizations that need help with their agentic aspirations.

Charles Dickens’ writing chalet and Hastings Castle added to list of English heritage sites at risk

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Left: Charles Dickens' Writing Chalet, Rochester, Kent. Right: Hastings Castle

Dickens’ chalet: Photo: Nick Johnson - The Imageworks. Hastings Castle: © Historic England Archive

The cabin where Charles Dickens spent the last day of his life, the home in Stratford-upon-Avon of William Shakespeare’s daughter Susanna and the castle built by William the Conqueror high on the cliffs above the town of Hastings are among 197 buildings and sites added to Historic England’s Heritage at Risk Register today (5 November).

The list identifies sites such as buildings, parks, battlefields and shipwrecks that are threatened by decay, neglect or inappropriate development. There are now 4,991 entries on the register, all of which are labelled as of national importance. This encompasses 197 sites that were added this year.

The miniature Swiss chalet which became the writing retreat of Charles Dickens was a Christmas gift to the author in 1864, arrived flat packed in 94 pieces, and was put together by a theatre carpenter after Dickens and his house guests failed. Dickens placed it at the furthest end of the garden, reached by a tunnel he built under the road at his last home, Gad’s Hill, near Rochester in Kent. He spent much of 9 June 1870 working in the chalet on The Mystery of Edwin Drood, before returning to the house where he died that night, aged just 58.The house, now a school, and tunnel survive, but the chalet was moved in the 1960s to the garden of Eastgate House in Rochester, where it is now too fragile for public access.

Hall’s Croft, an imposing timber-framed building dating from 1613, was the home of Susanna Shakespeare and her husband John Hall.After many uses, and residents including the playwright George Bernard Shaw and the bestselling Victorian romantic novelist Marie Corelli, it was acquired by the Shakespeare Birthplace Trust and opened to the public in 1951.It had already been closed for several years for extensive conservation work when, in October 2025, a car reversed into the facade and caused further serious damage.The playwright Ken Ludwig has donated £1m to the work but major fundraising will still be needed.

Hall’s Croft, Old Town, Stratford-upon-Avon, Warwickshire

Hastings Castle was one of the first three such sites built by William the Conqueror after his victory in the famous battle—and is depicted in the Bayeux Tapestry. The site, with a sweeping view across a stretch of the coast, has a long history, with remains of an Iron Age fort having been discovered there. The castle’s commanding site has made it very vulnerable to storms and coastal erosion and there have already been urgent works to stabilise its ruins. Future plans for the site include the creation of a new visitor centre and cafe.

Along with the sites that have been added this year there is good news: 85 sites for which solutions have been found, and which have now been removedfrom the register. They include the storm-lashed Newlyn Harbour in Cornwall and a little hut in the Gloucestershire garden of Edward Jenner, in which, in 1796, he performed the first-ever vaccinations.

A pair of elegant Georgian houses in east London, built around 1715 and Grade II* listed, had an unusual saviour: Tottenham Hotspur Football Club, which has converted them—with help from the Lottery Heritage Fund—into artists studios, gallery spaces, and a cafe. The space is run by Sarabande Foundation, which was founded by the late fashion designer Lee Alexander McQueen.

The Georgian terraced house saved from dereliction by Tottenham Hotspur FC. The house is now home to artists’ studios, galleries and café run by Sarabande Foundation, which was established by Lee Alexander McQueen

Rescue grants available include the Heritage at Risk Capital Fund and the Places of Worship Renewal Fund, both funded by the Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport and administered by Historic England, and the Countryside Stewardship Heritage Service.

The UK’s heritage minister Baroness Twycross said in a statement that the register has a crucial role in identifying historic places in need of support.“Every part of the UK is bursting with history, so it is vital we protect the heritage sites at the heart of communities which help tell the stories of our nation.”

Claudia Kenyatta and Emma Squire, the joint chief executives of Historic England, said the most effective way of protecting the sites was to bring them back into use.“The sites saved and removed from the Register this year show what can be achieved when partners and communities work together to deliver positive lasting change.”

Taylor’s in Loughborough faces uphill struggle in fundraising drive to restore decaying buildings and create new museum

After a £4.3m National Lottery Heritage Fund grant and years of restoration, the historic priory is set to open this summer

Cyprus: Island of Snakes, Cats, and Ophiolites

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The third-largest island in the Mediterranean Sea exposes an ancient piece of the ocean floor and hosts a clowder of cats.

The island of Cyprus began to form some 90 million years ago during the Late Cretaceous, with molten rock pouring from the seafloor, solidifying, and building new oceanic crust. Over time, portions of this oceanic rock were wrenched upward by tectonic forces, eventually emerging as a rugged island made of ophiolite, a type of rock with a mottled green appearance said to look like the skin of a snake. Remnants of that ancient island are still visible in the dark rocks of the Troodos Mountains.

During the Miocene, considerably younger layers of chalk, limestone, marl, and other marine sedimentary rocks accumulated around the island, providing material that developed into a second, smaller island to the south during a later period of geologic uplift. Over time, sediment poured from rivers into the channel separating the two islands and combined with marine sediments to create deltas and eventually two land bridges, or tombolos. The joined landmass comprises the Akrotiri Peninsula, now the southernmost tip of Cyprus.

The sandy edges of the peninsula were visible when Landsat 8 captured these images on October 10, 2026. Akrotiri Salt Lake, a coastal lagoon that was once an arm of the Mediterranean Sea, lies between the two tombolos. The more westerly feature, the Kouris River tombolo, formed first, developing about 50,000 years ago from river-deposited gravels and sands. The eastern tombolo extended southward from Cyprus, cutting off the Mediterranean Sea only within the past 2,000 years, according to geologists.

The lake's water levels now vary markedly with the seasons, typically filling in the late autumn and winter and drying out in the summer. The influx of winter water has historically attracted sizable overwintering flamingo populations, averaging about 4,000 birds. However, the wintering flamingo population has declined in recent years, plummeting to 30 birds in 2025, according to news reports. Flamingos have long flocked to the lake to feed on fairy shrimp, which are rich in colorful substances called carotenoids that give the birds their famously pink hue.

Snakes are another of the island's more notorious animal inhabitants. In antiquity, Cyprus was known as Ophiousa, or "land of snakes," likely due to its robust population of blunt-nosed vipers. According to legend, the island's snake communities grew to be so problematic during a drought in the fourth century that the Roman Empress Helena, mother of Constantine the Great, called for shiploads of cats to be sent from Egypt to control the venomous reptiles.

The first feline imports were released in a particularly snake-infested area on the southeastern part of the Akrotiri Peninsula, an area that came to be known as Cape Gata (Cape Cat in Greek). The cape today is still home to the Holy Monastery of St. Nicholas of the Cats, a place where legend says that monks encouraged newly arrived Egyptian cats to hunt snakes. Today, Cyprus has as many cats as people, with more than one million of the feline predators stalking the island. The handful of nuns who now live in the monastery still care for hundreds of cats, according to the St. Nicholas Center.

NASA Earth Observatory images by Michala Garrison, using Landsat data from the U.S. Geological Survey. Story by Adam Voiland.
