Solana (SOL), Ethereum (ETH), Near Protocol (NEAR) and Zcash (ZEC) Price Analysis For November 5: Crypto Market's Chance to Turn Bullish Again

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Solana (SOL) has lost its grip on the psychologically important $100 level, and the selling pressure keeps building. After a strong September rally that pushed SOL above $120, the token has slid into a long correction, with sellers increasingly in control of daily trading.

On November 4, SOL was trading around $92.69, extending its drop from the October highs near $124.

On the daily timeframe, the picture keeps getting worse, with Solana trading below all of its major moving averages. SOL has also fallen below the longer-term moving average near $98, which acted as support during the September recovery.

The breakdown puts Solana in a weak spot, especially now that its shorter-term moving averages have started turning down.

The $98–$100 zone is now the closest resistance. A proper recovery above it could improve sentiment and let SOL test the $103–$108 area. As long as the price stays below these levels, the chart stays bearish.

Solana's Relative Strength Index (RSI) has slipped toward the lower end of its recent range and is getting close to oversold territory. Selling pressure may run out at some point, but the indicator hasn't shown a convincing bullish reversal yet.

Trading volume has also picked up during the latest drop, which makes the breakdown even more meaningful.

The closest support sits near $90, where buyers could try to stop the drop. A clear break below it could send Solana toward $85 and then the $80 region. Reclaiming $100, on the other hand, would be the first real step toward rebuilding bullish momentum.

Unfortunately for the bulls, Solana stays bearish for now, with sellers firmly in control after the break below multiple moving averages.

Ethereum (ETH) is trying to find its feet after a correction that wiped out much of its September rally. It's showing early signs of a bounce from recent lows, but the second-largest cryptocurrency still faces heavy resistance, so the short-term outlook is far from clear.

According to the November 4 daily ETH/USD chart, Ethereum was trading around $2,317, a modest bounce after a brief dip toward the $2,200 region. However, buyers haven't taken back control yet.

The recent weakness started with a rejection from the 2,750–2,800 resistance area in early October. Since then, ETH has been making lower highs and lower lows, a clear sign of persistent selling pressure.

ETH is now trading below several important moving averages, including the shorter-term ones around 2,400–2,500.

The closest resistance is around $2,350, with more selling pressure possible near $2,400. ETH needs a proper breakout above these levels before anyone can say the decline is losing steam.

Ethereum is also hovering near its longer-term moving average around $2,300, which makes the current price zone key for the next move.

The Relative Strength Index (RSI) has dropped a lot from its October highs as bullish momentum faded. However, it's starting to flatten near the lower part of its range, which suggests selling pressure could be easing. That may support a short-term bounce, but a flat RSI alone doesn't confirm a trend reversal.

The closest support is around $2,250, followed by the psychologically important $2,200 level. A break below this zone could open the door to a deeper correction toward $2,100.

On the upside, reclaiming $2,350 would be a good first step, and a move above $2,400 could make the case for a recovery toward $2,500 much stronger.

NEAR Protocol (NEAR) couldn't hold on to its October rally, and sellers are back in charge. NEAR is back in correction mode, with the price dropping below several important technical indicators.

According to the November 4 daily chart, NEAR is trading around $4.02, well below its October peak near $6. NEAR has slipped below its shorter-term moving averages, a clear sign that bullish momentum is fading.

The $4.30–$4.60 zone is now the key resistance, where several moving averages collide. A recovery above it could open the way to a rebound toward $4.80 and possibly $5.

However, NEAR is still above its longer-term moving averages near $3.65 and $3, so the bigger recovery isn't completely broken yet.

The Relative Strength Index (RSI) has dropped toward the lower half of its range as buying pressure eases. It's getting close to oversold territory, but there's no convincing bullish reversal signal yet.

Trading volume is also fairly low compared with September's explosive rally.

The closest support is at $3.80, followed by stronger support around $3.65. A clear break below these levels could send NEAR further down toward $3.40. Reclaiming $4.30, on the other hand, would be an early sign that buyers are coming back.

While the longer-term trend still has some support underneath, NEAR's short-term outlook stays bearish for now.

Zcash (ZEC) seems to be waking up again after bouncing off its recent consolidation lows. Even after a big correction from its October highs, the privacy coin is still trading above its major long-term moving averages.

According to the November 4 daily chart, ZEC is trading near $1,288 after bouncing from a recent dip toward the $1,150 region.

ZEC is now trying to reclaim the $1,300 resistance, which capped the price again and again throughout October. A breakout above it could open the door to $1,350, and then to the psychologically important $1,400 level.

However, sellers have kept showing up around these prices, so buyers will need stronger momentum for a proper recovery.

ZEC is still comfortably above its longer-term moving averages near $1,050 and $850, which keeps the bigger picture fairly healthy.

The Relative Strength Index (RSI) has climbed back toward the neutral 50 level after dropping during October's correction. Selling pressure may be easing, but the indicator hasn't confirmed a clear bullish shift yet.

Trading volume is also moderate, so the latest bounce hasn't drawn the same crowd as September's explosive rally.

For now, Zcash looks stronger than many altcoins on the chart, but it still needs a confirmed breakout above $1,300 before the bulls can celebrate.

Bitcoin rebounds toward $79K – Can BTC keep climbing without US buyers?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Bitcoin’s [BTC] latest recovery is struggling to attract stronger participation from U.S. buyers, leaving an important source of market demand largely absent.

The Coinbase premium index measures the difference between the price on Coinbase and Binance.

As such, its extended period of time being in the negative direction clearly illustrates that the U.S. has continued to exert selling pressure against the amount of demand from U.S. buyers.

Notably, the index did show a negative reading even at a point when Bitcoin was rising toward $87,000 in September. This implies that there were other regions of the world supporting price appreciation at that time.

If this trend persists, it could cap the size of Bitcoin’s next advance. As a result, it could limit recovery strength and expose Bitcoin to further selling. Conversely, a sustained move above zero would indicate that U.S. demand is beginning to return.

Meanwhile, the combined spot and futures demand indicator for Bitcoin over a 30-day window is currently reporting a decline toward a reading of -200,000 BTC. These results reflect continued net outflows from the global market.

However, this week’s reading has shown some improvements as spot demand has become increasingly less negative. Therefore, these results indicate that sellers appear to be slowing down their sales momentum.

This disparity explains why it continues to be difficult for Bitcoin to maintain its current recovery amid easing downside pressure. On the other hand, declining negative demand could provide stability in terms of maintaining current prices around $77,000.

Ultimately, a sustained return to positive total demand for Bitcoin would significantly improve the prospects for a successful recovery. In particular, if spot buying resumes.

Although Bitcoin demand shows early signs of stabilization, its price structure still reflects considerable selling pressure. BTC has rebounded from $76,000 to around $78,490, but the recovery remains gradual.

This suggests short-term recovery and reduced selling rather than strong new buying. The rebound now faces a critical test near $79,000, where previous support has turned into resistance.

Closing prices above $79,200 could lead to higher prices such as $80,400 and $82,000. On the other hand, if prices are rejected at this level, BTC may decline toward $76,975 and possibly lower toward $76,000.

Overall, Bitcoin must regain the $79,200 price level in order to fortify its current recovery. Failure to do so could result in continued control of the broader downtrend.

Bitcoin [BTC] faces weak U.S. demand despite signs of easing selling pressure.

BTC must reclaim $79,200 to strengthen its recovery, with rejection risking a drop toward $76,000.

Why 17 Regional Gates Matter So Much To The Airline Carrying Over 60% Of Miami's Passengers

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Paul Bickford | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Miami International Airport (MIA) is preparing for a major transformation, and one of its most important developments involves an area that many passengers would rather forget. The $1 billion D60 expansion will replace an outdated regional boarding facility at Concourse D with 17 traditional contact gates, bringing an end to the bus transfers and outdoor boarding arrangements that have long characterized this part of the airport.

While the investment is substantial, its significance extends well beyond improving the appearance of a boarding area. For American Airlines, which carries more than 60% of Miami International Airport's passengers, the project represents an important investment in the infrastructure supporting its largest international gateway.

With approximately 400 daily departures from Miami, the airline depends on the airport to connect passengers across the US, Latin America, the Caribbean, and Europe. Improving just 17 gates could therefore have considerable consequences for its operations, passenger experience, and future growth.

Miami International Airport occupies a particularly important position in American Airlines' network. Unlike many other major American airports, Miami provides the oneworld carrier with an extensive collection of connections to destinations throughout Latin America and the Caribbean.

Its geographical position makes it a natural gateway for passengers traveling between the US and numerous international markets, giving the airline a substantial competitive advantage in the region. American Airlines operates approximately 400 daily departures from Miami International Airport, making the airport one of its most important operational bases.

Its presence is also reflected in its passenger market share, with the airline carrying more than 60% of the airport's traffic. This concentration means that improvements to Miami's infrastructure have an especially direct impact on American Airlines compared with smaller operators using the airport. The largest airlines at Miami International Airport by market share, as per data from the US Bureau of Transportation Statistics, are outlined in the table below.

The importance of the hub is also connected to the wider economic significance of Miami International Airport. The airport handled 55.3 million passengers in 2025, ranking it among the busiest airports in the US. According to Miami-Dade County, its economic activity generates approximately $212 billion in business revenue annually and supports nearly 946,000 jobs.

This demonstrates how closely aviation is connected to the surrounding region's prosperity. For American Airlines, maintaining a strong position at Miami International Airport is therefore about considerably more than operating a large number of flights.

The airport supports connections between domestic markets and destinations across the Caribbean and Latin America, while also providing opportunities to attract international travelers connecting through the US. Infrastructure that accommodates growing passenger numbers is essential if the airline wants to preserve and strengthen this position.

The current D60 facility is unusual compared with the conventional boarding arrangements passengers experience elsewhere at major international airports. Rather than providing individual boarding areas connected directly to aircraft through passenger boarding bridges, the existing arrangement uses a shared boarding space serving 17 ground-level regional aircraft positions.

Passengers must consequently follow a less convenient process to reach their aircraft. For travelers, this can mean leaving the terminal building and boarding a bus before being transported to an aircraft parked outside the main concourse. In other circumstances, passengers use outdoor boarding arrangements, exposing them to Miami's heat, humidity and unpredictable weather.

Although such procedures are not unusual at airports serving regional aircraft, they are increasingly difficult to justify at a major international gateway. The shared boarding arrangement also creates practical challenges during busy operating periods. Passengers traveling on different flights must use the same general waiting area.

This makes the environment potentially crowded and confusing when several departures are approaching simultaneously. Managing boarding announcements, passenger movements, and flight delays becomes more complicated when multiple aircraft depend on a common facility rather than separate gate areas.

These limitations are particularly noticeable given the scale of American Airlines' Miami operation. An airport handling tens of millions of passengers annually needs facilities capable of supporting consistent passenger flows, including during peak departure periods.

The existing regional facility represents an increasingly outdated part of an otherwise extensive hub, making its redevelopment an important step towards improving the overall experience. American Airlines' regional flights are operated under the American Eagle brand. The carrier's regional subsidiary's flights are operated by the aircraft detailed in the table above, according to data from ch-aviation.

The modernization plan is expected to last five to 15 years.

The D60 expansion will transform the existing regional boarding area into a three-level extension of Concourse D. Rather than simply renovating the shared waiting space, the project will convert the 17 ground-level regional aircraft positions into 17 traditional contact gates, each featuring its own passenger hold room.

This will provide a considerably more conventional boarding experience for passengers departing from the facility. The new gates will be designed to accommodate larger regional aircraft and narrowbody aircraft, providing greater flexibility than the existing arrangement. This is particularly important for American Airlines because aircraft requirements can change as passenger demand develops across individual routes.

The ability to accommodate different aircraft types gives the airline additional options when allocating capacity and planning its future operations. Passenger facilities will also receive considerable attention. Each gate will have an individual boarding area, while the expansion will introduce additional dining and shopping opportunities.

An improved baggage handling system is another important component, helping the airport support the increased operational requirements associated with the expanded facility. Together, these improvements should make the experience considerably more comfortable and convenient. The project is scheduled to break ground in 2027, with completion initially targeted for 2030.

However, Miami International Airport's published capital improvement information also identifies 2033 as the scheduled completion date, illustrating that the delivery timetable remains subject to planning developments. The project is primarily financed through airport bonds, alongside $34 million in grants from the Florida Department of Transportation.

One of the most significant benefits of D60 will be the removal of the bus boarding process for regional flights. Although the $1 billion investment includes several major infrastructure improvements, this particular change is likely to be among the most immediately noticeable for passengers.

Instead of traveling through a shared boarding area and transferring to an aircraft outside, travelers will use individual gates connected directly to the concourse. This creates a more predictable journey from the departure lounge to the aircraft. Passengers will be able to wait in designated boarding areas, receive flight information at their individual gates, and board without relying on a bus transfer.

The improvement should also make the boarding process more accessible for passengers with reduced mobility, families with young children, and travelers carrying additional luggage. Weather is another important consideration in South Florida. Miami's tropical climate can bring intense heat, heavy rainfall, and thunderstorms, making outdoor boarding less comfortable than conventional terminal facilities.

Eliminating unnecessary exposure to these conditions will provide a more pleasant experience, particularly for passengers traveling during the summer months when temperatures and humidity can become especially challenging. There are operational advantages for American Airlines as well. Removing the need to coordinate buses and outdoor passenger movements could simplify certain aspects of ground handling.

However, actual improvements to turnaround times will depend on staffing, aircraft scheduling, and other operational factors. More importantly, individual gates will provide a more organized environment in which airline employees can manage boarding and assist customers. The busiest domestic destinations from Miami International Airport last year are outlined in the table above.

The most common aircraft type will operate 4,363 flights from Miami International Airport in April 2025.

An especially important feature of the D60 development involves its connections to the US Customs and Border Protection facility. The expansion will provide third-level connections to Concourse D's customs infrastructure, creating the capability to accommodate international arrivals.

This is significant because Miami is not simply a domestic hub for American Airlines but one of its most important international gateways. The additional connections will help integrate the expanded facility into the airport's international passenger processing arrangements. International arrivals require appropriate separation and secure passenger circulation before travelers reach customs and immigration facilities.

Incorporating these connections into the design means the new gates can offer greater operational flexibility as American Airlines develops its international network. This is particularly relevant to the airline's extensive presence across Latin America and the Caribbean. Demand between Miami and these destinations can vary considerably, while aircraft assignments and route requirements evolve over time.

Infrastructure capable of supporting international arrivals provides additional possibilities for managing flights, rather than restricting the facility to a narrower range of regional operations. The busiest international routes from Miami International Airport last year are detailed in the table above. For passengers, improved integration could eventually mean more convenient international travel through Concourse D.

The benefits will depend on how individual services are allocated and how the airport implements its passenger processing arrangements, but the underlying infrastructure represents an important improvement. It also demonstrates that D60 has been designed around the long-term requirements of an international hub rather than simply replacing an inconvenient regional boarding facility.

D60 is part of Miami International Airport's wider Modernization in Action program, a multibillion-dollar investment covering improvements across the airport. The broader program includes developments to passenger facilities, terminal infrastructure, baggage systems, and other essential airport services.

Consequently, the Concourse D expansion should be understood as one component of a much larger effort to prepare Miami International Airport for continued passenger growth. Other major projects include the development of Concourse K, which is scheduled to open in 2029, and the redevelopment of the Central Terminal, currently targeted for completion around 2031.

These developments will complement improvements to Concourse D by increasing capacity and modernizing facilities across different parts of the airport. The combined investment is intended to create a more efficient and passenger-friendly international gateway. For American Airlines, these improvements are particularly important because of its dominant position at Miami International Airport, ahead of its rivals, Delta Air Lines and United Airlines.

An airport carrying more than 60% of its passengers on a single airline needs infrastructure that can support the demands of a major hub, rather than facilities designed around much smaller regional operations. Better gates, more comfortable boarding areas, and improved international connections all contribute to the airline's ability to serve its customers.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Ultimately, the significance of D60 lies in the difference between the airport American Airlines needs and the facilities it currently has available. 17 gates might appear relatively modest within an airport handling more than 55 million passengers annually, but their transformation addresses a longstanding weakness in one of the carrier's most important hubs. As such, Miami International Airport is making a substantial investment in the future of its busiest airline.

Little Ice Age cooling in Europe may have raised the risk of rickets

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Skeletal remains with signs of vitamin D deficiencies hint at how climate change impacted health

A volcano-fueled chilly climate during the 18th and 19th centuries may have made winter in England so bad, as depicted in this 19th century painting of a man collecting wood, that it raised the risk of kids getting rickets, a condition caused by vitamin D deficiency.

England isn’t renowned for its weather. But just a few centuries ago, climate change–fueled conditions may have been so bad that they put children’s health at risk.

Calcium mineralization in the teeth and skeletons of people who lived in rural northern England suggests that children growing up during the height of the Little Ice Age routinely developed vitamin D deficiencies, researchers report November 5 in Frontiers in Environmental Archaeology. In the worst cases, these deficiencies developed into rickets, a bone disorder that can occur when people don’t get enough ultraviolet light.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Previous skeletal studies suggest that rickets became more common among Europeans during the 18th and 19th centuries. One culprit may have been the Industrial Revolution, when many Europeans lived in UV-poor polluted cities and often worked indoors. The unusually cold climate may have added “an extra layer of difficulty” to getting enough sunlight, says Ellen Kendall, a bioarcheologist at Durham University in England

The Little Ice Age was a period of global cooling from the 13th to 19th centuries caused by extreme solar cycles and a series of volcanic eruptions, which can cool the planet by ejecting aerosols into the atmosphere that block sunlight. In the last 200 years of the era, 18 major eruptions helped spur a temperature drop of 2 to 3 degrees Celsius over northern Europe.

Kendall and colleagues wondered how this cold climate affected health. They examined calcium deposition — how the essential mineral is incorporated into bones — in the remains of 21 people buried from 1711 to 1829 in the town of North Shields. Calcium often doesn’t fully mineralize in people with vitamin D delicacies, leaving voids in teeth as they grow.

The team found bands of failed calcium mineralization in the teeth, suggesting seasonal spates of vitamin D deficiencies. Some skeletons also showed signs of rickets. North Shields was a small rural town that hadn’t yet undergone industrialization, so these deficiencies probably weren’t due to bad air conditions.

The unusually cold weather may have changed people’s behavior during the winter, Kendall says. Residents may have kept their kids indoors or bundled them up so that little skin was exposed when they did go outside, preventing them from getting enough UV light, she says.

However, climate was still just one factor among many — including gender roles, diet and pollution — that shaped vitamin D deficiencies at the time, says Kendall. “Humans are very messy to study,” she says. “That’s one of the things that makes them so very fascinating.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

E. Kendall. ‘The bright sun was extiguish’d’: metabolic health and vitamin D status in a Little Ice Age population (18-19th c.) in North East England. Frontiers in Environmental Archaeology. 2026. Doi: 10.3389/fearc.2026.1909791

Freda Kreier was a fall 2021 intern at Science News. She holds a bachelor’s degree in molecular biology from Colorado College and a master’s in science communication from the University of California, Santa Cruz.

Volcanic eruptions and less sunlight made Victorian Era kids sicker

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 12:00 AM EST

The Industrial Revolution overturned centuries of English agrarian life, but the rapid societal transformation came at steep costs to the laborers themselves. Miserable working conditions, poor housing, and pollution all contributed to health problems among factory employees as well as their families. Meanwhile, children faced countless diseases like rickets, which prevents developing skeletons from adequately mineralizing.

However, a recent investigation into a 19th century Quaker cemetery in northeast England indicates the Industrial Revolution wasn’t the sole culprit behind this rickets endemic. After analyzing teeth from some of the graveyard’s inhabitants, archaeologists now believe that reduced exposure to ultraviolet (UV) rays likely exacerbated vitamin D deficiencies during the waning years of the Little Ice Age. The dental evidence—along with the Quakers’ corroborating records—is presented in a study published today in the journal Frontiers in Environmental Archaeology.

“We think of climate instability as a modern challenge, but it’s really nothing new as human problems go,” explained study co-author and Durham University archaeologist Ellen Kendall.

Unlike today’s climate crisis, humans didn’t generate the Little Ice Age. Instead, the prolonged period of cooling during the 13th through 19th centuries was the result of increased volcanic eruptions and lower solar activity. For hundreds of years, these combined factors minimized the UV exposure humans often rely on to generate vitamin D.

Although the Little Ice Age is well documented in historical records, it is still largely analyzed for its effects on the environment and agriculture. Kendall’s team wondered if the same climatic shifts were visible in the remains of the people who lived through it.

“Human bioarchaeologists’ primary area of expertise is understanding health from the human skeleton,” explained Kendall. “To do that, we have to consider a wide range of issues: political, environmental, dietary, social, and more.”

The study’s authors suspected climate’s influence on human bodies during the Industrial Revolution still remained overlooked. To investigate, the team examined 30 teeth from 21 previously recovered individuals buried in a Quaker cemetery between 1711 and 1829. They particularly focused on mineralization problems, such as a lack of calcification in the inner tooth layers called dentin.

Deficient calcification sometimes occurs naturally, but biologists suspect vitamin D deficiency when teeth are regularly stratified by layers of hardened and soft dentin. Teeth grow on a predictable schedule, so the team looked at each individual’s dentin and confirmed most of their issues occurred during infancy and childhood. This is where many researchers would have ended their work, but meticulous historic records allowed Kendall’s group to date each individual’s dentin issues even more precisely.

“The Quakers were known to keep precise records, which makes them an important source of information about themselves, but also about the northeast of England, where they were very prominent in local history,” said Kendall.

Researchers couldn’t match teeth to specific individuals, but archival information allowed them to look at the wider community. Of 109 individuals matched to birth years, 91 were born within a decade following a major volcanic eruption. Of these, 41 were born during periods where volcanic activity and low solar energy overlapped.

Kendall and the study’s co-authors aren’t arguing the Industrial Revolution had less of an impact on human health than experts think. Instead, they contend that these natural factors also exacerbated the problems. It’s rare to find a sole culprit in these situations, but acknowledging the larger picture allows a fuller, more nuanced understanding of historical highs and lows. According to Kendall, humanity is a complicated species defined by biology, cultural systems, and the environment around us.

“No part overrides another; they all interact,” she added.

Yes, not a single second of gameplay — or any actual footage of the RPG whatsoever — has been made public.

However, the Official Bethesda Gear Store will still sell you a t-shirt or a hoodie with the game's logo plastered across it.

The t-shirt is $34.99 and the hoodie is $64.99 before shipping:

Watch ‘The Greatest’ Team Discuss How They Made the Impossible ‘Possible’ on Muhammad Ali Series

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As tall an order as it was to make a series about the greatest boxer of all time, “The Greatest” creator Ben Watkins had faith he assembled the right team for the job and the unique assurance of making it with the blessing of Muhammed Ali’s family. At IndieWire’s recent Consider This event at Pacific Design Center, presented in partnership with Prime Video, he broke down the process that brought the series to life.

“One of the things I want to do is humanize Muhammad Ali because everybody sort of pictures him as a fully formed icon and legend. And I want to disconnect people from that and introduce them to a kid who just had a dream. But that means I got to show all of him. I got to show flaws and fears and vulnerabilities and tell the truth like that. And it’s sometimes messy,” Watkins said, recalling his pitch to the boxer’s widow Lonnie Ali . “Her response was, ‘That’s what I want too,’ because that will make him more relatable. And as we introduce him to a younger generation and reintroduce him to the people who already love him, that is what will make this story fresh.” Related Stories Alan Cumming Says ‘Tip Toe’ Was His Greatest Role Ever — but He Never Wants to Think About It Again ‘The Greatest’ Review: Amazon’s Handsome Muhammed Ali Biopic Connects in Spite of Itself

Boxing is an individual sport, but making “The Greatest” was a team effort. Watkins was joined on the panel by many of his crafts team members, including editor Steve Lang, costume designer Marci Rodgers, production designer Elisa Suavé, composer Stanley A. Smith, music editor Jordan Wiggins, and music supervisor Robin Urdang.

One of the aspects that made the eight-episode limited series tracking the protagonist’s rise from Olympian gold medalist Cassius Clay to heavyweight champion Muhammad Ali feel fresh was the use of contemporary music, but that also came with its own challenges for the post production team.

“Going contemporary in an Olympic boxing match with so many things going on between commentary, the fight itself, the reactions, and then we also have a song playing. I think we went through about 120 songs,” said Lang, telling the story of how they landed on a key piece of music for the series opener.

“I usually come on pre-production, [but] I came on during post, and one of the first things was ‘We want an original song here and we need it now,’” Urdang added. “I said, ‘We can’t get it until we create the sound of the show because there’s no sound of the show right now.’”

That first music cue became the last song they used for the show, an original track from Grammy winner Wyclef Jean titled “Gladiator.” “There was a poem that [Ali] hears about a gladiator talking about his time in the Colosseum. Ben mentions that to Wyclef, who then takes those lyrics and turns them into the song that you hear during that fight,” said Lang.

“It ended up in two spots in episode one, and then it also became the main title [music],” said Wiggins. “It was a lot of cutting that Jordan did for us,” said Urdang.

The Colosseum also happened to be the setting for one of costume designer Marci Rodgers’ greatest challenges working on “The Greatest.” “I was put on the spot in Rome where I had to do a quick change on camera, which is actually the first scene [as well]” she said. “So we’re in the Colosseum and it’s Marci’s time. And so I reached out to one of my mentors, Paul Tazewell, who walked me through a process in which we see essentially his suit removed as the camera is moving around.”

“Those are the things that they said would not be possible. Then the first production meeting I said, ‘Hey, I want to do a shot where he’s in a suit and as we’re circling, it all goes away,’” said Watkins. First it was a challenge to even shoot on location, but “We ended up [as] the first television show to ever shoot in the Colosseum,” he said. Then it was getting confirmation that they could also shoot more around Rome. And finally, it was shooting the aforementioned pivotal scene. “At that point I said, ‘We’ll never get that shot off.’ And then Marci says, ‘No, we’re going to figure out a way to build a suit, to do it in a way to get the shot off.’ And then Elisa and her team figured out a way for us to not just be able to shoot in the Colosseum, but they recreated a 60s-level Italian neighborhood because, ‘Hey, we’re in Rome now, so let’s get the most out of it.’ And they got us all of that, all things they said they couldn’t get.”

For her part, Suavé said “When you look at the world in the ’50s and the ’60s, there was no digital anything. Every single sign, every single thing you think was made by our scenic artists by hand, not with projections that they were copying. We did it all by hand. We made all the molds for all the parking meters. We made the molds and we created the gold medals.” She added, “There was not one world that we didn’t touch or create.”

Composer Stanley A. Smith also chimed in on how the series kept things analog, saying “We use all live musicians in this score.” “No AI,” added Urdang.

“Ben was really great about going to bat for getting the budget to do that. And we used live string players.” said Smith. “Just because of the nature of the tone we wanted, we used predominantly people of color for our strings — not always — but because we wanted that feel. And actually, I’m going to go one step further. It was 60 to 40 percent female.”

Ending the panel, Watkins said “That’s the big testament to everybody up here. We took what I would say is a modest television budget and we made a high budget film.”

“The Greatest” is now streaming on Prime Video. Watch the complete panel with the showrunner and artisans who worked on the limited series in the video above.

Feds barred from pressing Washington state to toss voters’ ballots, for now

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A federal judge found Justice Department prosecutors demanded, not requested, that Washington set aside roughly 400 votersâ ballots and threatened election officials who count them.

(CN) â Federal prosecutors can ask Washington state to set aside votersâ ballots, but they canât demand it, a federal judge in Seattle said Thursday.

U.S. District Judge Jamal Whitehead temporarily barred the Justice Department from pressing the state over a list of about 400 voters.

The Joe Biden appointee partially granted a request from OneAmerica Votes, the Latino Community Fund of Washington State and the United Food and Commercial Workers International Union, whose members include naturalized citizens.

On Oct. 26, eight days before Election Day, Acting U.S. Attorney Pete Serrano of the Eastern District of Washington and First Assistant U.S. Attorney Neil Floyd of the Western District sent Secretary of State Steve Hobbs the list. Mail ballots were already coming back to county offices.

They gave Hobbs 24 hours to review it and told him to have county election officials hold off on checking the signatures on those votersâ ballots, or counting them, until their “citizenship can be determined.â

At a press conference that day, Floyd warned that anyone who helps someone vote illegally âwould be also guilty of that crime under the aiding abetting theory.â

Hobbs refused two days later. Washingtonâs review found the list unreliable. At least 62 people on it hadnât been sent a ballot because their registrations were canceled, inactive or couldnât be found.

âWashington reviewed the list and found that it includes United States citizens â people who had already shown the state proof of their citizenship. Nothing in the record suggests that anyone on the list has been charged with a crime. The federal government has kept up the pressure anyway and has promised more lists and more letters,â Whitehead said.

Serrano said on the radio the next day that the department would turn to county auditors and could send “39 letters to 39 auditors over the next couple days.â He described the goal as making sure these ballots werenât counted.

The government told the judge it had taken no further action since the Oct. 26 letter. Whitehead disagreed.

âTheir own statements say otherwise,â he said.

The government argued the letter was only a request Washington was free to turn down, pointing to a recent Ninth Circuit ruling that put a nationwide order against similar demands on hold.

Whitehead said the distinction was between asking and compelling. The government, he said, âmay ask a state to set ballots aside, but it may not compel one to do so.â

âOn this record, defendants did not ask,â he said.

The Ninth Circuit ruled last week in a California case that a law criminalizing noncitizens voting in federal elections doesnât let the Justice Department âtake charge of or intervene in a stateâs administration of an ongoing election.â The Supreme Court declined to disturb that ruling Monday.

Whitehead found union members face a real risk of being discouraged from voting or having their ballots set aside. Only one plaintiff needed a legal right to sue, he said, so he didnât decide whether the other two groups have one.

âVoters who followed the rules should not lose their votes to a list that even its authors admit may include citizens,â he said.

The order applies to the Justice Department, the Department of Homeland Security and four officials: Attorney General Todd Blanche, Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin, Serrano and Floyd.

They canât demand that any Washington election official remove specific voters from voter rolls, refuse them ballots or leave their ballots uncounted. They also canât threaten officials with criminal prosecution or investigation for refusing to comply.

The government can still investigate and prosecute people who vote illegally, share information with election officials, state its view of federal law and ask for cooperation. Whitehead did not order anything against President Donald Trump, who is also a defendant, and denied the governmentâs request to put the order on hold during an appeal.

The temporary restraining order lasts 14 days. Counties must certify results by Nov. 24. The government has until Nov. 12 to oppose a longer-lasting order, and Whitehead will hear arguments Nov. 18.

Representatives for the Justice Department and plaintiffsâ attorneys didnât immediately respond to requests for comment.

Bhatt, 39, is on trial over the 2024 disappearance and presumptive murder of his wife Mamta Kafle Bhatt, 28. He has pleaded not guilty but admitted during testimony to charges of defiling a body and concealing a body. He maintains he did not kill his wife.

Mamta Bhatt’s family members and friends, wide-eyed, sat in a row behind prosecutors. Spectators in the packed courtroom gasped at his admissions, which contrasted with his previous claims he had no knowledge of his missing wifeâs whereabouts. Prosecutors say he previously gave shifting accounts of his wife’s whereabouts after her disappearance.

Mamta Bhatt’s remains have not been found, leading prosecutors to lean on circumstantial evidence such as an increase in their home’s water usage and Naresh Bhatt’s web searches.

During his second day on the stand, Naresh Bhatt said that on July 30, 2024, he discovered his wife unresponsive and next to a poisonous substance in a pool of vomit.

Bhatt said he attempted medical interventions himself, pulling the body into the bathroom and pouring water into his wife’s mouth. Instead of dialing 911, he said he panicked and decided to get rid of the body.

“Why didn’t you call literally anyone else for help?” asked Tracey Lenox, his attorney and chief public defender for Prince William County. “Were you concerned about how it would look?”

“I was concerned about everything,” he said.

Lenox then asked if he was afraid he would be blamed for driving her to suicide.

Prosecutors have characterized Naresh Bhatt as abusive. He testified Wednesday that his wife’s demeanor had changed in the months following the birth of their child.

Naresh Bhatt has maintained that he never physically abused his wife. During cross-examination, Deputy Commonwealthâs Attorney Matthew Sweet derided this, pointing out that Bhatt chopped her up.

“She loved you, always,” the prosecutor said.

Naresh Bhatt testified that after unsuccessfully trying to rouse his wife, he took his infant daughter to a babysitter’s house and returned home, where he considered hanging himself. Instead, he went to Home Depot, “to find anything to hide a body.”

He said he considered dumping his wife’s corpse in the Potomac River but then decided to chop the body up, “just to make it a smaller size so it fit in the can,” he said.

It was difficult. “I was trying to lower the body apart,” he testified. “Like, separate the legs from the body.”

Mentally and physically, he felt powerless, he added, explaining that initially, he did not have the strength to continue.

Later, he got back to work, partially using knives but primarily depending upon a saw, in which prosecutors claimed to have found blood.

Lenox asked if Naresh Bhatt finished cutting the body before neighborhood trash pickup came by, and if he knew the body would then be taken away. Naresh Bhatt answered affirmatively.

Bhatt said he did not throw his wife’s body in trash dumpsters around the area, as prosecutors contend. Instead, he only tossed out clothing and trash in the dumpsters, he said. While wiping his eyes on occasion, he mostly spoke without hesitation.

Mamta Bhatt was a nurse, and attendees included a contingent of nurses dressed in scrubs. When Sweet pointed to Bhatt and said, “You’re a liar,” and Bhatt responded, “yes,” applause rippled through the crowd.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

(CN) â The California Coastal Commission voted unanimously on Thursday to approve a massive beach armoring project in San Clemente, California, in an effort to protect a valuable railroad line that runs along the coast that critics say comes at the expense of public beach access.

The permit allows the Orange County Transportation Authority, or OCTA, to retain and place about 50,000 tons of riprap, or large rocks, along the coast over the next 10-15 years to prevent the rail line from slipping into the ocean. It also includes the placement of 690,000 cubic yards of much-needed sand for beach nourishment and the construction of a seawall against part of the railroad.

The rail line in San Clemente has been increasingly threatened after recent storms in Southern California and the onset of Super El Nino.

Proponents of the project say it is necessary to protect the only railroad that links Los Angeles to San Diego, known as the LOSSAN Rail Corridor, that they say is vital to the regionâs economy and national security.

But critics of the permit describe the plan as the largest loss of public beach in the commissionâs history. They say that OCTAâs strategy of placing riprap along the beach has been irresponsible, shortsighted and ultimately a net negative for the rail corridor and the public.

Parts of San Clementeâs beaches have seen their width greatly reduced in the past decade, in some cases going from 200 feet in shore width to just 80, the commission staff said in its report on the permit.

Since 2021, the rail line has become increasingly vulnerable to coastal hazards, in part related to sea level rise and climate change, the commissionâs staff wrote. It has issued 15 similar track stabilization permits to OCTA and the Southern California Regional Rail Authority since then.

A few dozen residents and beach advocates expressed their disappointment during the meeting, held in San Francisco, that the continued approval of these permits has not helped anyone.

âThis is not a rail corridor,â Sarah Spinuzzi, legal director of the Orange County Coastkeeper, told commissioners.â©âIt is a community that has shaped California’s surf culture, home to the most prominent surfboard shapers around the world and the site of the 2028 Olympic surf venue.â

Spinuzzi was joined by a coalition that demanded guarantees from OCTA that it will maintain public beach access with a proactive approach and not just continue to armor the remaining beach.

Many public commenters asked for an amendment to OCTAâs permit to include a minimum of 50 feet of sandy beaches in front of the rocky revetments. Other groups, such as Save Our Beaches San Clemente, want to see an 80-foot minimum width of sandy beaches.

âOCTA has already destroyed Cypress Shore Beach and created impassable boulder piles where pristine beach once stood,â Lisa Gant, of Save Our Beaches San Clemente, told the commissioners. âPlease minimize the armoring footprint that OCTA is allowed to place on our beaches. Require OCTA to maintain a minimum sandy beach in front of their armoring. â¦ Require that they replenish that sand in a standardized schedule of monitoring and maintenance.â

Ocean waves scour the beach sand after the rocks are dumped, leaving less beach each year, said Save Our Beaches San Clemente President Suzie Whitelaw, who has a degree in marine sedimentology.

âThis process is inexorable,â she told the commission. âWeâre gonna be back here talking to you again and again and again for the next 10 years, and you’re gonna be seeing more and more rock and seawall added to our beaches, unless you do one simple thing, which is require them to maintain a wide, sandy beach in front of any armoring that they put in.â

Some commissioners found it difficult to approve a plan that had a long track record of failure.

âAlthough minimized, the impacts on public access and recreation are significant and unavoidable at this stage,â the commissionâs staff report said of the project.

Commissioner Susan Lowenberg, of San Francisco, described the transportation authorityâs plan as the definition of insanity. Other commissioners also expressed skepticism about OCTAâs proposal, given the history of beach armoring in San Clemente.

However, commissioners, officials and members of the public agreed that the sand nourishment proposal was much needed. Whether the proposed amount of sand was enough is not clear, commissioners and staff said.

The entire project might fall through if it wasnât approved today, Coastal Commission Executive Director Kate Huckelbridge said.

OCTAâs proposal is also contingent on $305 million in state and federal grant funding.

âI think doing nothing is never a good option, particularly in this case,â Commissioner Raymond Jackson, of Hermosa Beach, said. âThere’s no perfect solution. There is not even a good solution.â

Commissioners ultimately agreed to amend the permit to include a beach width assessment within five years of the project. The permit was approved, 11-0.

A spokesperson for OCTA did not respond to requests for comment.

(CN) â Montana appeared before a federal judge Thursday in an attempt to reinstate its ban on “sexually oriented performances.”

âTo answer a question likely to arise: yes, we do not defend every word as written,â Noonan said. âThat is not a confession of judgment; it is an opening move of the narrowing case.â

The dispute centers on Montana House Bill 359, signed by Governor Greg Gianforte in 2023. The bill restricted drag performers from reading books to children and banned minors from attending what it described as âsexually oriented shows.â

Under the law, owners, mangers and employees of those shows would have faced fines from $1,000 to $10,000, and, for a third offense, would have lost their business licenses. Teachers and librarians could have their teaching or administrative certificates permanently revoked for a second violation. It also created a private right of action for aggrieved parents to sue those parties for up to 10 years.

A coalition of Montana businesses, nonprofits and performers who said the bill targets their First Amendment rights sued the state and in late 2023, U.S. District Judge Brian Morris ruled in their favor, preliminarily blocking enforcement of the bill.

This March, the Ninth Circuit upheld the injunction, finding the legislationâs restrictions are content-based limits on âpurely expressive activity.â

The plaintiffs and the state appeared before Morris, a Barack Obama appointee, again on Thursday with duelingmotions for summary judgment.

The state argued the plaintiffs donât have standing to challenge section two of the bill since it applies only to a âsexually oriented business,â which is defined as a ânightclub, bar, restaurant, or similar commercial enterpriseâ that serves alcohol and provides performances.

However, the plaintiffs contended that at least the Roxy Theater, a performing arts venue in Missoula, arguably falls within that definition.

âBars serve alcoholic beverages, restaurants serve food, nightclubs offer entertainment; the Roxy Theater does all three,â said Constance Van Kley, attorney representing the plaintiffs. âIf that isnât a similar commercial enterprise, I donât know what is.â

The state argued that while the theater serves alcohol, it is not a bar since alcohol isnât the primary source of its gross income.

The state also argued the court should adopt a narrowing construction and sever any portions of the bill it deems invalid.

âWeâre asking the court to apply the actâs own obscenity definition so the ban only reaches a performance that is obscene as to a minor and sever any remaining portions of the bill the court may find unconstitutional,â Noonan said.

But the plaintiffs argued federal courts canât issue a binding interpretation of state law and lack the power to interpret it in a way that revises the law.

âInstead, this is a question of âLetâs try to give effect to the legislatureâs intent,ââ Van Kley said.

Likewise, Morris seemed wary of altering the interpretation of the law.

âHow do I, looking back at what the Legislature did, fairly and accurately represent the views of those legislators in narrowing or changing the language of the statute?â Morris asked the state.

Montana argued the requested changes would work with what the legislature intended.

âWhich was, overall, keep certain performances away from minors that in this case would be obscene as applied to minors,â Noonan said.

The plaintiffs argued the statute is void for vagueness.

âWeâve spent three-and-a-half years talking about this statute and have not settled on a conclusive interpretation of it; I don’t know how an ordinary citizen or a law enforcement officer or a county attorney could do something weâre not able to do,â Van Kley said.

Morris questioned the state about the law’s limitations on parental consent.

âWhere do we draw the line here? Can a parent not consent to taking a child under this bill?â Morris asked.

Montana explained the state has an interest in shielding minors from explicit sexual content, much in the same way places like strip clubs have an age restriction.

Morris said he intends to return a decision before the end of the year.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

SEATTLE (CN) â A Washington judge on Thursday ordered Albertsons to pay a whopping $900 million judgment after finding the grocery pharmacy chain neglected to guard against the misuse and diversion of addictive medications.

âThe state of Washington is in the throes of an opioid epidemic that is so wide-reaching and damaging that it has impacted Washingtonians throughout the state,â wrote King County Superior Court Judge Janet Helson in a 219-page opinion. âBased on the evidence presented at trial, the court finds that defendants Albertsons Companies, Inc. and Safeway, Inc. substantially contributed to the creation and maintenance of this public nuisance, and that the same yearslong unfair acts or practices that contributed to the nuisance also violated Washingtonâs Consumer Protection Act.â

The judgment follows a nine-week bench trial in which the state presented evidence of Albertsons failing to investigate or report shipments of significant quantities of suspicious orders of opioids into their own pharmacies.

The evidence also showed Albertsons had improper safeguards that led to diversion â transfer of prescription opioids to the black market â and failed to conduct due diligence when dispensing staggering quantities of opioid prescriptions that raised red flags of diversion.

âThe opioid crisis has been an equal opportunity monster, bringing its destruction not only to urban areas, but to many small towns and rural areas throughout our state,â Helson wrote. âThis ongoing crisis has affected, and interfered with, public health, public safety, public spaces, and the day-to-day life of Washington residents to the point that any efforts to remedy it will necessarily be massive in size, scope, and time.â

Albertsons acts as both a distributor and dispenser of prescription opioids and is required by the Drug Enforcement Administration and the Controlled Substances Act to provide effective controls against diversion. Helson found the national pharmacy chain failed to do so.

âThe preponderance of evidence demonstrates that Albertsons never implemented and enforced policies or procedures that were effective at preventing the shipping of suspicious orders of prescription opioids to their stores, or the dispensing of âred flagâ prescriptions from those stores, without the required due diligence,â Helson wrote. âMoreover, Albertsons possessed, but routinely disregarded or ignored, information and tools that could have helped prevent diversion, choosing sales and profits over patient safety.â

The state presented evidence showing instances where tens of thousands of opioid pills were either diverted or inappropriately dispensed. Helson found the disregard for the health and safety of Washingtonians substantially contributed to the ongoing opioid crisis in the state.

Albertsons owns and operates more than 200 pharmacies in the state.

The court also found Albertsons repeatedly violated the Controlled Substances Act by dispensing and distributing addictive painkillers to increase its profits. The state presented evidence showing the pharmacy filled red-flagged prescriptions more than 6.5 million times without documenting its efforts to make sure the prescriptions were legitimate.

In a footnote, Helson acknowledged that many factors have contributed to the opioid epidemic â manufacturers misleading the public and medical community, well-intentioned state policies and medical community guidance regarding the need to treat pain, incompetent prescribers â but wrote that the existence of other factors, even substantial factors, does not excuse Albertsonsâ conduct.

Helson found Albertsons didnât properly staff its pharmacies, nor properly train the pharmacists on dispensing controlled substances. Nor did the pharmacy establish an adequate compliance department, putting company profit over patient safety, Helson determined.

Washington Attorney General Nick Brown celebrated the courtâs ruling.

âThis is a victory for the people of Washington. When giant corporations put profits above safety, they will be held accountable,â Brown said in a statement. âThis verdict canât undo the devastating harms of the opioid crisis, but it will provide desperately needed resources to support treatment and prevent future crises.â

An expert witness testified that more than 19,000 state residents died from opioid-related overdoses between 1999 and 2023. During that time, the death rate nearly quadrupled from 3.5 to 36.2 per 100,000.

Over the last three years, 7,400 Washingtonians have died from opioid overdoses.

The stateâs evidence illustrated how Washingtonâs opioid crisis has decreased public safety, increased criminal justice costs and strained local government and law enforcement, Helson wrote. It also showed how the crisis has impacted public spaces, with a rise in homelessness, public drug use and litter of used needles and drug paraphernalia.

The state said the $900 million will help expand treatment and prevention resources.

Albertsons did not respond to a request for comment before press time.

Airlines accused of sharing pricing data to inflate fares

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Two airline passengers say seven major carriers used a shared database to monitor pricing and suppress fare competition.

(CN) â Seven major airlines used a shared database to monitor each other’s prices, curbing competition and driving up the cost of domestic air travel, two airline passengers claim in a proposed class action.

Ashley Ward and Gregory Morris sued the airlines, Airline Tariff Publishing Company and its subsidiary 3Victors on Wednesday in the U.S. District Court for the Western District of Washington. The plaintiffs claim the companies conspired to exchange confidential pricing information in violation of federal antitrust law.

The airline defendants include American, Delta, United, Southwest, Alaska, Hawaiian and JetBlue. Ward says she bought tickets from Southwest while Morris purchased flights from United, and both claim they paid artificially inflated fares.

At the center of the lawsuit are two products operated by 3Victors: Iris, an airline shopping and pricing data aggregator, and PriceEye, an analytics tool that lets airlines monitor competitorsâ fares and pricing changes.

The plaintiffs say airlines contribute shopping data and dynamically generated prices to Iris in exchange for access to PriceEye. The dashboard provides pricing alerts, competitive benchmarks and tools to detect unusual changes, allowing airlines to track rivalsâ pricing in near real time.

The arrangement operates on a âgive-to-getâ model. âParticipation in Iris is based on a simple principle: the more you contribute, the more you receive,â the plaintiffs say.

Ward and Morris say the system undermines the secrecy that dynamic pricing was supposed to bring to airline ticket sales. Airlines can use individualized offers to compete for customers, but sharing that information with competitors makes it easier to monitor and respond to price cuts.

3Victors advertises that PriceEye lets airlines âconfidently monitor the competitive landscape and adjust pricing strategies as markets shift â hour by hour, not day by day,â the plaintiffs say in the complaint.

They trace the arrangement to a working group called Airline Tariff Publishing Company, or ATPCO, formed in 2016 to study dynamic pricing. A study presented at an industry conference the following year illustrated how airlines unable to monitor one anotherâs private offers could engage in price wars, driving fares down.

ATPCO subsequently developed a system to standardize dynamic pricing data and began collecting airlinesâ shopping information. The company announced a program in 2023 that allowed airlines to analyze collective shopping data, and it acquired 3Victors later that year.

By 2025 and 2026, the plaintiffs say, ATPCO had integrated the technology into Iris and PriceEye, creating a system that allowed participating airlines to exchange and analyze sensitive pricing information.

The plaintiffs, represented by Steve W. Berman of Hagens Berman, seek to represent classes of domestic airline ticket purchasers. They want the court to block the information-sharing arrangement and award damages.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The United Association National Pension Fund says it has met all requirements to certify a class of investors injured by Carvana, which is accused of artificially inflating stock prices.

PHOENIX (CN) â Attorneys for disgruntled Carvana investors rehashed arguments Thursday that the used car retailer caused massive drops of stock value through misleading statements regarding compliance with titling and inspection standards.

In a Phoenix courtroom, United Association National Pension Fund attorney Rachel Cocalis said the group has met all qualifications to certify the class of investors who lost money after Carvanaâs dramatic market plummets, and argued the retailer has not severed the link between price impacts and the specific misrepresentations it’s accused of.

Carvana instead blames its stock drops on national economic trends.

Amid high inflation and rising interest rates coming out of the Covid-19 pandemic, âsurprisingly car sales went down,â Carvana attorney Peter Hammel told the court.

The parties did not disagree that the pension fund meets the standards necessary to represent a class: that the class is numerous, that there is a common question of law or fact, that the claims of the representative plaintiffs are typical of the class as a whole and that the representative plaintiffs will adequately protect the interests of the class.

But Hammel argued that no class period can be established because no more than one statistically significant stock price fluctuation occurred in the proposed period of May 2020 to October 2022.

âTheyâre trying to start a class period on a day that nobody was misled,â Hammel said. âItâs impossible for an investor to have been injured by an accurate statement.â

Because Carvanaâs experts found only one statistically significant price fluctuation, there is no range of misdeeds to establish the beginning and end of a class period, he argued.

Representing the plaintiffs, Rachel Cocalis said a lack of statistical significance is not a death sentence for this type of claim if the defendants cannot prove a lack of causation.

United Pension Fund attorneys point to 10 public statements made by Carvana between 2020 and 2022 that artificially inflated stock prices by falsely ensuring compliance with title and inspection standards.

From 2020 on, Carvanaâs rapid sales growth outpaced its ability to obtain legal titles for those cars and register them in the states they were sold. The plaintiffs say Carvana lied in SEC filings and shareholder communications when it insisted that its reliance on temporary out-of-state plates complied with all licensing regulations and titling laws. The plaintiffs also accuse Carvana of lying about vehicle inspection standards and exaggerating its profit margins.

Relying on these assurances, investors bought Carvana stocks at artificially high prices, only to lose money when the truth finally came out, according to a 2022 lawsuit.

Plaintiff’s attorney Rachel Cocalis, of the San Diego firm Robins Geller, connected Carvanaâs tumultuous stock turns to specific Carvana statements followed by revelations of misdeeds.

Carvana touted retail sales growth in August 2021, raising stocks by 9%, only for news to later break that North Carolina suspended Carvanaâs dealer license in Wake County for title and registration violations. Stocks similarly fell six months prior after Michigan put Carvana on probation for violating inspection standards.

All the while, Carvana assured investors it was in compliance.

âBut Carvana continues its innocent until proven guilty approach despite knowing it would lead to DMV fines and loss of dealership access,â Cocalis said.

Defending Carvana, Hammel said the plaintiffs cannot prove that stock price changes had anything to do with statements made by Carvana.

âAfter four years, thereâs no statistical evidence that carvana stock went up on any specific day,â Hammel said.

Statisticians brought on as experts reported no statistical significance in the stock fluctuations occurring around specific events referenced by the plaintiffs, save for one.

The exception was Feb. 24, 2022, when stock price rose by 17%.

âThat is evidence of price impact,â Cocalis said.

On that day, though, Carvana announced a $2.2 billion cash acquisition of ADESA U.S., the nation’s second-largest wholesale vehicle physical auction business.

Because U.S. District Judge Michael T. Liburdi already dismissed the specific statement the plaintiffs linked to that fluctuation. Hammel argued nothing else could legally be used to explain it.

âThere was an obvious reason why the stock price did move,â he said.

In general, Hammel blamed the eventual drops not on any revelation of wrongdoing, but on external market factors that both Carvana and its direct competitors faced alike. Many of those competitors went out of business.

âThere were dramatic macroeconomic factors affecting the stock price at this time,â he said, referring to a global pandemic and economic recession.

Hammel maintained that none of Carvanaâs challenged statements were actually misleading, but Cocalis argued they donât need to be, as long as they omitted the truth about Carvanaâs title and registration violations.

âIf they didnât engage in that conduct, those sales numbers would be lower,â she said.

Liburdi, a Donald Trump appointee, said he would try to rule in the next month.
