Making Immersive LLM Chat Experiences More Accessible

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Eric is a Boston-based designer who helps create straightforward solutions that address a person’s practical, physical, cognitive, and emotional needs. More about Eric ↬

Weekly tips on front-end & UX.Trusted by 182,000+ folks.

I have had the privilege of helping to improve the accessibility of multiple immersive LLM chat experiences in the course of my career. Now I want to share this knowledge with you.

Accessibility is a core part of a functional digital experience. As the LLM chat pattern continues to spread like wildfire across the web, we risk excluding people if these experiences are not built with accessibility as a core consideration.

It is also worth re-mentioning what “exclusion” means here: It’s the loss of knowledge, the loss of opportunity, the loss of utility, the loss of employability, the loss of entertainment, and more.

“Immersive LLM chat experience” is a catch-all term for full-screen experiences like Claude, ChatGPT, GitHub Copilot, Gemini, America.gov, Glean, and other similar websites and web apps:

The majority of what this post covers applies to both web apps and native apps. This is because these experiences are typically built using web technologies, then placed in a native wrapper via frameworks such as Electron or Tauri.

Immersive LLM chat experiences are not patterns such as a pop-up chat, inline text generation utilities, or summarization tools. These simpler applications of LLM technology will have different considerations and expectations. Instead, this is a starting point for making those other experiences accessible.

There is nothing special about chat-based UIs when viewed via the lens of the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines. They are interactive experiences, built using web technologies. The losses I’ve described — and the legal liability associated with them — are access barriers in the same way any other web content can be an access barrier.

I would also like to mention the social model of disability here: the idea that exclusion can be created by the built environment. For example, consider an elevated entryway that uses a gently sloping ramp in place of stairs.

Built environments also extend to digital experiences, and the social model also applies here. The main activities undertaken to operate an LLM chat are already well-established:

It is how we facilitate — or fail to facilitate — those interactions that’s the salient bit.

Once a week. Useful tips on front-end & UX. Trusted by 207.000 friendly folks.

Before we get into the technical aspects, we must consider the overwhelming visual bias that permeates software creation.

We live in a highly visual culture, so our outputs unconsciously reflect and reinforce that. What we need to do here is be aware of and think through the non-visual considerations.

While not all disabilities are blindness or low vision conditions, a lot of the core interactions of an LLM chat do need specific accommodations for these two types of disability.

Recall the social model of disability here, and how it applies to the main flow of using an LLM-based chat. You:

Let’s demonstrate at a high level what someone who can fully see the screen experiences compared to someone who uses a screen reader and cannot see the entire screen, or large portions of it:

Visually, we can see the LLM reply appear. Someone who uses a screen reader does not know new content has appeared because:

So, how do we create an experience for someone who uses a screen reader that is equivalent to the experience of someone who can fully see the screen? To start, we use live regions.

Live region announcements will enable someone to know what the LLM has responded with without having to move focus from the input used for prompting. This is equivalent to someone who can see the screen, glancing upwards to read the LLM response when it appears.

First, read this excellent article by TetraLogical if this is your first attempt at getting live region announcements working. They’re tricky to get right, and this article sets the record straight.

One common pitfall I want to call specific attention to is applying aria-live on each LLM response message. Don’t do this. Instead, use a dedicated live region area to announce each message’s content.

The live region area should be placed at the end of the DOM and used for all live region messaging. This method is far more stable and reliable, which is important for ensuring everyone understands what the experience is communicating.

With that established: What we want to do is trigger a live region announcement when the LLM’s chat reply shows up in full on the screen.

This creates an outcome where you can read the message if you can see the screen, and hear or feel the message if you cannot.

There are a few things to do when creating a good user experience for the people who rely on these live region announcements. These considerations mean the difference between a compliant-yet-unusable outcome and something that genuinely works for the person using it.

Attempting to announce the message as it streams in may create a juddering and confusing experience that’s difficult for someone to parse.

It is better to wait until the message has completed streaming, and then announce the message’s content in full.

Responses from an LLM often take time to complete. Generally speaking, there are three ways to show a response is still processing:

These experiences are typically only shown visually. If you cannot see the screen, it may lead to it feeling like the experience has stalled or crashed — especially if the response is for a large or complicated request that takes a while to work through.

To address this, we’ll want to use live region announcements:

If text is visually shown on the screen: Ensure a companion live region announcement is also triggered at the same time the loading message text is displayed. The text string for the live region announcement should be the same as what is shown visually, to be sure to ensure parity of experience:

If a text-less loading animation is shown on the screen: Ensure the loading state is also communicated to assistive technology.

A live region announcement that mentions the reply is loading after a set amount of time will facilitate an equivalent experience for this interaction. It doesn’t have to be too verbose; the word “Loading” can suffice:

Triggering the loading message five seconds after prompt submission seems to be the sweet spot, according to the inclusive user research I evaluated. You can then have your logic repeat the loading message every five seconds until the LLM completes its actual reply.

Be sure to also announce the reply immediately as it finishes streaming. You don’t need to wait until the next five-second loading message cycle is complete.

Also consider randomly selecting from a set of different phrases that communicate that a response is being formulated after the initial “Loading” message is dispatched. This helps in scenarios where someone may think the loading experience itself has crashed or stalled.

We’ll eventually be able to replace the technique TetraLogical outlined with ariaNotify(). It is a much-needed and welcome addition to the platform, and will drastically lower the hacks and headaches many developers encounter when creating and managing live region announcements.

Baseline lists the ariaNotify() method as having cross-browser availability at the time of this article’s publication. However, there are two factors to consider here:

Also consider that while a polyfill for ariaNotify() is available, you need to factor in whether it has both:

What this realistically means is holding off for a while on ariaNotify() in favor of the tried and true TetraLogical live region technique.

Headings are the most popular way people who use screen readers navigate around an experience. Because of this, I advocate for inserting visually-hidden headings before each chat message.

Here’s a simplified version of that in code:

And here’s what the code might look like using the actual content from a human question and an LLM response:

So, why the “You said:”/”Cody said:” prefix? Remember the visual bias. Visually, we can see the left- or right-hand alignment of messages, used to indicate who is saying what.

The prefix serves the same purpose: identifying the message’s owner. This allows people to quickly move through a conversation the same way someone might quickly scroll upwards to re-establish context, checking who said what and when.

Using landmark regions to wrap the larger overall areas of your LLM chat experience helps people understand its overall layout. It can also enable quick screen reader navigation to each area:

Landmarks allow someone who cannot see the screen to learn about its overall makeup. This is the equivalent of how we quickly move our eyes and tilt our heads to different overall areas when scanning the screen to initially learn the overall layout of an experience.

I am hesitant to wrap chat messages in section or other landmark elements. The reason for this is that they “pollute” the list of landmarks a screen reader can generate, making navigation more laborious. This is especially relevant for long-running sessions where many messages are present.

Compare the following and ask yourself which one would be easier to move through with your keyboard if trying to jump from the prompt content area back to the sessions area:

Here, our visually hidden heading elements serve as a dedicated navigation mechanism for moving through the LLM conversation.

In addition to headings and landmarks, there are two other ways to let people get around quickly:

Skip links — sometimes referred to as skip navigation — allow you to quickly jump to another area on the same page or view.

Skip to main content links are commonplace on many mature websites and web apps. Immersive LLM chat experiences should be no different.

Place a skip link in the DOM that allows someone to jump to the main conversation area before any other DOM content that is shown visually on the screen.

Also consider using additional skip links that enable:

Composite widgets are used when a discrete section of content contains multiple interactive elements. Focus is then managed within this section by the developer, so that:

For the purposes of an immersive LLM chat experience, we want to make conversation lists composite widgets. This enables someone to quickly navigate both within and past the entire list.

Here, consider the use case of someone who exclusively relies on a keyboard to navigate. Compare pressing Tab 50 times to get through each of the 50 conversation sessions you’ve created versus pressing Tab once. Now consider motor control conditions where those keypresses can be fatiguing, or even painful to perform.

Once someone places focus on a conversation list composite widget, they can use the Up and Down arrow keys to navigate through each conversation session list item.

As a quality-of-life improvement, I also like to support the following keyboard interactions:

To save you a lot of headaches and prototyping: Corner cases around focus management and usability expectations for people who rely exclusively on keyboards work against you if you try to go with a feed pattern approach.

There’s no need to unnecessarily overcomplicate things. The article elements’ wrapping messages with visually-hidden headings approach that I’ve described earlier is both simple and effective.

People who use assistive technology understand the simpler approach, and it involves far less effort to maintain. It’s a win-win!

Pressing F6/Shift+F6 to hop around different areas of the app is an older navigation technique some people who use Windows screen readers employ when navigating through installable desktop apps. Think of it a lot like navigating by landmark.

LLM chat experiences are effectively apps in their own right. Because of this, we can recreate the F6 navigation experience and accommodate those who might attempt it. That means:

There’s no secret sauce here. For starters, place an id on each landmark, as well as tabindex="-1". Also ensure each landmark other than main has an accessible name, granted via either aria-label or aria-labelledby.

After that, have an event listener set up to recognize Control+F6 and Control+Shift+F6 key presses. Set focus to:

And voilà! We now have web-based F6 navigation.

As it is an older technique, people who are younger or less experienced with screen readers may not know F6 navigation exists.

Consider socializing the capability in the accessibility portion of your application’s settings menu to address this lack of awareness. Also consider exposing and making F6 navigation re-bindable as another discovery technique, provided your application supports keyboard shortcut rebinding.

Again, this advice only applies to an immersive LLM chat experience. Help agent popups and other less-sophisticated experiences have different expectations.

There are two areas that deserve more finesse when considering where to set focus when F6 navigation is used: the prompting input and the conversation.

Setting focus directly on the input used to type prompts — and not its parent form landmark element — allows someone to quickly get into the action.

Similarly, setting focus on the visually hidden heading of the last message displayed allows them to quickly jump to the content the LLM generated — a data attribute such as data-last-message can be really helpful for managing this.

Using F6 and Shift+F6 keypresses allows someone to hop from the chat input to what the LLM outputs, then back again. This enables rapid keyboard-based review and iteration on an LLM’s output.

Adjust the text size of your experience and double-check that content is not clipped, or that there’s horizontal overflow.

People increase the text size of the experiences they use, and more than you’d probably guess. They also resize application windows. Also consider that most LLM-based chat designs tend to use a smaller text size to allow for more information shown on screen.

Because of these two factors, it makes sense to evaluate how your experience holds up when the browser font size is increased.

Customization lives at the heart of accessibility. It’s only natural that LLM-based chat experiences have preferences to better accommodate all the different ways people use them.

In that vein, thoughtful preference offerings can go a long way to helping out people with access needs. Here are some that I’ve found to be particularly useful.

Some LLMs perform interim actions that are circumstantially less important than the messages they ultimately generate. Here, consider programming-oriented LLMs. They can perform actions like researching and reasoning, code search, and tool calls.

These secondary actions typically have a smaller, less visually important treatment compared to the messages that contain the main LLM response content. This is because the results are more important than the methodology when using this type of interaction.

Announcing each of these secondary actions could lead to a confusing or overwhelming experience, especially if multiple actions are clustered and fire off live messages in rapid succession.

To address this, you can allow people to circumstantially switch off the announcements for this type of ancillary information. The content in the conversation is still present; it just isn’t announced via a live region. This is equivalent to how someone who can see the screen can skim over them.

Letting someone re-enable these types of announcements also grants parity in experience, in that it allows them to know the actions are happening if they want a more information-intensive experience. This is equivalent to someone who can see the screen, intentionally paying close attention to the LLM’s actions.

It might also be worth investigating letting people toggle visually hiding and showing these less important announcements, as well. This can help people with attention or cognitive considerations, who may be less familiar with technical jargon.

Loading animations and large animated effects, like streaming conversations and side panels expanding and collapsing, can be distracting for some people with attention and cognitive considerations. That can go a long way toward breaking someone’s flow.

To address this, you can provide a toggle to turn off animations, reducing their transition time to zero.

You should also strive to be a respectful and courteous steward of the experience. Default your animation toggle to an off state if a request for a reduced motion experience has been in the device operating system. You can use the prefers-reduced-motion media feature to achieve this. Also consider a setting that can re-enable animation, even if a reduced motion request is present.

Some people can get distracted by, or have their focus disrupted by, sound effects. This is similar to animation considerations.

Letting people turn off app-specific sound effects means they don’t have to mute their entire device. Here, consider things like other apps playing flow state-inducing music or white noise that they might be using to help them focus.

Alternatives for sound-based notifications include, but are not limited to:

I am of the school of thought that any experience that uses keyboard shortcuts also needs to allow both editing and being switched off, both in bulk and on a shortcut-by-shortcut basis.

You may be quick to think of conflicts with assistive technology, but I’d also like to take this space to remind you of different language keyboard layouts.

Live region messages are plain text strings. These strings are announced by screen readers in full in one go from start to end.

Semantic information that comes for free by using the appropriate HTML element isn’t present in live region announcements—it’s stripped out. Because of that, we need to think about how we could make our live region content both clear and useful.

Each type of special content has its own considerations. For my work, I created special cases for tables and blocks of code:

A table’s cell content will sound odd when announced without the companion semantic structure conferred by elements such as table, th, tr, and td. Relationships between columns and rows aren’t present in a live region announcement, either.

Because of this, we can convert the live region announcement of tables to use a concise description of the number of rows and columns, plus a general description and overall trends it might highlight.

A block of code sounds like gibberish when the line breaks are stripped away, and the content is flattened into a single string.

And if it’s a large block of code, it can be a tedious amount of gibberish to parse — nobody wants to sit through an announcement of 2,376 lines of code, painstakingly announced character-by-character.

To accommodate this, we can convert the live region announcement of code blocks to use the filename or file type, plus how many lines of code the block contains.

Also note here that people can revisit the actual message content itself. The actual message content will ideally have the appropriate semantics applied to it. This means things like tables being announced as, and navigated through as, table content.

In this way, the live region message is a context-setting preview of what to expect. This is the equivalent of quickly visually skimming a message as it is streaming in, then scrolling back to review its content with more attention afterward.

All of the previous is built atop feedback from testing different approaches and noting what works, and what could be improved. You want to facilitate disabled people being the experts in their own lived experience, and not treat others’ advice as the more credible source.

Inclusive user research is a vital way of determining if an experience is actually usable. Centering disabled people’s observations and perspectives is a way to take an experience beyond baseline compliance and create something usable and equitable.

It is vital to ensure that everyone can use chat-based LLM interactions as the pattern continues to proliferate. Although this is not an exhaustive list of everything you’ll need to do, the content above covers a wide swath of the main places accessibility barriers and friction may show up.

It is my sincerest hope that the improvements I’ve learned about and implemented can help you with your efforts to make chat-based experiences on the web more accessible and usable for people who use assistive technology to get what they want or need online.

Thank you to Sarah Higley, whose research and efforts in this area informed key parts of this work.

I Toured Unitree's Factory. I'm More Bullish on the West

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Everyone thinks China's robot factories are automated. They aren't. The robots are Chinese; the robots that make robots will be Western.

On Wednesday I toured Unitree's factory in Hangzhou, and I wasn't allowed to take pictures. In the building that makes the actuators, the motors that drive every joint, there were one or two new automated lines. The rest of the floor was filled with humans operating old-school winding machines. (Winding means wrapping copper wire around a motor's core.) I don't know what percentage of the motors are made on the automated lines. But staffing the floor with humans made sense.

Unitree has a viral video (28 million views) of a humanoid doing assembly in its own factory, which it calls "an actual deployment". Its IPO prospectus calls it a pilot, and says assembly is "currently mainly manual". At the end of 2025 all of Unitree's machinery was worth about $1.7 million on its books, at a company that made almost 32,000 robots that year: some $50 of machinery per robot!

Unitree Embodied AI Model Manufactures Robots in Factory🤩 Based on Unitree’s UnifoLM-X1-0 embodied AI model, this is an actual deployment at Unitree’s own robot factory.

It doesn't need more. Labour was 8% of what it cost Unitree to make its robots in 2025, outsourced assemblers included, and most of the rest was parts. With those hands it made about 26,000 robot dogs in 2025, more than the roughly 20,000 IDC reckons the whole world shipped the year before.

So there's only so much a machine can save: halve a cost that's 8% of the bill and you save 4%. Unitree expects the motor line it's building to cut the labour in each motor by more than half, and the cost of one robot model by under 2%. Its labour per robot had roughly halved since 2023 (our arithmetic), and the filing credits scale, standardised processes and more skilled staff. None of those is a machine; two are kaizen, and I named my company, KAIKAKU, after the other word.

The other reason Unitree builds by hand starts in the showroom. Our host told us Unitree's CEO, Wang Xingxing, asked a G1 running GPT-6 to fetch him a bottle of water from another room. It did. Slowly. Unitree's own slide puts robots' "ChatGPT moment", when they can do 80% of tasks they've never seen 80% of the time, two or three years out. That slide is from last year, so it's as soon as next year.

Until then, humanoids are mostly toys for researchers: our host named Nvidia, Amazon, Meta and universities. Three quarters of Unitree's humanoid revenue came from research and education in the first nine months of 2025, and under 5% from factories or other industrial work (half or more of what Unitree files as "industry" is robots giving guided tours at companies!). When someone asked what the GD01, a rideable mecha from $650,000, is for, the answer was that Wang had wanted one since he was a child.

Unitree Unveils: GD01, A Manned Transformable Mecha, from $650,000 👏 The world's first production-ready manned mecha. It can transform. It's a civilian vehicle. It weighs ~500kg with you inside. Please everyone be sure to use the robot in a Friendly and Safe manner.

A product without a job changes every year, and a custom machine only works if the product is kind of frozen, so a machine built for this year's robot is scrap next year. It's like tailoring a suit for a teenager. Right now everything's ever-changing, except the motor. Unitree is automating its joint modules first because they're the parts its robots share most. The same Wednesday I toured his factory, Wang told a conference in Wuzhen that Unitree had spent ten years on the robot's body and would now put its weight on the brain. When I toured a drone factory, the circuit boards and motors were made by machine and final assembly was done by hand. In a drone the motor is a whole component; in a humanoid it's half of one, the copper-and-magnets half, and that half has stopped changing, like the AA battery in a toy that's redesigned every Christmas.

In May I said China's new factories "bolt robots into every one" and the West should "erase the factory floor". The first bit wasn't true of the plant I visited. For now Unitree is right. The second bit I still believe, and it's why I left Hangzhou more bullish on the West than ever. China's robots are made by cheap hands, not machines, so the machines are still up for grabs, and the West is good at building machines. AI makes it easier: a rented System 2 model can now do much of a robot programmer's job, so the West doesn't need China's army of engineers to automate the simpler work.

(The next day, at BYD, I saw the other end of this: a car plant where everything I saw was automated. More on that at the end.)

Ford is the best guide to what happens next, because he started exactly where Unitree is. His first moving assembly line, at Highland Park in 1913, put the magnets on Model T flywheels to make magnetos, small magnet-and-coil generators, kin to the motors on Unitree's winding floor. The work was split into 29 operations, each worker doing, in Ford's words, "as nearly as possible only one thing with only one movement".

The machines came once Ford froze the Model T, and by 1914 Highland Park was full of special machine tools. When the Model A replaced the T in 1927, Ford shut its plants for six months and rebuilt or scrapped most of its machine tools, and Chevrolet outsold it for the first time. A year later GM changed a Chevrolet model in three weeks. Unitree, with a new robot every year, is GM in 1927, not Ford. In 2011 Foxconn's Terry Gou promised a million robots within three years; by late 2016 Foxconn had about 40,000, and said it was slowing down "because humans are cheaper and more flexible to adapt to an ever-shorter life cycle of consumer products".

A robot is like a computer: most of the cost is the software. Programming and integrating a robot is typically 50 to 70% of the cost of the application, and every redesign means paying for the programming again. When GM put robots into its Hamtramck plant in the 1980s, they reportedly attached Buick bumpers to Cadillacs because they couldn't tell the cars apart.

When hands get too pricey, a factory has three doors:

Cross the Pacific and Unitree's sums flip. An hour of factory work costs about six times as much in America as in China (our estimate), so at American wages labour would be about 40% of Unitree's costs, not 8%. In Hangzhou labour is the tip on Unitree's bill; in America it would be the main course. And moving won't help: Chinese hands are being shut out.

America has run this experiment before. In 1964 about half a million Mexican farmworkers were shut out. In 1965, 20% of California's tomatoes were picked by machine; by 1970 the figure was 99.9%. The tomato was bred to be tougher and squarer, so the machine could pick it. The product froze to fit the machine.

In July the FCC stopped approving new humanoids and robot dogs unless they're made in America with more than 65% American parts by cost (75% from 2029).

The Pentagon goes further for the drones its programme buys: from August 2027, their stators have to be wound outside China and a handful of other countries.

The motor line Unitree expects to save 1.7% of one robot model's cost would save about 9% at American wages. The machine is worth five or six times more in America than in Hangzhou, so the West can afford one nobody can switch off.

Assembly is not vertical integration 1X bends the copper, solders the phases, potts the stator and builds the magnet arrays. Raw materials to finished actuators on our automated line. That is vertical integration. That is reindustrialization

Somebody has to build those lines, and we in the West are extremely good at building the machines that build things, even if China passed Germany as the top exporter of machine tools last year. America itself can't yet: on 28 October the White House found that it "relies on foreign imports for most types of robotics, industrial machinery, and their parts and components".

The country that's short of hands usually builds the machines. Labour-short Japan did it to America: GM bought the first Unimate industrial robot in 1961, Kawasaki licensed it in 1968, and by 1990 America's first robot makers had been sold to Europe. Now America is short of hands, and the machine builders are moving in: Mazak has built 40,000 machines in Kentucky, Yaskawa will make robots in Wisconsin, and Fanuc has bought a site in Michigan.

Then there's the programming. Paying for it is why a custom machine only made sense for a frozen product, and AI is cutting that bill. HD Hyundai and Palantir say AI agents cut the average time to fix a robot-programming issue from 21.5 days to 7.9. Apple estimated that it would have taken up to nine months to hire 8,700 industrial engineers in America, versus 15 days in China. China will have the same AI tools, but an engineer's time is three to five times more expensive in America, so each hour AI saves is worth that much more. The advantage China will lose quickest is its army of robot programmers.

Software went through all this first. Big programs used to be planned like factories: a change took months of programmers' time, so teams froze the spec and shipped once a year. These days a coding agent makes the change before lunch, and products ship every week. In my last essay I called the robot version agentic robotics: an off-the-shelf System 2, a model like Claude, writes the robot's program, runs it and fixes it itself, on any task a sensor can check. Everything is a drawer except for laundry, and most of a motor line is drawers.

China makes the magnets, but MP Materials has finished qualifying its magnets with GM, and its Texas plant begins deliveries before the end of 2026. BMW makes an EV motor in Steyr that uses no permanent magnets, and wants capacity for more than 100,000 of them this year.

This is how we lose. By 2022, all of the top ten manufacturers of solar panel-making equipment were Chinese. China also makes many of the machines that build electric-car motors. Within a few years Chinese builders of hairpin winding lines caught up with German and Italian ones, with changeovers done in software, undercut them by as much as half, and have started exporting whole lines to Europe. The winder is a small part of a motor's cost, and American wages are high enough that even a winder at twice the price still saves 8 to 9% of the cost of a robot. What the West can't afford is one that's slow to change over.

Terrible, short-sighted move. We are shooting ourselves in the foot here. I am happy to spend time with policymakers to convince them of the idiocy of banning Chinese hardware in 2026, and how the US might regain a competitive edge in hardware. USA is not in the position of Show more

Yesterday, Congress took up the GUARD Act, bipartisan legislation to keep adversary-controlled robots out of American infrastructure. Our CEO Peggy Johnson wrote about why this hearing matters, in a new op-ed with @PirateWires. 🧵

Eric Jang thinks banning Chinese hardware is idiocy and wants an American Unitree instead. Agreed! But build it with machines. (Disclosure: I invest in Standard Manufacturing, which builds autonomous assembly lines, and in Dirac, Isembard, RMFG and Minerva, a US humanoid maker that gains from the FCC rule.)

Tesla alone wants a million Optimus a year. Schaeffler counts 25 to 30 main-joint actuators in a humanoid, hands not included, so call it 25 million motors. The FCC rule says 65% of a humanoid's parts by cost must be made in America. And the machines that make motors? Mostly imported.

Hands win in China. Machines win in the West.

The next day I went to BYD, to a final-assembly plant two and a half hours east of Shenzhen. When BYD started making batteries in 1995, its founder, Wang Chuanfu, did Unitree's arithmetic, as CCTV tells it: an imported machine cost $200,000, written off at RMB 20,000 a month, enough to hire a dozen workers. "A person plus a jig equals a robot," he told CCTV. Thirty years later, everything I saw was automated: robots welding the bodies, and finished cars rolling off the line and onto BYD's own ship.

Nothing exotic. The robots I saw were Fanuc and KUKA arms on pre-programmed paths, similar to what Tesla uses, with grippers bolted together out of aluminium extrusions full of holes. Anyone can buy them, and nothing I saw is beyond an American plant, except the people. Fanuc sells the drills; BYD has the carpenters. BYD employs almost as many people in R&D alone as Tesla has in total.

So what are all those engineers doing in a factory run by robots, and could an agent do their job? That's part 2.

Two weeks inside Chinese companies, written up one at a time.

Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash: Fast and cost-effective with longer clips

Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash brings 15-second clips, stronger consistency, and faster, more cost-effective generation.

Your browser does not support the audio element.

We’re rolling out Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, which offers native 15-second video creation, improved reference adherence, and multi-turn conversational editing. This update lowers costs by 30% and provides better consistency for your professional projects. You can start using these capabilities today across the Gemini API, Google Vids, and Gemini for business to produce high-quality video content at scale.

Today, we’re rolling out Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, and making it generally available across the Gemini API and Gemini for business. Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash introduces native 15-second video creation, multi-turn conversational editing capabilities, and enhanced reference following. We have dropped the price 30% from Gemini Omni 1.1 making it available at $0.07 per second for 720p video.

Beyond the lower price, the workflow keeps costs down: you can draft fast at 360p or 720p, fix a detail with a follow-up edit instead of re-generating the entire clip, and then upscale your best creations to 1080p or 4K.

You can now generate and edit videos natively up to 15 seconds long, up from 10 seconds with Omni 1.1. You can also specify duration in one-second increments from 3 to 15 seconds, giving you room to tell complete stories, create product explainers, or produce full social ad spots in a single generation.

You can guide generation combining character, product, or style images, short motion clips (up to three seconds), and audio cues — and Omni 1.2 Flash holds onto those details from start to finish. Faces, garments, packaging typography, and brand colors stay consistent across camera angles, making it easy to turn existing product photography or character sheets into virtual try-on clips, catalog spins, and branded video ads.

Combining one model photo with front-and-back product shots of three outfits, Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash generates a 15-second virtual try-on sequence across 360-degree turns and match cuts while keeping the model’s identity and each garment’s cut, drape, and back details consistent.

You can maintain stronger character, object, and scene consistency from the first to the last frame, with less visual drift or morphing across motion and camera transitions. Whether you’re interpolating between a first and last keyframe, or extending a clip to build a longer sequence, products, characters, props, and environments stay consistent from shot to shot.

Given the same static studio lighting exhibition poster and five-layer animation prompt, Gemini Omni 1.1 Flash does not match the reference image as closely as 1.2 does. Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash builds the poster sequentially layer by layer on the dusty-pink cyclorama rising the hexagonal plinth, assembling the ribbed glass, warm filament, faceted crystal, and coiled-cord letterforms for HALO, GLOW, and LUMEN, and locking every material, shadow, and typographic detail consistently through the final frame.

You can refine the same clip turn by turn — such as swapping a product or background, changing the lighting or weather, or switching the aspect ratio — instead of starting over from scratch. Multi-turn conversational editing works across full 15-second clips, making it easy to produce ad variants for different audiences and placements without re-briefing or re-generating.

Starting from one generated clip, multi-turn conversational prompts swap the background turn by turn — from a striped pavilion tent to an oak forest, a stable with a white horse, a market fair, and a stone castle — while keeping both characters’ performances, armor reflections, and camera framing intact.

You can generate scenes with more fluid, natural-sounding dialogue, clearer lip-sync, and distinct multi-speaker conditioning. This will show up whether you’re directing multi-character dialogue in Flow, creating video explainers in Gemini for business, or creating polished, pro videos with avatars, music and narration in Google Vids.

Conditioned on one conservatory scene image alongside four character, mouth, and one-second voice references, Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash routes each sampled voice to the right character with expressive lip-sync and natural comedic timing across a 15-second animated scene.

You can direct complex, continuous camera movements like sweeping FPV flythroughs to dramatic zoom-outs and orbits with significantly fewer unwanted jump cuts, steadier scale, and a more polished, cinematic look.

Guided by two architectural reference photos, Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash directs one continuous FPV drone flight across the piazza, through the bronze doors, and up through the Pantheon’s coffered dome to the oculus with zero jump cuts.

Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash generates clips up to 15 seconds long that hold references together better than before. You can scroll through to see characters, text, and camera moves stay consistent across longer shots, multiple turns and transitions with no cuts.

15 second long clips: Turns one style frame into a 4-shot, 15-second macro science explainer with accurately spelled, motion-tracked callouts.

Multi-turn motion transfer: Generates a rippling octopus in Turn 1, then replaces it in Turn 2 with a dancer whose gown mirrors the exact fluid motion.

First-to-last frame consistency (1.1 vs. 1.2): Connects an exterior start frame and interior end frame in one continuous camera push in 1.2 Flash. You can see subtle differences like the cats on the roof, the soup ladle turning into chopsticks, and some other minor changes.

Unbroken crash zoom-out: Pulls continuously from a tight shoreline close-up to an aerial island vista in one 10-second shot with natural scale and zero cuts.

Report: Apple and Samsung outperform rivals as smartphone chip shipments tumble 21%

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

According to Counterpoint Research, Apple and Samsung were the only major smartphone chip vendors to gain market share in Q3 2026, as global shipments declined 21% year-over-year. Here are the details.

At this point, most 9to5Mac readers are probably familiar with the challenges that smartphone vendors have been facing due to rising memory costs, and how Apple has managed to weather the impact better than many of its competitors.

Over the past few months, many reports have noted that while most smartphone vendors raised prices more than once throughout the year, Apple held off on increasing iPhone prices until September, with the launch of the iPhone 18 Pro series.

Those same reports have been showing the iPhone outperforming the broader market in smartphone, even as the industry experiences a sharp downturn.

That said, Counterpoint Research recently published a report (via MacMagazine) examining how rising memory costs and slowing smartphone demand have affected the global smartphone chip market.

According to the report, Apple remained in third place among smartphone chip vendors in the third quarter, with its market share increasing from 17% in Q3 2025 to 21%.

MediaTek still led the global smartphone SoC market with a 28% share (down 6 percentage points year-over-year), followed by Qualcomm with 22% (down from 25%).

After Apple, UNISOC ranked fourth with 13% (down from 14%), while Samsung remained in fifth with an 8% share, up from 6% in Q3 2025. Other manufacturers went from a 4% share to 8%.

Overall, the market saw a 21% decline year-over-year, though the report didn’t disclose how many chips were shipped during the quarter.

Commenting on the numbers, Counterpoint Research Senior Analyst Shivani Parashar said:

“We saw three notable flagship chipset launches in September 2026, based on TSMC’s 2nm process node: Apple’s A20 series, MediaTek’s Dimensity 9600 Pro and Qualcomm’s Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 series. These launches are expected to boost premium smartphone SoC shipments in Q4 2026, although volumes are still likely to remain below Q4 2025 levels.”

Looking ahead, Counterpoint expects global smartphone chip shipments to decline 20% year-over-year in 2026, followed by another 3% to 4% drop in 2027. The research firm also expects memory prices to continue rising next year.

During its fiscal Q4 2026 earnings call, Apple described the current memory pricing situation as “unprecedented,” warning that further increases would continue to put pressure on its profit margins in the coming quarters.

To read Counterpoint’s full report, follow this link.

When Arsenal can qualify for Champions League knockout phase after Slavia Prague win

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Arsenal took a giant step towards sealing qualification for the Champions League knockout phase with victory over Slavia Prague on Wednesday night.

Noni Madueke and Viktor Gyokeres had the Gunners in cruise control at half time at the Fortuna Arena, the latter producing, arguably, his best all-round performance since arriving from Sporting last summer.

Arsenal had chances to extend their lead through Eberechi Eze and Mikel Merino, but were almost punished for such profligacy.

As Slavia head coach Jindrich Trpisovsky tinkered with his system during the interval, a double substitution - combined with the Gunners not matching the high standards they set in the opening 45 minutes - meant the second half told a different story.

Gabriel was forced into a desperate clearance under his own bar, David Raya produced one sensational save to tip a header onto the post and Arsenal survived a VAR check for a Slavia Prague penalty.

Wiktor Nowak was able to pull a goal back for the hosts in second half stoppage time, but the Gunners held on to move up to third in the 36-team Champions League table following defeats for Liverpool and Real Betis.

It is now three wins and a draw for Arsenal from their opening four games in this season’s league phase, and victory in Prague should be enough to avoid an early elimination from the competition.

In fact, the result sees the Gunners move back to within two points of holders Paris Saint-Germain and Real Madrid - the only two sides with perfect winning records at the halfway stage.

Arsenal still have to play Jose Mourinho’s side, but next welcome Borussia Dortmund to the Emirates Stadium before rounding off the league phase against Real Betis and Sabah in the new year.

Of course, in this new Champions League format, the luxury a top-eight finish brings is automatic qualification to the last 16 as well as avoiding two extra games in the play-off round for the knockout phase.

As things currently stand, Mikel Arteta's side still have plenty of work ahead to ensure they finish in the top eight for a third consecutive season.

Jindrich Trpisovsky issues Arsenal verdict after Champions League defeat

Arsenal dealt new injury worry as Mikel Arteta forced into substitution

Mikel Arteta concerned by Arsenal's second half in win over Slavia Prague

However, finishing in the top 24 is already within reach - based on the points totals from the last two league phase campaigns.

In 2024-25, Club Brugge qualified with 11 points, while nine points were enough for Benfica to scrape through in dramatic fashion 2025-26.

Arsenal already have 10 points from a possible 12. Therefore they can, perhaps, guarantee a place in this season’s Champions League knockout phase with victory over Dortmund on November 24 before targeting a bye into the round of 16.

Videos Show Significant Flood Damage at Colorado Trail Southern Terminus

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Unconfirmed footage on social media appears to show extensive damage at the Colorado Trail’s southern terminus after two severe back-to-back flood events hit the area in the past two weeks.

One video shows much of the trail and its surroundings washed out at the Junction Creek trailhead, which doubles as the terminus of the 485-mile Colorado Trail. Once situated in a wide, flat area well back from the creek, the sign where Colorado Trail thru-hikers traditionally mark the start or end of their journeys now appears to stand at the edge of a badly eroded wash. A separate unconfirmed report on Facebook suggests that the Colorado Trail bridge over Junction Creek leading up to Gudy’s Rest has been completely wiped out.

Meanwhile, footage from Colorado Parks and Wildlife Durango shows “a 20 foot waterfall” taking the place of what was once a section of Junction Creek Road, the primary access road for the trailhead and local community, which sits several miles northeast of Durango proper.

In addition to housing the Junction Creek trailhead, the area is also residential. In recent days, posts from local residents have described scenes of chaos in the aftermath of the flood, with many seeking possessions carried off by the floodwaters.

A Trek blogger standing at the CT southern terminus at Junction Creek in late summer 2026. In the aftermath of the floods, footage appears to show that all the ground up to the sign has been washed away. Photo: Caroline Egener

Official information about the extent of damage in the backcountry is currently limited as officials wait for floodwaters to recede. Both San Juan National Forest, which encompasses the southern terminus, and the Colorado Trail Foundation have said they are waiting for conditions to become safe enough to assess the damage. A condition report on the US Forest Service website for the Junction Creek Trail says “huge rock and heavy debris is blocking the trail 1/2 mile from Junction Creek Trailhead (lower).”

Traveling in flood-damaged areas is currently strongly discouraged. Doing so is unsafe, and putting yourself in a situation where you might need rescue also directly pulls on resources that are urgently needed elsewhere in affected communities. “Record flooding across Southwest Colorado has left San Juan National Forest roads, trails, and other infrastructure inaccessible, severely damaged, and, in many cases, impassable. Don’t become a statistic — rescue will not be readily available due to the current demand for search and rescue services,” said San Juan National Forest on Facebook.

“We have received word of extensive damage to the CT in the Durango area but will not be able to assess the extent of the damage and next steps until it is safe to do so,” said the Colorado Trail Foundation (CTF) in a Facebook post.

“Like you, our hearts are broken by the pictures and videos coming from the communities impacted by the flooding along the southern terminus of the Colorado Trail. Right now our focus is on the people, families and communities who are affected by this devastating flooding,” the CTF post continues. “Please join us in supporting the communities of southwestern Colorado as they recover and heal from these devastating storms.”

Featured image: The Junction Creek trailhead photographed earlier in 2026. Photo: Caroline Egener

Zillow Says Lawsuits in the Past, ‘Not Obvious’ How Market Recovers

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Real estate’s biggest name is struggling, just like everyone else, but company leadership is promising that it—and the industry—have ways to hang on amid a tech revolution, a sluggish market and legal woes the company claims are in the past now.

Speaking to investors after reporting earnings for Q3 2026, Zillow CEO Jeremy Wacksman said lawsuits are “not impacting our business,” with CFO and COO Jeremy Hofmann adding that legal costs are “normalized” going forward as the company hunkers down for what is expected to be a tough environment for real estate.

“It’s not obvious to us how things are necessarily going to improve,” Hofmann said.

Zillow’s report comes as mortgage rates remain elevated from earlier this year and existing home sales are on track to come in around 4 million—down more than 20% from pre-pandemic norms. The company’s stock fell sharply, down around 17% in after-hours trading.

The company still faces a proposed class-action lawsuit from a real estate agent who claims Zillow’s “monopoly power” inflates fees and commissions. A judge ruled in September that the lawsuit could move forward.

Democratic lawmakers previously offered pointed criticism of some of Zillow’s business practices, which are also used by at least some other portals, as the party made big gains in the midterms yesterday.

On the tech side, Zillow said it has not seen major impacts from increased use of AI by consumers, or Google’s ongoing push into residential real estate.

“(Google’s program) is very different than a ‘Preferred’ connection and an integrated transaction and helping agents grow their business that way. So we haven’t seen any impact from it today, even though it’s supposedly live nationwide,” Wacksman said.

As the company previously announced, agent partners are all being transitioned from other programs to the company’s Zillow Preferred service, which executives said is integrated with Zillow Home Loans.

The transition is expected to be fully implemented by the first half of 2027, and 75% complete by the end of this year.

Zillow’s residential for-sale revenue was down 4% year-over-year, $419 million in Q3 2026 compared to $435 million in Q3 2025. On the other hand, mortgage revenue grew 32%, and the company touted durable rental income, up 26% from the same period last year.

Overall revenue came in up 7% from last year at $724 million, with the lawsuit payment—$165 million after insurance covered part of the settlement—dinging the top—line. Outlook for Q4—historically the slowest for real estate—was $635 to $665 million, roughly the same as last year’s reported $654 million.

Late last year, Zillow was targeted by a high-profile class-action firm with claims from recent homebuyers focused on those “lead diversion” and loan referral practices. A judge initially threw that lawsuit out (though plaintiffs are seeking to revive it).

The company also agreed last week to pay $212.5 million to settle a lawsuit over its long-shuttered iBuying business, something the company said it was happy to put behind it.

“I think we’ve been pretty consistent and transparent to the degree we could be, that we didn’t expect these cases to impact business. And I think as you’re seeing them resolve, get settled, move on, you’re seeing that come to light. They’re not impacting our business,” Wacksman said.

As far as the macro picture, Hofmann said that without any sign of a market rebound, “its our job (to) just keep driving on the underlying structural growth drivers we have,” including software and rentals that are not rate dependent.

“We’re also fortunate in that the business has diversified pretty rapidly over the last four or five years,” he said.

Addressing the use of agentic AI and potential changes in how consumers search for homes, Wacksman and Hofmann both promised that Zillow’s model was built to thrive in this new environment.

“We see the human experiences remaining human,” Wacksman said. “The dreaming and shopping part is the part you want to do and the transaction part is the part you need to do, and Zillow is the best place to do both those things…and then our ability to make the professional’s job easier so they can serve the customer better is where we’re focused, and why we are so confident in the strategy.”

Jesse Williams is content director for RISMedia Premier.
